THÈSE Pour obtenir le grade de [SOURCE: Page_1]

DOCTEUR DE L’UNIVERSITÉ GRENOBLE ALPES École doctorale : STEP - Sciences de la Terre de l'Environnement et des Planètes Spécialité : Sciences de la Terre et de l'Environnement Unité de recherche : Institut des Géosciences de l'Environnement (IGE) Présence, mobilité et risques des pesticides utilisés à proximité d'une zone de conservation prioritaire dans l'est du Michoacan (Mexique) Occurrence, mobility, and risks of pesticides used in the vicinity of a priority conservation area in eastern Michoacan (Mexico) Présentée par : Ana Edith MERLO REYES Direction de thèse : Céline DUWIG                                                               Directrice de thèse Directrice de recherche, IRD Christine BADUEL                                                           Co-directrice de thèse Chargée de Recherche, IRD [SOURCE: Page_1]

María Isabel RAMIREZ RAMIREZ                                               Co-directrice de thèse Universidad Nacional Autónoma de México [SOURCE: Page_1]

Rapporteurs : Olivier GRUNBERGER CHARGE DE RECHERCHE HDR, IRD délégation régionale Occitanie Jaqueline GARCIA HERNANDEZ SENIOR SCIENTIST, Centro de Investigación en Alimentación y Desarrollo Thèse soutenue publiquement le 21 mai 2024, devant le jury composé de : Aurélien DOMMERGUE,                                                   Président PROFESSEUR DES UNIVERSITES, Université Grenoble Alpes Céline DUWIG,                                                         Directrice de thèse DIRECTRICE DE RECHERCHE, IRD délégation régionale Sud Est Olivier GRUNBERGER,                                                   Rapporteur CHARGE DE RECHERCHE HDR, IRD délégation régionale Occitanie Jeremy MCNEIL,                                                        Examinateur PROFESSEUR EMERITE, University of Western Ontario [SOURCE: Page_1]

Invitées : Christine BADUEL Chargée de Recherche, Institut de Recherche pour le Développement Blanca PRADO PANO Senior scientist, Universidad Nacional Autónoma de México (UNAM) [SOURCE: Page_1]

Abstract Although pesticides provide advantages in terms of pest management, safeguarding agricultural yields, their mishandling can have adverse effects on human health and the environment. The purpose of this work was to evaluate the risks posed by pesticides on human health, aquatic organisms, and water sources, by the analysis of agricultural practices and occurrence of pesticides in waters from the eastern region of Michoacan. To achieve this goal a multi-approach methodology was employed, using qualitative techniques along with chemical analysis and the application of risk indicators. [SOURCE: Page_3]

The eastern region of Michoacan has extended agricultural lands surrounded by the Monarch Butterfly Biosphere Reserve (RBBM). A protected natural area that serves as the overwintering habitat for the monarch butterfly (Danaus plexippus) and home to hundreds of flora and fauna species, making it a priority conservation area, named a World Heritage Site by UNESCO in 2008. Over the past fifteen years, agricultural activity in the vicinity of the RBBM has intensified, mainly marked by the replacement of traditional crops with avocado plantations. [SOURCE: Page_3]

Through face-to-face semi-structured interviews, we collected information on the types of pesticide used, timing, frequency of application, and handling practices in two agricultural watersheds: Senguio in the northern and Zitácuaro in the southern of the MBBR. Using the survey data and databases on pesticides toxicological properties, the human health risk assessment was calculated using the Quebec-IRPeQ pesticide risk indicator. A robust and precise analytical method based on solid-phase extraction and LC-MS/MS was developed and validated to quantify 21 different pesticides in 16 water samples. The potential aquatic ecological risks of the pesticides detected in water samples were assessed in terms of both acute exposures based on toxic units (TUs) and chronic exposures based on risk quotients (RQs). The mobility impact of pesticides was assessed with the pesticides impact rating index (PIRI), and the aquifer vulnerability assessment was computed by the DRASTIC-L method coupled with geographic information systems. [SOURCE: Page_3]

Senguio watershed primarily focused on annual-cycling crops like maize, fodder crops, strawberries, and husk tomatoes, while Zitácuaro watershed had predominantly perennial plantations such as avocado, guava, and peach. In Zitácuaro, there was a higher frequency of pesticide application, particularly for avocado monocultures, requiring monthly treatments to prevent infestations. Conversely, in Senguio, pesticide application was limited to the production period of annual-cycling crops. Despite these differences, both watersheds exhibited similarities in the lack of personal protective equipment usage among farmers and [SOURCE: Page_3]

i [SOURCE: Page_3]

improper pesticide handling practices, including storage, application timing, and disposal methods. In total, 28 active ingredients of pesticides are used, including glyphosate (29% of respondents), imidacloprid (27%), and benomyl (24%). [SOURCE: Page_4]

The pesticide analysis showed the presence of 13 different pesticides and degradation products in the water samples. The highest concentrations were found for imidacloprid (1195 ngL-1) and carbendazim (a degradation product of benomyl; 932 ngL-1), along with the metabolite of pyrethroid insecticides, 3-PBA (494 ngL-1). Among the most used pesticides, the insecticide lambda-cyhalothrin and the fungicide benomyl pose the highest risk to human health, while pesticide residues of atrazine, diuron, carbendazim and the metabolite 3-PBA present high risk to the aquatic ecosystem. [SOURCE: Page_4]

The pesticides evaluated with the PIRI index exhibited a low mobility potential to groundwater, with exception of the insecticide imidacloprid, which presented high mobility in sandy loam soils. The DRASTIC-L Index indicate high vulnerability to contaminants in agricultural areas and low vulnerability in zones covered by dense forest. Combining these results, imidacloprid a commonly used pesticide and highly toxic for non-target insects present a potential risk of aquifer contamination in the highly vulnerable zones displayed by the DRASTIC-L method, which are mainly representative by agricultural lands. These results show a first insight into assessing the groundwater vulnerability, however, specific pesticide degradation and sorption studies in those soils is necessary to perform models with more accurate results. [SOURCE: Page_4]

This work represents a pioneering effort to fill several knowledge gaps in the eastern region of Michoacán. It not only provides the initial documented insights into agricultural practices and the associated adverse effects, but also presents the first findings concerning pesticide occurrence in regional water sources and their environmental implications. Furthermore, it offers a preliminary indication of potential risks of pesticide contamination to aquifers. This comprehensive understanding offers scientific-based insights to prioritize the development of sustainable agricultural strategies. [SOURCE: Page_4]

ii [SOURCE: Page_4]

Résumé Bien que les pesticides offrent des avantages en termes de lutte contre les parasites et de préservation des rendements agricoles, leur mauvaise utilisation peut avoir des effets préjudiciables sur la santé humaine et l'environnement. L'objectif de ce travail était d'évaluer les risques posés par les pesticides sur la santé humaine, les organismes aquatiques et les ressources en eau, en analysant les pratiques agricoles et la présence de pesticides dans les eaux de la région orientale de Michoacan (Mexique). Pour atteindre cet objectif, une méthodologie multi-approche a été employée, utilisant des techniques qualitatives ainsi que des analyses chimiques et l'application d'indicateurs de risque. [SOURCE: Page_5]

La région orientale de Michoacan possède de vastes terres agricoles entourées par la réserve de biosphère du papillon monarque (RBPM). Cette zone naturelle protégée sert d'habitat migratoire au papillon monarque (Danaus plexippus) et héberge des centaines d'espèces de flore et de faune, ce qui fait d'elle une zone de conservation prioritaire, nommée site du patrimoine mondial par l'UNESCO en 2008. Au cours des quinze dernières années, l'activité agricole s'est intensifiée à proximité de la RBPM, principalement marquée par le remplacement des cultures traditionnelles par des plantations d'avocats. [SOURCE: Page_5]

Par le biais d'entretiens semi-structurés en face à face, nous avons recueilli des informations sur les types de pesticides utilisés, le calendrier, la fréquence d'application et les pratiques de manipulation dans deux bassins versants agricoles : Senguio dans le nord et Zitácuaro dans le sud de la RBPM. En utilisant les données des enquêtes et les bases de données sur les propriétés toxicologiques des pesticides, l'évaluation du risque pour la santé humaine a été calculée à l'aide de l'indicateur de risque des pesticides de l'IRPeQ. Une méthode analytique robuste et précise basée sur l'extraction en phase solide et la LC-MS/MS a été développée et validée pour quantifier 21 pesticides différents dans 16 échantillons d'eau. Les risques écologiques aquatiques potentiels des pesticides détectés dans les échantillons d'eau ont été évalués en termes d'expositions aiguës basées sur les unités toxiques (UT) et d'expositions chroniques basées sur les quotients de risque (QR). L'impact des pesticides sur la mobilité a été évalué à l'aide de l'indice d'évaluation de l'impact des pesticides (PIRI) et l'évaluation de la vulnérabilité de l'aquifère a été calculée à l'aide de la méthode DRASTIC-L couplée à des systèmes d'information géographique. [SOURCE: Page_5]

Le bassin versant de Senguio était principalement axé sur les cultures annuelles comme le maïs, les cultures fourragères, les fraises et les tomates vertes, tandis que le bassin versant de Zitácuaro comportait surtout des plantations pérennes comme les avocats, les goyaves et les pêches. À Zitácuaro, la fréquence d'application des pesticides était plus élevée, [SOURCE: Page_5]

iii [SOURCE: Page_5]

en particulier pour les monocultures d'avocats, ce qui nécessitait des traitements mensuels pour prévenir les infestations. À l'inverse, à Senguio, l'application de pesticides était limitée à la période de production des cultures à cycle annuel. Malgré ces différences, les deux bassins versants présentent des similitudes en ce qui concerne le manque d'utilisation d'équipements de protection personnelle par les agriculteurs et les mauvaises pratiques de manipulation des pesticides, notamment en matière de stockage, de calendrier d'application et de procédures concernant l'élimination des déchets. Au total, 28 ingrédients actifs de pesticides sont utilisés, dont le glyphosate (29 % des répondants), l'imidaclopride (27 %) et le bénomyl (24 %). [SOURCE: Page_6]

L'analyse des pesticides a révélé la présence de 13 pesticides différents et de produits de dégradation dans les échantillons d'eau. Les concentrations les plus élevées ont été relevées pour l'imidaclopride (1195 ngL-1) et le carbendazime (un produit de dégradation du bénomyl ; 932 ngL-1), ainsi que pour le métabolite des insecticides pyréthroïdes, le 3-PBA (494 ngL-1). Parmi les pesticides les plus utilisés, l'insecticide lambda-cyhalothrine et le fongicide bénomyl présentent le risque le plus élevé pour la santé humaine, tandis que l'écosystème aquatique présente un risque élevé en raison des résidus d'atrazine, de diuron, de carbendazime et du métabolite 3-PBA. [SOURCE: Page_6]

Les pesticides évalués à l'aide de l'indice PIRI présentaient un faible potentiel de mobilité vers les eaux souterraines, à l'exception de l'insecticide imidaclopride, qui présentait une mobilité élevée dans les sols sablo-limoneux. Les pesticides évalués à l'aide de l'indice PIRI présentaient un faible potentiel de mobilité vers les eaux souterraines, à l'exception de l'insecticide imidaclopride, qui présentait une mobilité élevée dans les sols sablo-limoneux. L'indice DRASTIC-L indique une forte vulnérabilité aux contaminants dans les zones agricoles et une faible vulnérabilité dans les zones couvertes de forêts denses. En combinant ces résultats, l'imidaclopride, un pesticide couramment utilisé et hautement toxique pour les insectes non ciblés, présente un risque de contamination de l'aquifère dans les zones hautement vulnérables indiquées par la méthode DRASTIC-L, qui sont principalement représentées par des terres agricoles. Ces résultats donnent un premier aperçu de l'évaluation de la vulnérabilité des eaux souterraines, mais des études spécifiques sur la dégradation et la sorption des pesticides dans ces sols sont nécessaires pour réaliser des modèles avec des résultats plus précis. [SOURCE: Page_6]

Ce travail représente un effort pionnier pour combler plusieurs lacunes en matière de connaissances dans la région orientale du Michoacán. Il fournit non seulement les premières informations documentées sur les pratiques agricoles et leurs effets négatifs, mais présente également les premiers résultats concernant la présence de pesticides dans les sources d'eau de la région et leurs implications pour l'environnement. En outre, elle donne une première indication des risques potentiels de contamination des aquifères par les pesticides. Cette [SOURCE: Page_6]

iv [SOURCE: Page_6]

compréhension globale offre des perspectives scientifiques permettant d'établir des priorités dans le développement de stratégies agricoles durables. [SOURCE: Page_7]

v [SOURCE: Page_7]

General Introduction Crop pests and diseases pose a significant threat to the livelihoods of rural families and to global food security. On average, between 10 and 40 percent of the production of primary crops such as maize, wheat, rice, and potatoes is estimated to be lost worldwide due to pests (Savary et al., 2019). And this crop production losses to pests are projected to increase by 10 to 25 percent due to warming climate (Deutsch et al., 2018). According with a recent report of the International Plant Protection Convection (IPPC) of the Food and Agricultural Organization of the United Nations (FAO), climate change is expected to exacerbate stressors such as insect pests and pathogens, impacting not only agriculture but also forestry (IPPC and FAO, 2021). [SOURCE: Page_23]

Pesticides play a crucial role in controlling the spread of forest pests and minimizing potential crop losses. While pesticides themselves may not directly enhance crop yields, their effective management of these threats is influential in safeguarding agricultural productivity (Nieder, Benbi and Reichl, 2018). The near-future scenarios of climate change, paired with population growth, are expected to trigger a decrease in food production caused by droughts and pest infestations, alongside an increase in food demand. As a result, it is expected an increase of pesticides usage (Deutsch et al., 2018; IPPC and FAO, 2021). [SOURCE: Page_23]

However, the intensification of pesticide use raises significant concerns about human health and negative impacts on surrounding ecosystems. The spread of hazardous pesticides in the environment is a threat to beneficial terrestrial and aquatic insects and wildlife and can lead to long term contamination of soil, groundwater, surface water and drinking water resources (Relyea, 2009; Nieder, Benbi and Reichl, 2018; Sharma et al., 2019; Silva-Madera et al., 2021). The extent of pesticide contamination in the environment, depends on the chemical and physical properties of the chemical, as well as meteorological conditions, soil characteristics, and water environmental conditions (Nieder, Benbi and Reichl, 2018). At the farm scale, other important factors that may increase environmental and human exposure are the local agricultural practices related to application technique of application (manual or motorised), inadequate storage of pesticide bottles, (Hassaan and El Nemr, 2020), pest pressure, price of agricultural inputs and pesticide application rate (Nagesh et al., 2023). [SOURCE: Page_23]

Globally the, the use of pesticides is quite large, 3.5 million tons were reported in 2021. However, it is likely that greater worldwide use will be associated only to some few active ingredients (Popp, Pető and Nagy, 2013; Hassaan and El Nemr, 2020). These substances are largely unmonitored and pose a high health concern to those who are in direct contact with pesticides, such as farmers and field-workers (Koutros et al., 2016). Exposure to pesticides [SOURCE: Page_23]

1 [SOURCE: Page_23]

has been linked to various acute and chronic health issues, as well as certain types of cancer or fatal outcomes (Salas, Duran and Wiener, 2000; Mostafalou and Abdollahi, 2013; Tang et al., 2021). According to the World Health Organization, pesticide poisoning affects an estimated three million individuals annually, resulting in up to 220,000 deaths, with the majority occurring in developing countries (Popp, Pető and Nagy, 2013). [SOURCE: Page_24]

Over the last four decades, the Integrated Pest Management (IPM) has been implemented in Europe and US as a solution to manage pests and safeguard crop production, reducing the exposure to toxic compounds for farmers, consumers and non-target species, but also reducing problems caused by crop-resistant pesticides (Rajinder et al., 2009; Stenberg, 2017; EPA, 2021). However, pesticide usage patterns can vary widely across regions due to differences in crop types, climate, and consumer demands (Popp, Pető and Nagy, 2013), and specific diagnosis are necessary to achieve that vision in the most vulnerable zones in the world (Deutsch et al., 2018; IPPC and FAO, 2021). [SOURCE: Page_24]

In eastern Michoacan, México, lies a priority conservation area, the Monarch Butterfly Biosphere Reserve (MBBR). This area, which encompasses numerous communities and ejidos*, is vital for indigenous populations reliant on agricultural and forest resources. This Reserve covers a buffer zone where only sustainable land use activities are allowed and a core zone where only ecotourism and scientific research are allowed core (CONANP, 2000). Every winter it hosts the migration of the monarch butterfly (Danaus Plexippus) population (UNESCO, 2008). The MBBR also shelters endangered species, such as the salamanders Ambystoma rivulare and Pseudoeurycea bellii (Parra-Olea et al., 2005), and it is home to hundreds of flora and fauna species (DOF, 2000). The MBBR’s transition area, like many other protected natural areas of Mexico, is surrounding of agricultural lands, largely managed by small and medium-sized scale producers. [SOURCE: Page_24]

The central and southern municipalities of eastern Michoacan, which are part of the MBBR transition area, are predominantly cover with avocado plantations (SIAP, 2024b). This monoculture has been replacing traditional crops for the last twenty years (De la Vega-Rivera and Merino-Pérez, 2021; Denvir, 2023; Špirić et al., 2023). The changes in land use/land cover imply shifts in pesticide inputs. It is known that perennial plantations require higher volume of pesticides compared to the annual cycle agriculture associated with traditional crops (Mattikalli and Richards, 1996; Wang et al., 2018). This increase on pesticide usage requires well- [SOURCE: Page_24]

* Ejidos are a rustic modality of property founded by the Mexican State and unique in the world, resulted [SOURCE: Page_24]

of 20th-century agrarian reform, which a significant part of the territory was distributed to agrarian nuclei through communal land tenure systems (Morett-Sánchez and Cosío-Ruiz, 2017). [SOURCE: Page_24]

2 [SOURCE: Page_24]

informed users, which is necessary to mitigate the health risks and environmental contamination associated with improper agricultural practices (Blanco-Muñoz and Lacasaña, 2011; Lammoglia et al., 2017; Mehmood et al., 2021; Nandi et al., 2022). [SOURCE: Page_25]

In México, farmers have very little information about the health hazards associated with pesticide exposure for themselves and their family, for the consumers as well as the potential ecotoxicological impacts on the receiving ecosystems (Blanco-Muñoz and Lacasaña, 2011). Eastern Michoacan, is no exception, although scientific research covers many issues from the zone, such as deforestation, disturbance of local flora and fauna, unbalanced demand for freshwater resources, land use changes, etc. (Honey-Rosés, Baylis and Ramírez, 2011; Chávez-León et al., 2012; Ramírez et al., 2019; Denvir et al., 2022; Denvir, 2023; Špirić et al., 2023). The study of agricultural practices and human and environmental health adverse effects presented a knowledge gap, which is addressed in this present work. [SOURCE: Page_25]

Overall, we need to take action to prevent more deaths, and the development of more acute and chronic diseases related to the inappropriate use of pesticides, as the use of pesticide is projected to increase with the growing demand for food. Hundreds of research studies highlight the need for sustainable agricultural methods and policies to mitigate the negative impacts on human health, environment and biodiversity while safeguarding food sovereignty. [SOURCE: Page_25]

Objectives This thesis aims to evaluate the risks to human health and water contamination posed by pesticide use in eastern region of Michoacan. By a detailed evaluation of the agricultural practices and an analysis of pesticide monitoring in water. This study addresses various unexplored research areas in the region establishing the groundwork towards more sustainable pest management strategies. To achieve this, a multidisciplinary approach encompassed three main aspects, presented as specific objectives which were addressed through six scientific questions as outlined in figure I, which employed diverse methods that complement each other in a general methodology to achieve the main objective (Figure II). [SOURCE: Page_25]

5 [SOURCE: Page_27]

Encountered limitations [SOURCE: Page_28]

The fieldwork conducted and the soil characteristics obtained from the techniques outlined in the initial section of chapter V were originally intended to serve as inputs for a model. Initially, this Ph.D. thesis aimed to use a 1-D model to analyse the mobility of pesticides within intact column soils of predominant soils from the region. Collaborative efforts between the Institute of Geology (UNAM) in Mexico and the Institute of Environmental Geosciences (IGE- UGA) in France were undertaken to obtain the physico-chemical and hydrodynamic parameters of each sampled intact column soil. Unfortunately, due to the limitations of the PhD project's timeline, it was not feasible to analyse the degradation and sorption of pesticides in the specific sampled soils, which are necessary parameters in the model. While alternative approaches to address these parameters were contemplated, it became evident that they would not generate data of comparable quality to the initial analysed dataset. Consequently, incorporating such data into the modelling process would introduce significant uncertainty into the results. [SOURCE: Page_28]

The initial section of the chapter V presents processed results projected to be used once the sorption data is obtained. Transitioning to the second part of the chapter, the generated results were used as input for a pesticide mobility index and for an aquifer contamination vulnerability index in both watersheds. This effort was assumed to maintain consistency within the principal background of the thesis: the evaluation of water contamination risks. [SOURCE: Page_28]

Manuscript organization After this general introduction the present manuscript is structured into six chapters: [SOURCE: Page_28]

Chapter I is divided into two sections; the first one presents a literature review on the classification of pesticides and on the physical and chemical factors influencing pesticide mobility and fate in the environment. The second section presents an updated overview of the agricultural sector in México and Michoacan and summarizes the historical and contemporary research reviews based on pesticide residues studies. It concludes with an overview of the country’s challenging pesticide regulatory framework. [SOURCE: Page_28]

Chapter II examines the geographical characteristics of the eastern region of Michoacán, such as physiographic and geological features, hydrological patterns, edaphology, climate, and land use/land cover classifications. The description is particularly focused on three key zones studied in the thesis. These zones include two agricultural watersheds that have their recharge zone within the Monarch Butterfly Biosphere Reserve (MBBR). The Senguio watershed (86 km^2) is located in the northern part of the MBBR representing the traditional [SOURCE: Page_28]

6 [SOURCE: Page_28]

crops cultivation, and Zitácuaro watershed (442 km²) in the southern part of the MBBRR, representing the avocado plantations. The core zone of the MBBR is the third study zone. [SOURCE: Page_29]

Chapter III responds to the two first questions of the thesis: What are the most commonly used pesticides in the region? and do these pesticides pose a risk to human health? Achieving the first objective through a mixed-method approach, where qualitative techniques based on semi-structured interviews allow us to describe the most common agricultural practices. Subsequently, from the extracted information regarding the pesticide usage responses, a pesticide risk indicator was performed to evaluate the human health risks, which farmers are more exposed to. [SOURCE: Page_29]

Chapter IV details a robust chemical analysis developed to analyse pesticides residues in water samples from the three study zones. The results addressed the two questions of objective 2: What pesticides are present in water bodies near agricultural lands? And do these pesticides pose a risk to aquatic organisms? Representing the very first results in the region about the concentration of pesticides in different water bodies throughout both watersheds and in the core zone of the MBBR. The pesticide surface water concentrations obtained from the analysis served as input to calculate the environmental risk on aquatic organisms on three trophic levels. [SOURCE: Page_29]

Chapter V considers the types of soils in the region to respond to the las two questions: What are the intrinsic properties of soils in eastern Michoacan? And does the aquifer present a contamination risk according to pesticide mobility and soil and landscape characteristics? It first shows the results of physico-chemical and hydraulic properties analysed for five soil profiles sampled within the three study zones. The sampling strategy and methods along with the methodology followed by the second part of the chapter are also displayed. The second part of the chapter presents the potential mobility of pesticides through predominant soils and the groundwater pesticide contamination vulnerability. Both were evaluated with a compilation of geohydrographic information and the results of the soil characteristics as inputs. Although precise data are used for most necessary variables, the methodology of this chapter presents significant limitations due to the lack of other variables such as flow rate (in both watersheds), depth level measurements of the aquifer in the Senguio basin, as well as sorption and degradation measurements of the pesticides studied. However, this is the first study conducted in the region related to the vulnerability of groundwater contamination by used pesticides in the area. [SOURCE: Page_29]

Chapter VI this chapter presents the general conclusion along with the encountered limitations, and recent applications of the main findings, it concludes with the perspectives for further work in the region. [SOURCE: Page_29]

7 [SOURCE: Page_29]

8 [SOURCE: Page_30]

Chapter I Literature Review & State of the art on pesticide use and pesticide residues in México [SOURCE: Page_31]

First, this chapter conducts a thorough literature review on the physical and chemical factors influencing pesticide mobility and fate in the environment. It considers existing research, highlighting key concepts that will be broadly relevant to the subsequent five chapters. Then the chapter present an updated review of the agricultural   sector   in   México   and   summarizes    significant documented efforts in historical and contemporary research reviews based on residual pesticide studies to finally conclude with the review on the challenging pesticide legislation framework of the country. [SOURCE: Page_31]

9 [SOURCE: Page_31]

Chapter I: Literature review and state of the art on pesticide use and pesticide residues in México. [SOURCE: Page_32]

1.1 Literature review [SOURCE: Page_32]

1.1.1 Definition and classification of pesticides According to the US Environmental Protection Agency (EPA), a pesticide is "any substance or mixture of substances intended for preventing, destroying, repelling, or mitigating any pest". Pesticides consist not only of active ingredients, but also of inert ingredients that perform various functions such as carriers or stabilisers. The combination of active and inert ingredients is known as the pesticide formulation. Inert ingredients may enhance the efficacy of the active ingredient or improve the safety and handling of the product, but they do not contribute directly to the pesticidal action (Rajmohan, Chandrasekaran and Varjani, 2020). Pesticides can be used in agriculture, forestry and urban areas, although the agriculture sector is the major consumer (Sánchez-Bayo, 2019). The term agrochemical is often used to refer to all pesticides, fertilizers, plant growth regulators, and chemicals used in agriculture and animal husbandry (Bhanse et al., 2022). [SOURCE: Page_32]

Pesticides active ingredients (PAI) are classified based on various criteria such as their target application, chemical composition, mechanism of action, mode of entry, formulations, source of origin, and toxicity (Yadav et al., 2015a; Weis et al., 2019), most known classification relies on the target pest/organism, the chemical structure, and on the mechanism of action related to the mode of entry (Yadav et al., 2015a). In terms of public health, PAI are classified based on their toxicity: the World Health Organization (WHO) has classified PAI based on their hazardous effects (WHO, 2020), and the International Agency for Research on Cancer (IARC) based on the carcinogenic potential of chemicals, agents and exposures to humans (WHO- IARC, 2015). [SOURCE: Page_32]

1.1.1.1 Classification based on target organisms. Classification of PAI according to the target organism will present different mechanisms or modes of action that kill pests and weeds. Table 1.1 shows many categories, but in the world the most widely used are herbicides, fungicides, and insecticides, respectively. In 2020, worldwide consumption of herbicides reached almost 1.4 million tons, whereas consumption of fungicides and bactericides stood at around 606 thousand tons and insecticides at 471 thousand tons (Figure 1.1). [SOURCE: Page_32]

1.1.1.2 Classification based on chemical structure. The active ingredients of most pesticides are either organic (containing carbon) or inorganic (copper sulfate, ferrous sulfate, copper, sulfur, etc.) (Hassaan and El Nemr, 2020). Organic pesticides can be further categorized into two groups: natural (derived from naturally occurring sources) and synthetic (created through artificial chemical synthesis) (Kim, Kabir and Jahan, 2017). The most widespread and appropriate method for classifying pesticides depends on their chemical composition and description of the active ingredients. Figure 1.2 shows examples from this category. Depending on the chemical composition, synthetic organic formulations are classified into 4 main categories: organochlorines, organophosphates, carbamates, and pyrethroids (Damalas and Eleftherohorinos, 2011), their definition, and main characteristics are briefly described in Table 1.2. [SOURCE: Page_34]

act through a variety of mechanisms: primarily by targeting the nervous system, disrupting nerve function and causing paralysis to death, by acting on the sodium channel in nerve cell membranes. Pyrethroid insecticides work by these mechanisms, causing prolonged opening and overexcitation of the channels, leading to nerve dysfunction and paralysis in insects (Casida, 2009). Other types of mechanisms are respiratory or metabolic disruption, which cause mitochondrial disruption, damaging cell membranes. This is the mechanism of by which glyphosate disrupts cell membranes in plant tissues, leading to desiccation to death. Interfering with biochemical pathways in pests is             another common mechanism of action found in pesticides, for example, the herbicide atrazine acts by photosynthesis inhibition, causing the plant to die due to a lack of energy production (Casida, 2009; Ruomeng et al., 2023). Table 1.3 shows some examples of mechanisms of action related to pesticides. [SOURCE: Page_37]

1.1.1.4 Classification based on pesticide toxicity. Pesticides active ingredients are classified based on their toxicity, which refers to their ability to cause injury or illness. The study of pesticide toxicity primarily focuses on the investigation of the adverse effects of chemicals on human and animal individuals, but also at the population, community, and ecosystem level. Due to its closely linked with ecology the term [SOURCE: Page_37]

15 [SOURCE: Page_37]

used for this approach is ecotoxicology which integrates both toxicology and ecology (Campbell et al., 2022). [SOURCE: Page_38]

The classification proposed by the World Health Organization(WHO, 2020) (Table 1.4) considers three factors: the average lethal dose (LD50) of the formulated pesticide, the contact form of the pesticide (oral or dermal) and the type of formulation (solid or liquid). LD50 is a statistical estimate of the number of mg of toxicant per kg of body weight required to kill 50% of the test animals (WHO, 2020). The hazard in using a pesticide is related to the likelihood of exposure to harmful amounts of the pesticide (Damalas and Eleftherohorinos, 2011). The toxicity of a pesticide is determined by subjecting test animals to varying dosages of the pesticide's active ingredient (Erhirhie, Ihekwereme and Ilodigwe, 2018). [SOURCE: Page_38]

Table 1.4. Recommended classification of pesticides by hazard (WHO, 2020). [SOURCE: Page_38]

LD50 for the rat (mg/kg of body WHO class weight) Oral                     Dermal Ia        Extremely hazardous             <5                       <5 Ib        Highly hazardous                5-50                     50-200 II        Moderately hazardous            50-2000                  200-2000 III       Slightly hazardous              Over 2000                Over 2000 U         Unlikely to present acute 5000 or higher hazard [SOURCE: Page_38]

In the context of long-term pesticide toxicity, endpoints are used to assess chronic effects resulting from prolonged exposure to pesticides. These endpoints can include highly predictive adverse effects, mortality, reproductive performance, and developmental effects in different organisms (Mineau, 2005). There is also a growing emphasis on the development and validation of non-animal test methods, called New Approach Methods (NAMs), to conduct these toxicity tests, as they are considered to be more ethical and, in some cases, more cost- effective. Some of the NAMs include in vitro bioassays, computational models, cell-based models, and organs-on-chips. These methods are used to predict the potential effects of the bioavailable fraction(s) of a substance, mixture of substances or chemical on biological organisms and their ecosystems. (Erhirhie, Ihekwereme and Ilodigwe, 2018). [SOURCE: Page_38]

16 [SOURCE: Page_38]

1.1.2 Human health impact Effects on human health have been observed based on experimental and clinical evidence of toxicity as well as epidemiological evidence of long-term effect. People who are directly and indirectly exposed to pesticides can suffer from acute toxic effects and chronic diseases. [SOURCE: Page_39]

1.1.2.1 Acute toxic effects The acute effects of pesticides on humans can vary depending on factors such as the type of pesticide, the time of exposure and the route of exposure (refer to section 1.3). These health problems can be severe and are characterised to emerge shortly after exposure or within 48 hours of a single episode of ingestion, inhalation, or dermal contact with the pesticide (PAN-UK, 2017). Symptoms of acute toxicity from pesticides can include respiratory tract irritation, sore throat, cough, abdominal pain, dizziness, allergic sensitization, eye and skin irritation, nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, headache, loss of consciousness, extreme weakness, seizures, and even death in severe cases (Mohamed et al., 2009; Gawarammana and Buckley, 2011; Negatu, Dugassa and Mekonnen, 2021; Sudan et al., 2022). [SOURCE: Page_39]

In a study of 6,300 pesticide-users from 24 countries, headache and dizziness (52% of all identified pesticides), followed by nausea/vomiting (38% of all product reports) were reported as the most common acute effects associated with the use of insecticides, fungicides and herbicides (Tomenson and Matthews, 2009) (Figure 1.3). Synthetic pyrethroids and fungicides were the most likely to be associated with a sign or symptom every time used (Tomenson and Matthews, 2009). [SOURCE: Page_39]

17 [SOURCE: Page_39]

Figure 1.3. Acute effects of different types of pesticides reported by users. Source: Tomenson and Matthews (2009). [SOURCE: Page_40]

1.1.2.2 Chronic diseases Pesticide exposure has also been associated with long-term chronic health effects, including changes in neural development, increased risk of neurodegenerative diseases, and disorders in the reproductive and developmental systems (Mamane et al., 2015; Koutros et al., 2016; Kim, Kabir and Jahan, 2017; Osborne et al., 2021). Chronic diseases are characterized by their generally slow progression and long-term duration, which are considered as the leading cause of mortality in the new world (Islam et al., 2022). Pesticide long-term effects include an elevated rate of chronic diseases such as different types of cancers, diabetes, neurodegenerative disorders like Parkinson's, Alzheimer's, and amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS), birth defects, and reproductive disorders (Kim, Kabir and Jahan, 2017). There is well- documented evidence regarding the correlation of occupational exposure to pesticides and chronic bronchitis (Tarmure et al., 2020), bladder cancer risk (Koutros et al., 2016), respiratory pathologies (Tarmure et al., 2020) [SOURCE: Page_40]

18 [SOURCE: Page_40]

There is also evidence of the association of pesticide exposure with the incidence of epigenetic modifications, endocrine disruption, mitochondrial dysfunction, oxidative stress, endoplasmic reticulum stress, and impairment of ubiquitin proteasome system (Mostafalou and Abdollahi, 2013). [SOURCE: Page_41]

The carcinogenic properties of pesticides can be influenced by a number of complex factors, including age, sex, individual susceptibility, level and duration of exposure, and concurrent exposure to other carcinogenic chemicals (Mostafalou and Abdollahi, 2013). There is documented evidence from laboratory and epidemiological studies of the carcinogenicity of substances associated to different types of cancer. Based on these type of studies the International Agency for Research on Cancer's (IARC) part of the World Health Organization (WHO) classifies substances included pesticides active ingredients into cancer hazards agents, the IARC categorized these agents into “groups” and publish reports through the program of Monographs, the aim of the Monographs is to evaluate evidence of carcinogenicity at any stage in the carcinogenesis process (IARC, 2015b). Agents are selected for review on the basis of two main criteria: (a) there is evidence of human exposure and (b) there is some evidence or suspicion of carcinogenicity (IARC, 2015b). Table 1.5 shows the classification groups of the IARC and some examples. [SOURCE: Page_41]

Table 1.5. Classification of carcinogenic substances proposed by the IARC. Source: (International Agency for Research on Cancer, 2020) [SOURCE: Page_41]

Group         Characteristics                 Examples Group 1       Carcinogenic to humans          Lindane, HCB, Arsenic. Group 2A Probably carcinogenic to Glyphosate, Diazinon, Malathion, Parathion. humans Group 2B Possibly carcinogenic to 2,4-D, Dieldrin, Heptachlor humans Group 3       Not    classifiable  as carcinogenic in humans Group 4       Probably            not carcinogenic to humans [SOURCE: Page_41]

Additionally, the combined impact of multiple pesticide residues on human health and the environment remains limited. The "cocktail effects" of pesticides can amplify toxicity or produce unexpected effects (Hernández, Gil and Lacasaña, 2017). This impact extends to pesticide metabolites as well, which are the by-products formed when pesticides break down. Most metabolites have not been thoroughly studied, and their potential toxicity is largely unknown. [SOURCE: Page_41]

19 [SOURCE: Page_41]

1.1.3 Ecological effects [SOURCE: Page_42]

Pesticides have significant ecological effects that can disrupt biodiversity, alter community structures, and impact ecosystem functions. The impact of pesticides on the environment goes beyond the soils and organisms directly exposed to them on crop fields. Studies have shown that several non-target organisms, including bees, butterflies, and other beneficial insects, are affected by the use of pesticides (Yadav et al., 2015b; Ccanccapa, Masiá, Andreu, et al., 2016; Krishnan et al., 2020). [SOURCE: Page_42]

Particularly herbicides and insecticides have been studied regarding the effects on freshwater aquatic systems (Rumschlag et al., 2020). For example, herbicides can decrease phytoplankton abundance, leading to reduced oxygen levels and impaired aquatic life. In contrast, insecticides may initially increase phytoplankton productivity through changes in zooplankton populations, but ultimately disrupt the balance of aquatic ecosystems (Rumschlag et al., 2020). Pesticides pose risks to a wide range of wildlife, including birds, fish, and beneficial insects like bees. Direct exposure can lead to various health issues, including cancer, reproductive problems, and neurotoxicity (Jepson et al., 2020). [SOURCE: Page_42]

The effects of pesticides can vary depending on the organism. For instance, in monarch butterflies, certain pesticides such as the herbicides atrazine and metolachlor, as well as the fungicides azoxystrobin and trifloxystrobin, have shown no impact during the larval stage. However, these chemicals do affect adult monarchs, leading to reduced wing length. This reduction has significant implications for the migratory success of monarchs from North America (Olaya-Arenas et al., 2020). [SOURCE: Page_42]

Birds face significant problems from exposure to pesticides, which can lead to direct mortality, sub-lethal effects, and indirect impacts through habitat and food web disruption. According with Li et al., (2020) the increased use of neonicotinoids between 2008 and 2014 in United States resulted in statistically significant declines in bird biodiversity, particularly affecting grassland and insectivorous birds. These groups experienced average annual population reductions of 4% and 3%, respectively, compared to a scenario without neonicotinoid use. When considering the dynamic effects of declining bird populations on future growth, these rates increase to 12% for grassland birds and 5% for insectivorous birds. While neonicotinoids also negatively impacted non-grassland and non-insectivorous birds, the average annual reduction for these groups was smaller, at 2% during the same period (Li, Miao and Khanna, 2020). [SOURCE: Page_42]

20 [SOURCE: Page_42]

1.1.4 Routes of pesticide exposure Exposure to hazardous pesticides poses thus a serious health risk to humans, particularly for those working in the agricultural sector (Damalas and Eleftherohorinos, 2011). Occupational exposure in agriculture, constitutes one of the most serious health hazards associated with pesticides used for agricultural purposes. These concerns arise from the frequent and direct exposure of farmers to pesticides during the mixing and application of pesticides, as well as while working in treated fields. Farmers exposure occurs through different routes : skin contact, intoxication, and inhalation during the handling and/or treatment of pesticides to crops (Bergmann, 2019). In addition, farmers, but also the general population, can be indirectly exposed to pesticides via contaminated media such as air, food, soil, and drinking water, especially if they live in the vicinity of agricultural fields (Kim, Kabir and Jahan, 2017; Seddik, Marstani and ALazzam, 2017). [SOURCE: Page_43]

Exposure is characterized by identifying the processes and mechanisms that bring organisms into contact with the stressor(s) of concern and quantifying the frequency, magnitude, and duration of such contact (Bartell, 2008). The main routes of human exposure to pesticides are through the food chain, air, water, soil, flora, and fauna (Kim, Kabir and Jahan, 2017). Exposure to pesticides can occur directly from occupational, agricultural, and household use, while they can also be transferred indirectly through diet (Figure 1.4). Occupational exposure to pesticides occurs in various settings, including the manufacturing, transport, storage, preparation, application, and re-entry into treated fields. Agricultural workers, such as farmers and farm workers, are among the most significantly affected groups, along with pesticide manufacturing industry workers (Mamane et al., 2015). Moreover, the general population may be exposed to pesticides due to their application on golf courses, around major roads, agricultural fields, etc.(Kim, Kabir and Jahan, 2017). [SOURCE: Page_43]

There are four common ways pesticides can enter the human body: dermal, oral, eye, and respiratory pathways. The risk associated to pesticide exposition usually increases with the dosage applied or concentration exposed to, frequency of exposure, windows of exposure and of course to the toxicity of the chemical of interest (Rico-Martínez et al., 2022). [SOURCE: Page_43]

Concerning dermal exposure, solid forms of pesticides (e.g., powders, dusts, and granules) are in general not as readily absorbed through the skin and other body tissues as liquid formulations. However, the hazard from skin absorption increases when workers are handling (e.g., mixing) concentrated pesticides (e.g., one with a high active ingredient content). Certain parts of the body are more susceptible to pesticide absorption than others (Kalyabina et al., 2021). For example, the rate at which dermal absorption proceeds is different for each part of the body (Figure 1.4). The risk of exposure can be reduced by the use of personal [SOURCE: Page_43]

21 [SOURCE: Page_43]

protective equipment, good handling, and protective application procedures (Blanco-Muñoz and Lacasaña, 2011). [SOURCE: Page_44]

Figure 1.4. Route of exposure to pesticides and potential effects to humans (a) and intensity of dermal exposure to pesticide on different part of the body. (Kim, Kabir and Jahan, 2017; Kalyabina et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_44]

1.1.5 Environmental fate of pesticides The environmental fate of pesticides depends on the physical and chemical properties of the pesticide as well as the environmental conditions. Pesticides may become airborne, get into soil, enter water bodies, be taken up by plants or enter in the food chain (metabolized or bioaccumulated), be degraded or persistent in the environment for a long time (Arias-Estévez et al., 2008) (Figure 1.5). The physical and chemical properties of the pesticide determine how likely it will be mobile in the environment (sorption properties), how well it dissolves in water (water solubility), and how likely it is to become airborne (volatility) (Kalyabina et al., 2021). The environmental conditions will also have an impact on the pesticides fates: air, water and soil temperature and humidity, pluviometry, solar radiation, and soil properties (organic matter content, microbial activity, soil structure or porous system, mineralogy). Management practices such as type of cultivation, time and rate of application, method have to be taken into account (Sarmah, Müller and Ahmad, 2004; Boithias et al., 2014). [SOURCE: Page_44]

22 [SOURCE: Page_44]

Figure 1.5. Schematic representation of pesticide transport routes after application. Source: Kalyabina et al., 2021. [SOURCE: Page_45]

1.1.5.1 Environmental transport and transformation processes Environmental transport and processes of pesticides can be related to many factors and pesticide intrinsic properties. Their mobility will also depend on agricultural practices, and local-meteorological conditions, as well as the overall properties of the soil and landscape, which can shift the pathway of pesticides mobility (Figure 1.6) (Gish et al., 2011; Prado et al., 2016; Bergmann, 2019). The move of pesticides from soil occurs through various processes, including leaching, runoff, and erosion (Maggi, Tang and Tubiello, 2023). Understanding these processes is crucial for assessing the potential environmental impacts of pesticide mobility and developing strategies to mitigate their movement and accumulation in sensitive areas. The study of pesticide fate in the environment involves different scales in space and time, which makes complex the studies that take into account multiple factor (Figure 1.7) (Gish et al., 2011). [SOURCE: Page_45]

23 [SOURCE: Page_45]

1.1.5.2 Sorption-desorption Sorption is one of the major processes affecting the interactions between pesticides and the solid phase in the soil environment. It plays an important role in regulating the rates and magnitudes of other processes that govern the fate and transport of organic contaminants in soils, sediments, and water systems (Sarmah, Müller and Ahmad, 2004). [SOURCE: Page_47]

The terminology associated with sorption includes the process of adsorption, which involves the accumulation of a substance or material (adsorbates) at the interface between a solid surface (adsorbent) and a surrounding solution. The umbrella term "sorption" is employed to characterize any system wherein a sorbate (such as an atom, molecule, or molecular ion) interacts with a sorbent (the solid surface), leading to an accumulation at the interface between the sorbate and the sorbent (Figure 1.8) (Borda and Sparks, 2008). [SOURCE: Page_47]

Figure 1.8. Schematic representation of adsorption, and desorption processes in soils.                  Source: https://citrusindustry.net/2020/10/14/understanding-the-fate-and-persistence-of-herbicides-in-soils/. [SOURCE: Page_47]

Typically, sorption is a reversible phenomenon, and its opposite process is referred to as desorption. Consequently, sorption is accountable not only for the retention of substances but also for their release. In most cases, this process is quantified by specific equations that describe the quantity of substance adhered to the surface based on the fluid concentration. [SOURCE: Page_47]

25 [SOURCE: Page_47]

These equations, known as isotherms, include the well-known Henry’s, Langmuir, and Freundlich isotherms (Fesch et al., 1998; Artioli, 2008). [SOURCE: Page_48]

It is possible to define two kinds of adsorption: physical adsorption (or physisorption) when the adsorbate adheres to the surface because of weak physical forces (van der Waals interactions), and chemical adsorption (or chemisorption) if the adsorbate is chemically bound to the adsorbent’s surface through stronger forces: indeed in this case adsorbate forms a real chemical bond (usually covalent) with the adsorbent’s surface (Artioli, 2008). [SOURCE: Page_48]

Pesticide sorption by soils is expressed by the adsorption coefficient (Kd or Kf depending on the mathematical description of the isotherm) (Weber et al., 2000). It is commonly determined using the slurry method which consists of measuring the quantity of pesticide adsorbed from a specified concentration in liquid phase by a specified mass of soil (Weber, Wilkerson and Reinhardt, 2004). Kd will be equal to the concentration of chemical in soil divided by the concentration of chemical substance in water. Values for Kd can vary greatly depending on the organic content of soil. Since adsorption occurs predominantly by partition into the soil organic matter, it is useful to normalize the Kd to the organic carbon content of a soil and express the distribution with the organic carbon-water partition coefficient the Koc or Kfoc. The carbon-water partition coefficient (Koc) measures the adsorption of a chemical substance onto soil organic matter per unit of water (Knauer et al., 2017) and is expressed as follows: [SOURCE: Page_48]

Koc or Kfoc= (Kd * 100)/ % Organic carbon [SOURCE: Page_48]

A very high value means it is strongly adsorbed onto soil and organic matter and does not move throughout the soil. A very low value means it is highly mobile in soil. For pesticides, a higher Koc or Kd means that such pesticides are less likely to leach and contaminate groundwater (Salazar-Ledesma et al., 2018). [SOURCE: Page_48]

1.1.5.3 Volatilization Volatilization is the transfer of pesticides from soil or water to the atmosphere (Lin, 2008). Potential volatility is related to vapor pressure of the pesticide: the higher the vapor pressure, the more volatile the pesticide will be (Tudi et al., 2021). Volatilization of pesticides depends on environmental conditions, such as temperatures, relative humidity, and air movement (Spencer, Farmer and Cliath, 1973). When the pesticide is volatized it can move with the wind away from it source of emission and contaminating nearby areas such as other fields or crops and urban areas (Figueiredo et al., 2021). Pesticides can reach the atmosphere by different pathways: by the wind during drift, by the evaporation of drop-lets emitted from the nozzles, or by the wind soil erosion processes (Figure 1.9) (Bedos et al., 2002). [SOURCE: Page_48]

26 [SOURCE: Page_48]

Figure 1.9. Processes involved in the transfer of pesticides to the atmosphere. Source: Bedos et al., 2002. [SOURCE: Page_49]

1.1.5.4 Biotic and abiotic transformations Biotic and abiotic transformations of pesticides occur simultaneously in the environment. Biotic transformations involve the degradation of pesticides by microorganisms, while abiotic transformations include processes such as hydrolysis, oxidation-reduction, and photochemical reactions (Chen et al., 2022). [SOURCE: Page_49]

Biodegradation [SOURCE: Page_49]

The biodegradation of pesticides is a natural process in which microorganisms break down pesticide molecules. Microorganisms such as bacteria, fungi, and algae are involved in the biodegradation of pesticides. This process is furthermore dependent on the presence or absence of oxygen; it means aerobic or anaerobic conditions (Jorgensen, 2008). The efficiency of pesticides biodegradation can vary depending on the specific pesticide properties and environmental conditions (Pathak et al., 2022). The rate of biodegradation in soil depends on four variables: (1) availability of pesticide or metabolite to the microorganisms, (2) physiological status of the microorganisms, (3) survival and/or proliferation of pesticide degrading microorganisms at contaminated site and (4) sustainable population of these microorganisms (Singh, 2008). Pesticide biodegradation is a targeted remediation technique that, under controlled conditions, offers an environmentally friendly method for treating contaminated soils (Romantschuk et al., 2023). [SOURCE: Page_49]

Photodegradation [SOURCE: Page_49]

Photodegradation stands out as one of the most potent mechanisms through which pesticides can diminish their half-lives in the environment, occurring in the plant, soil surface, and air though the photolysis process (Katagi, 2004). In the process of photolysis, radiant [SOURCE: Page_49]

27 [SOURCE: Page_49]

energy in the form of photons directly or indirectly disrupts chemical bonds. Upon absorption of a photon by the compound, the bond undergoes instant breakage or reordering, leading to the formation of a more stable compound (Rico-Martínez et al., 2022; Wasewar, 2022). [SOURCE: Page_50]

There are two types of photolysis: direct and indirect. Direct photolysis occurs when a pesticide molecule absorbs photons and undergoes a chemical reaction, while indirect photolysis involves the participation of other molecules, such as dissolved organic matter or reactive oxygen species, to initiate the transformation of the pesticide molecule (Katagi, 2018). [SOURCE: Page_50]

The process of photolysis can be influenced by various factors, including the chemical structure of the pesticide, the wavelength of the light, and the environmental conditions. For instance, the absorption and emission spectra of a pesticide can provide insights into its excited states and potential reactions during photolysis (Katagi, 2004). Photolysis is a significant process for the degradation and transformation of pesticide residues, particularly in aqueous environments and on plant and soil surfaces. [SOURCE: Page_50]

Hydrolysis [SOURCE: Page_50]

Hydrolysis of pesticides involves the breakdown of pesticide molecules through a reaction with water. The hydrolysis of a pesticide involves a reaction with a water molecule, facilitated by specific catalysis from protons, hydroxides, and at times, inorganic ions like phosphate ions found in the aquatic environment, contributing to general acid-base catalysis (Katagi, 2002). Chemically mediated hydrolysis can occur in the presence of H2O, H3O+, and OH–and has been termed neutral, acid, and alkaline hydrolysis (Sarmah, Müller and Ahmad, 2004). Hydrolysis can occur in various environmental settings, such as the soil surface, the root zone, or whenever a source of water is available. The hydrolysis of pesticides can lead to the formation of degradation products, which may have different properties and environmental behavior (Katagi, 2004). Some insecticides such as carbofuran, dimethoate, methyl parathion, monocrotophos, phorate, and trichlorfon are known to be sensitive to hydrolysis. The susceptibility of pesticides to hydrolysis is an important consideration in understanding their environmental fate and potential risks (Dyguda-Kazimierowicz, Roszak and Sokalski, 2014). [SOURCE: Page_50]

1.1.5.5 Bioavailability and bioaccumulation Bioavailability is referred to as the extent and rate to which the active ingredient of a substance or active portion from the product is absorbed and becomes available at the site of action (Shein-Chung, 2014).The bioavailability and overall toxicity of current-use pesticides can undergo significant changes due to the presence of dissolved and particulate organic matter, as well as the lipophilicity of compounds. Consequently, commonly recognized predictors of the bioavailable fraction values are employed to characterize the behaviour of [SOURCE: Page_50]

28 [SOURCE: Page_50]

pesticides, such as the logarithm of the octanol-water partitioning coefficient (log(kOW)), the logarithm of the organic carbon/water partition coefficient (log(kOC)), the logarithm of the octanol/air partition coefficient (log(KOA)) (Seth, Mackay and Muncke, 1999; Kalyabina et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_51]

The octanol-water partition coefficient (kOW) is a measure of the relationship between the lipophilicity (fat solubility) and hydrophilicity (water solubility) of a chemical compound (Leo, Hansch and Elkins, 1971). Substances with high log kOW values tend to bioconcentrate in living organisms because of their affinity to fat and lipids. Therefore, kOW is used as a screening test for bioaccumulation potential, with chemicals having very high log KOW values being of greater concern due to their potential to bioconcentrate in living organisms (Cumming and Rücker, 2017). [SOURCE: Page_51]

For a chemical to be bioaccumulated by the organism, it must be bioavailable, available for uptake. Bioaccumulation is the process wherein organisms accumulate and enrich contaminants in relation to their concentration in the surrounding environment. This phenomenon represents the overall outcome of various processes, including respiratory and dietary uptake, loss through egestion, passive diffusion, metabolism, transfer to offspring, and growth (Figure 1.10) (Borga, 2008; Katagi, 2009; Ray and Shaju, 2023). Bioaccumulation is not a passive arbitrary process, but a process that is influenced by the molecular properties of the chemical, the amount of particulate matter in the water, and the properties of the algae or animal such as size and lipid content (Borga, 2008). Some of the pesticides more highly bioaccumulate present characteristics such as lipophilicity, stability (persistent compounds), and high affinity for proteins. Some examples include organochlorine pesticides such as DDT, chlordane, and dieldrin, as well as certain organophosphate pesticides like chlorpyrifos and diazinon, and pyrethroids such as cypermethrin, deltamethrin, permethrin, bifenthrin, among others (Jayaraj, Megha and Sreedev, 2016; Main et al., 2020; Ray and Shaju, 2023). [SOURCE: Page_51]

Figure 1.10. Bioaccumulation of contaminants (dots) to an organism (fish) as a net result of uptake and loss processes (arrows). Uptake is direct from the water by respiration and indirect through the diet. Source: Borga, 2008 [SOURCE: Page_51]

29 [SOURCE: Page_51]

Bioaccumulation encompasses more specific processes, namely bioconcentration and biomagnification. Bioconcentration involves the direct partitioning of chemicals between water and organisms, resulting in elevated concentrations within the organisms. On the other hand, biomagnification occurs due to the uptake of contaminants from the diet, leading to higher concentrations in the consumer compared to the original diet. Consequently, biomagnification causes an increase in chemical concentration with each higher trophic level in the food web (Figure 1.11) (Borga, 2008; Katagi, 2009; Ray and Shaju, 2023). [SOURCE: Page_52]

Figure 1.11. Bioaccumulation and biomagnification toxic poisoning process diagram. Source: Free image from stock.adobe.com [SOURCE: Page_52]

1.1.5.6 Surface runoff Runoff is determined by the slope of the region where the water movement is taking place, by the soil properties and soil cover. Runoff occurs when rain or irrigation water adds water to a surface faster than the rate with which water enters soil; pesticides can move with the runoff as dissolved compounds or attached to soil particles of the eroding soil (Rico- Martínez et al., 2022). Erosion is the movement of soil particles from the application site by heavy rains or excess irrigation (Mac Loughlin, Peluso and Marino, 2022). [SOURCE: Page_52]

Runoff can occur in almost all agricultural soils, even in plane terrains. However, its frequency depends on the weather. Surface runoff is mainly generated by two mechanisms, infiltration excess (Horton) runoff and saturation excess (Dunne) runoff (Zhenghui et al., 2003). The Hortonian runoff is produced when the capacity of infiltration and superficial storage of soil is exceeded the current precipitation; on the other hand, in the saturation runoff is generated when the groundwater level ascends to the soil surface; in these cases, any rain on the soil causes immediate runoff (Tudi et al., 2021; Rico-Martínez et al., 2022). 30 [SOURCE: Page_52]

1.1.5.7 Leaching/infiltration The movement of dissolved pesticides through the soil profile is often caused by the action of water, rainwater, or irrigation water. In this way, the vertical transport of several pesticides into the soil column by runoff is often observed and the removal of these molecules from the topsoil to lower subsoil depths. This process is known as leaching, and it is through this phenomenon that the downward migration of organic contaminants through the unsaturated zone to groundwater can occur (Vagi and Petsas, 2022). [SOURCE: Page_53]

Leaching occurs when soluble pesticides move with infiltrating water through the soil profile to the water table (Pathak et al., 2009). The closer the water table is to the surface, the greater the risk of contamination. However, the mobility of pesticides through soil depends on soil type (soil physical and chemical characteristics), and chemical and physical characteristics of pesticides. (Pathak et al., 2022). [SOURCE: Page_53]

Pesticides that are highly water soluble, relatively persistent, and not readily sorbed to soil particles have the greatest potential for movement. Strongly adsorbed pesticide molecules do not leach or move unless the soil particles to which they are adsorbed move with water (Glinski et al., 2018). Strongly adsorption of pesticides molecules into soil particles can be translated in high KOC values. Depending on the KOC value, once applied to soil a portion of the pesticide will be adsorbed to soil particles, while the remaining pesticide mass will be associated with the liquid and/or vapor phases (Gish et al., 2011). KOC is linked to the organic carbon content of the soil. Water drainage or infiltration of water will depend on the soil hydraulic conductivity, which is directly linked to the soil porous system, more specifically to the size and connection of the pores. Pesticides movement of preferential flow through well connected pores are responsible of early contamination of groundwater (Beulke, Brown and Jarvis, 2001). [SOURCE: Page_53]

31 [SOURCE: Page_53]

1.2 Context of pesticides use in Mexico. [SOURCE: Page_54]

1.2.1 Agricultural land and of crops in Mexico México has a land area of 1 million 964 thousand 375 square kilometers, of which 23.4 million hectares are devoted to agriculture, making it the 14th largest nation in the world and the 12th largest in terms of agricultural production (SADER and SIAP, 2023). In 2022 the Ministry for agriculture and rural development (Secretaría de Agricultura y Desarrollo Rural [SADER] in Spanish) through the Agri-food and Fisheries Information Service (Servicio de Información Agroalimentaria y Pesquera [SIAP] in Spanish) reported 20.6 million hectares cultivated encompassing 304 crops (SIAP, 2024a). From these crops, 676 varieties covering 288 crops are grown in the open field, while another 157 varieties of 57 crops are grown in greenhouses or other type of protected agriculture^6, using shade nets or polyethylene tunnels (polytunnel) (SIAP, 2024a). [SOURCE: Page_54]

Of the 23.4 million hectares of national agricultural lands, a quarter is occupied by maize cultivation (SADER and SIAP, 2020). However, the greatest yield is obtained from sugar cane, which covering only 3.6% (841,613 ha) of the country's total agricultural area, had a production of 51,086,765 tons in 2023, more than double the production of maize (22,046,099) (SIAP, 2024a). For the same year, alfalfa cultivation comes in second place with a yield of 35,846,327 tons covering 401,100 hectares of the total agricultural land. On the other hand, the largest exported yields are for avocados and tomatoes with an amount of exported tons in 2023 of 2,652,500 and 1,807,464 respectively (SIAP, 2024a). According to the last agricultural census of 2022, in the last fifteen years, tomato production has increased the double due to production under protected agriculture (INEGI, 2023b). [SOURCE: Page_54]

The main cyclical crops^7 with the highest production in tonnes are maize (grain), fodder oats, sorghum, wheat grains, green chilli and tomatoes, while the highest production of perennial crops^8 encompass sugar cane, alfalfa, orange, lime, banana, avocado and agave [SOURCE: Page_54]

6 Protected agriculture is agricultural production utilizing protective structures such as greenhouses and [SOURCE: Page_54]

shade houses to protect crops from climatic and biological damages and improve growth conditions (Wu, Guan and Huang, 2022). 7 Cyclical crops are those whose growing season is less than 12 months and require a new sowing to [SOURCE: Page_54]

obtain harvests. They are concentrated in two productive periods: -Spring-summer -Autumn-winter (SADER and SIAP, 2023). 8 Perennial: defines long-cycle crops, whose vegetative period extends beyond 12 months; once planted, several harvests are obtained. For administrative records, they are considered from January to December (SADER and SIAP, 2023); 32 [SOURCE: Page_54]

(Figure 1.13 and 1.14 respectively). These crops include open field and protected agriculture production in 2023, they have been in the same ranking for the last 10 years, although in 2023 fodder oats production decreased 41.57% in relation to the historical production over the last 10 years (SADER and SIAP, 2023), whereas the avocado and agave production increased 55% and 25% respectively in comparison to the production of 2013 (1 467 837 tons and 1 899 735 tons) (SIAP, 2024a). [SOURCE: Page_55]

Figure 1.12. Highest yields of annual cycle crops in Mexico (2023). Numbers represent the production in tons for the year 2023. Source: Elaborated with data of SIAP (2024). [SOURCE: Page_55]

Figure 1.13. Highest yields of perennial crops in Mexico (2023). Numbers represent the production in tons for the year 2023. Source: Elaborated with data of SIAP (2024) [SOURCE: Page_55]

33 [SOURCE: Page_55]

As far as exports are concerned, Mexico is part of 14 free trade agreements, which have created a network for exports to 55 countries, the main destinations being the United States, Japan, and Canada. In December 2023, for the ninth consecutive year, Mexico has a surplus in agri-food exports, with the main agricultural products corresponding to avocado, tomato, citrus, cucumber, strawberry, cabbage, among others (Figure 1.15) (SADER and SIAP, 2024). Mexico is the world's leading producer of avocados and blackberries, the second largest producer of raspberries and limes, and the third largest producer of asparagus and grapefruit (SADER, 2024). [SOURCE: Page_56]

1.2.2 Agricultural land and crops in Michoacan According to the results of the 2022 agricultural census, the state of Michoacan has a total rural area of 5.9 million hectares, of which 1.3 million hectares are used for agriculture. Of the total agricultural area, 77% is reported as active or cultivated. Michoacan has the 7th largest agricultural area among the 32 states of the Mexican Republic, where 85.8% of the agricultural labor force is made up of men and 14.2% of women (INEGI, 2023a). Michoacan, produces more than 50 crops covering 208 varieties in open field and protected agriculture. In terms of protected agriculture, it accounts for almost 20% of the national total, making it the State with the largest area devoted to this type of agriculture for the third year running. Red tomatoes, green chillies and berries (blueberries, strawberries, raspberries, blackberries and redcurrants) are the main crops grown with this type of agriculture (SADER and SIAP, 2023; SIAP, 2024a). Of the 49 crops produced in Michoacan, the highest yields are reported for avocado, corn, sugarcane, lime, gladiolus, two fodder crops: oats and alfalfa, strawberries, [SOURCE: Page_56]

34 [SOURCE: Page_56]

agave, and sorghum (SIAP, 2024b) (Figure 1.16). The state is the largest producer of avocados, making Mexico the world's leading producer and exporter. [SOURCE: Page_57]

Avocado production [SOURCE: Page_57]

The avocado orchards are concentrated in western and central Michoacán, which is known as the 'Avocado Strip' or 'Avocado Belt'. Based on the most recent information provided by the SIAP, as of 2024, there are 19 municipalities in the central-western region and 5 municipalities in eastern Michoacán where agricultural lands are actively dedicated to avocado plantation. This municipalities collectively form the Avocado Strip of Michoacán (Figure 1.17). This area is favored due to its suitable climatic conditions for avocados and its fertile volcanic soils, which are rich in organic matter (Barsimantov and Navia Antezana, 2012). Over the last two decades, avocado orchards have been consistently expanded across different soil conditions and numerous municipalities within the state, reaching the eastern region of Michoacán. The global popularity of the avocado has led to a rapid increase in demand, with production in Mexico increasing more than doubling in the last decade, with Michoacan producing the 72.8% of the national production in 2023 (SIAP, 2024b) (Figure 1.18). [SOURCE: Page_57]

1.2.3 Pesticides use in Mexican agriculture. Despite the enormous agricultural land, pesticide use in Mexico remains below the global average with an average of 1.82 kg per hectare of cropland according with the FAO (2020), but, there are several notable shortcomings in the country's pesticide management system, with no specific overarching national policy on the use of pesticides, this represent a major risk to the environment, farmers and the population in general (OCDE, 2021). [SOURCE: Page_59]

Pesticides utilized in Mexico are categorized based on their composition and intended application. These include chemical formulations designed for agricultural, domestic, forestry, industrial, horticultural, livestock, and urban purposes. Among the most prevalent chemical groups are arsenicals, organochlorines, triazines, organophosphates, benzoylureas, carbamates, neonicotinoids, dinitro compounds, organometallic compounds, thiocarbamates, pyrethroids, as well as derivatives of coumarin and urea (Bejarano-González, 2018). [SOURCE: Page_59]

Figure 1.19 illustrates pesticide use per hectare of cropland in leading agricultural countries. However, data on actual pesticide use in Mexico is inconsistent, as pesticide sales in the country are reported only in terms of total volume rather than the volume of active ingredients. This reporting method limits the ability to compare Mexico's pesticide use with that of other countries, where data on active ingredients is typically available (OECD, 2021). [SOURCE: Page_59]

Figure 1.18.Quantity (kilograms) of pesticides used per hectare of cropland in different countries. Source: Ourworldindata.org with FAO data. [SOURCE: Page_59]

37 [SOURCE: Page_59]

In Mexico, highly hazardous pesticides are used in intensive agriculture (monocultures and agro-industrial chains of the domestic market), as well as in extensive agriculture (small- scale production by peasants and indigenous communities) (Bejarano-González, 2018). The Official Pesticide Catalogue of the Federal Commission for the Protection against Sanitary Risks (COFEPRIS, in Spanish) published a list of 283 authorized pesticides in Mexico, with at that moment 20 prohibited pesticides, and other 12 listed as restricted use (Gobierno de México, 1991a). The list was updated in 2016 and the list of prohibited pesticides increased to 25 prohibited pesticides. [SOURCE: Page_60]

Nevertheless, there are 137 authorized pesticides in México that are banned or not authorized in other countries due to their toxicity and the risks they pose to human health and the environment (Gómez-González, 2017) and in 2024 the authorized pesticides in Mexico have more than 9499 registered pesticide formulations (COFEPRIS, 2024). Table 1.6 shows the list of pesticides banned in more than 30 countries that are still authorized in Mexico. It also shows the list of prohibited pesticides and "restricted pesticides" that, according to the official catalogue, can only be used under the guidance of qualified personnel (Gobierno de México, 1991b). This catalogue remark DDT as a pesticide reserved for the Ministry of Health to control malaria and other vector-borne diseases, and it cannot be commercialized or used for other purposes, a pesticide that has been banned for any purpose in more than 70 countries. The same goes for the herbicide paraquat, which is labelled as a restricted pesticide, but is a widely used pesticide in agriculture, as reported in several recent studies in México. [SOURCE: Page_60]

According with the results, the state with the highest number of studies was Nayarit, followed by Yucatan and Tabasco in the south of Mexico. The analysis showed the use of 277 pesticides [SOURCE: Page_61]

formulated with a single active ingredient (a.i.), as well as 48 pesticides whose formulations contained more than one active ingredient (SEMARNAT and INECC, 2022). Of the total single a.i. pesticides, 137 are classified as Highly Hazardous Pesticides (HHPs) according to the PAN International 2021 List and, 134 are banned in different countries of the world (PAN, 2021). [SOURCE: Page_62]

The most frequently reported pesticides used in México during the period 1980-2018 include according with this analysis were: methyl parathion (89 records), carbofuran (85), 2,4- D (76), cypermethrin (74), endosulfan (71), among others (Figure 1.20). Of the 277 a.i. substances                   contained     in     the   reported   formulated   products,   the   majority   were acaricides/insecticides (46%), followed by bactericides/fungicides (27%) and herbicides (20%). The information about patterns on the pesticides use in Mexico is a challenging task which can only be reached through interviews with the users or suppliers, due to the lack of monitoring. García-Hernández et al. (2018) has published an exhaustive review on pesticide- related research in México of the last 20 years of published data, where from 394 analysed scientific articles only 2% are related with agricultural practices encompassing pesticide use patterns. This 2% comes from just ten states in Mexico (Nayarit, Sonora, Sinaloa, Morelos, Yucatán, Guanajuato, Estado de México, Chiapas, and Oaxaca), underscoring the limited research on pesticide use that incorporates local knowledge. These studies, which assessed [SOURCE: Page_62]

40 [SOURCE: Page_62]

the most used pesticides through qualitative methods, provided valuable insights into the actual national pesticide usage. Table 1.7 lists the identified pesticide active ingredients for each evaluated state; however, information on the quantities used is unfortunately unavailable. [SOURCE: Page_63]
