1.2.4 Pesticide monitoring in México. Despite the large number of highly hazardous pesticides authorized in México and the historical evidence of their use, there is not a monitoring program to control the use and application of those pesticides. The agricultural practices are not enough studied and poorly understood in all the country (García Hernández et al., 2018), despite the fact that the assessment of pesticide risks to human health depends on it. (Rosas-Sanchez et al., 2021) highlight the lack of investment in the continuous monitoring of pesticides in Mexico, as well as the structural deficiencies of Mexican legislation, which make the proper use and control of pesticides a challenge. [SOURCE: Page_64]

Mexico has recently updated its regulatory framework for maximum residue limits (MRLs) of pesticides in the environment, with the Mexican Official Standard for Maximum Residue Limits, and the Technical Guidelines and Procedures for Authorization and Review (NOM-082-FITO/SSA$_1$-2017) and is aimed at establishing MRLs for pesticide residues in crops, applying to pesticides that require registration for use in the national territory. Previously, Mexico applied Codex Alimentarius or US EPA MRLs (OECD, 2021). Mexico does not currently have specific guidelines for Maximum Residue Limits (MRLs) related to water or soil matrices, in such studies international agreements are followed as guiding standards. These pesticide analysis in matrices such as sediment, soil and water require sophisticated analytical methods and high precision and resolution equipment, which is a challenge due to the limited financial support in this type of studies (Rosas-Sanchez et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_64]

Pesticide residues studies in México Over the course of decades, the scientific community in Mexico has evaluated pesticide concentrations across different types of matrices in different regions of the country. The first efforts of these type of studies were evaluated by (Albert, 1996) in a review to 15 years of publications (1975 to 1990), which provided an insight into the situation in Mexico concerning persistent pesticides residues in water, sediments, aquatic biota, birds, food, and human tissues in the 19th century. The study uncovered notable residual concentrations of highly hazardous pesticides across various matrices in numerous regions of Mexico. However, the most noteworthy observation made by the author is the absence of discussion on acute (short- term) and chronic (long-term) environmental and health hazards among all the studies, highlighting a deficiency in adopting an interdisciplinary approach. [SOURCE: Page_64]

The pesticides residues on breast milk, serum and urine have been described by Rosas-Sanchez (2021), Albert (1981) and Waliszewski et al., (1996), reported high concentrations of total DDT between 1978 and 1996 respectively. They report concentrations [SOURCE: Page_64]

42 [SOURCE: Page_64]

between a range of 0.266 mg/kg and 6.44 mg/kg in breast milk samples. In Mexico, DDT was used for the eradication of Malaria and was applied in national campaigns in 1951 (Rosas- Sanchez et al., 2021) Currently, DDT is not fully prohibited as it is in most countries; however, its use is restricted exclusively to the health sector. In more recent studies, results showed lower concentrations of the same compound in samples from Guerrero state, with a media of 0.045 mg/kg (Chavez-Almazan and Diaz-Ortiz, 2018). Other old compounds such as Ʃ- Chlorobenzenes, Ʃ-HCH, Ʃ-Chlordanes, Ʃ-drins, endosulfan II, and mirex were also found in breast milk from the south of Mexico in a range of concentrations between 0.277 mg/kg lipid and 0.975 mg/kg lipid (Rodas-Ortíz and Ceja-Moreno, 2008; Rosas-Sanchez et al., 2021). Meanwhile, the presence of organophosphate and pyrethroid pesticide metabolites (3,5,6- trichloro-2-pyridinol and paranitrophenol and 3-phenoxybenzoic acid [3-PBA]) at high concentrations (3.56, 1.63 μg/g creatinine and 1.83 μg/g creatinine, respectively) was detected in more than 70% of urine samples collected from farmers in Sonora, Mexico (Silveira-Gramont et al., 2018). [SOURCE: Page_65]

Pesticide residues in Mexico have been detected in crops, meat, fish, milk, and other edible products. The repercussions of exposure to persistent pesticides also have been detected in breast milk, blood, serum, and urine of farmers, children, teenagers and women living in agricultural areas (Albert, 1981; Waliszewski et al., 1996; Rodas-Ortíz and Ceja- Moreno, 2008; Ramirez-Jimenez and Mejia-Saucedo, 2014; Chavez-Almazan and Diaz-Ortiz, 2018; López-Galvez and Wagoner, 2018). Nonetheless, the environmental and human health effects using real residues levels have continued to be little developed in México (SEMARNAT and INECC, 2022). [SOURCE: Page_65]

1.2.5 Legal and regulatory framework in Mexico Mexico lacks a specific comprehensive national policy on pesticides (OECD, 2021), despite the fact that a century has passed since the introduction of the first pest control law. According to Albert, (2019), synthetic pesticides began to be used in Mexico right after the second world war (Albert, 1996) in 1946. However, the country was being regulated for 34 years with a law written six years earlier, which did not take into account the new pesticides introduced in 1946 and the years that followed. Legislative regulations for agriculture and the environment where just issued in 1984 and 1988, marking a gap of 40 years from the beginning of the use of synthetic pesticides (L.A. Albert, 2019). About the safety of farmers and in agricultural workers, specific standards are still under development. The Mexican Official Standard 003-STPS-1999 which establishes safety and hygiene conditions in agricultural activities (farming activities) was proposed to be reformed in 2016. However, it was not until January 2024 that it was approved, and will enter into force until 2025 (DOF, 2024). [SOURCE: Page_65]

43 [SOURCE: Page_65]

Nonetheless, this Official Standard is primarily aimed at large-scale producers, who are a minority within Mexican agriculture (L.A. Albert, 2019). [SOURCE: Page_66]

The protection of health and the environment (linked to pesticide management) is reflected in several articles of the Mexican Federal Constitution, which is the absolute source of law in the country (OECD, 2021) (Figure 1.21). Article 1 addresses the protection of human rights, Article 4 the protection of human health, Article 6 the right to information, Article 27 the protection of water and soil, and Article 123 the protection of workers (L.A. Albert, 2019). [SOURCE: Page_66]

Federal Constitution [SOURCE: Page_66]

International agreements [SOURCE: Page_66]

Federal and General Laws [SOURCE: Page_66]

Federal regulations and bylaws [SOURCE: Page_66]

Official Standards (NOM) and Voluntary Standards (NMX) [SOURCE: Page_66]

Figure 1.1. Hierarchy of Mexican Laws(OECD, 2021); Modified from OECD, 2021; [SOURCE: Page_66]

Mexican regulation regarding pesticides is based on three main laws: General Health Law (LGS in Spanish), General Law on Ecological Balance and Environmental Protection (LGEEPA in Spanish), and the Federal Plant Health Law (LFSV in Spanish) (L.A. Albert, 2019). Based on these laws, several agencies are involved in the production, distribution, and use of pesticides. Sanitary aspects are regulated by the Ministry of Health (SSA in Spanish) through the Federal Commission for Protection against Sanitary Risks (COFEPRIS in Spanish), while environmental aspects depend on the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (SEMARNAT in Spanish) (Bejarano-González, 2018). [SOURCE: Page_66]

The biological effectiveness of products for agricultural use and the Maximum Residue Limits (for food) is managed by the Ministry of Agricultural and Rural Development (SADER in Spanish known before as SAGARPA), assisted by the National Service for Agri-Food Health, Safety and Quality (SENASICA in Spanish) in the case of pesticides, and the for the Sub- Ministry of Agriculture in the case of plant nutrients (SADER and SENASICA, 2019). [SOURCE: Page_66]

44 [SOURCE: Page_66]

Other laws are also involved with specific tasks, for example the General Law for the Prevention and Integral Management of Waste (LGPGIR in Spanish), which requires that pesticides and their containers be treated as hazardous waste and be subject to a management plan (OECD, 2021), and the Labor Law, which confers authority to the Ministry of Labor and Social Welfare (STPS in Spanish) due to its involvement in pesticide regulation, aiming to promote workplace safety. Furthermore, certain aspects of pesticide management are addressed in legislation regarding to the Law of sustainable rural development, roads, bridges, and vehicular transportation, through the ministry of communications and transportation (SCT in Spanish) (L.A. Albert, 2019; OECD, 2021). [SOURCE: Page_67]

Each of the ministries mentioned above has its own set of technical guidelines or procedures to follow. Once these guidelines are transformed into Mexican Official Standards (NOMs), they become obligatory for the specified audience. These guidelines were adopted since the 1994. There are currently more than twenty NOMs that address pesticides. For example, seven NOMs are related to data evaluation, nine to aspects of registration procedures, and four to labelling and packaging. However, there are no NOMs that regulate the maximum permissible limits of pesticides in soil, sediment, surface water, wastewater or air (Ortíz, Avila-Chávez and Torres, 2014; L.A. Albert, 2019). Moreover, certain NOMs show various deficiencies, including unclearly established coordination among the agencies responsible for their implementation, and in some instances, overlapping areas of jurisdiction with other sectors (L.A. Albert, 2019). [SOURCE: Page_67]

The inconsistency also arises among the general laws for example Mexico's General Health Law establishes that the use of persistent and bioaccumulative pesticides can only be authorized if they are not hazardous to human health and it is not possible to replace them with other less hazardous pesticides. However, Mexico does not have a clear definition and criteria for registration decisions, i.e. it does not refer to or define unacceptable risk in principle. The General Health Law only mentions "acceptable risk" and only in relation to organ transplantation (OECD, 2021). [SOURCE: Page_67]

In the words of the North American Free Trade Agreement (NAFTA) Commission for Environmental Cooperation, “Mexico's regulatory framework for chemicals is one of the most complicated and confusing parts of the country's environmental framework” (OECD, 2021) [SOURCE: Page_67]

Current National strategies to regulate and mitigate pesticides. The current National Development Plan (2020-2024) (DOF, 2019),                   which is the highest-level political declaration in the country during each federal administration, does not specifically address pesticides. Instead, goals and objectives regarding these substances are [SOURCE: Page_67]

45 [SOURCE: Page_67]

included in various political and regulatory Sector Programs (SEGOB, 2020; SEMARNAT, 2020). [SOURCE: Page_68]

The Sector Program for Agriculture and Rural Development 2020-2024 (SEGOB, 2020) has three priority objectives: [SOURCE: Page_68]

1. Achieving food self-sufficiency by increasing production and productivity in agriculture, livestock and aquaculture and fisheries. 2. Contribute to the well-being of the rural population by including historically excluded producers in rural and coastal productive activities, taking advantage of the potential of the territories and local markets. 3. Increasing sustainable production practices in the agriculture, livestock and aquaculture-fisheries sectors in response to agroclimatic risks. [SOURCE: Page_68]

Within the third priority objective states the action plan 3.4.5. mentioning the following: “Promote regulatory standards for the use of pesticides and the coordination of local and territorial actions to protect the survival, biodiversity and abundance of pollinators.” Which is under the priority strategy 3.1: “Enhance sustainable production systems for the conservation, restoration and use of agrobiodiversity.” Being the only on mentioning direct action plans on the use of pesticides. [SOURCE: Page_68]

The Health Sectorial Plan 2020-2024 does not directly address the effects of pesticides in relation to human health. The Environmental and Natural Resources Sectorial Plan 2020- 2024 addresses pesticides as part of an assessment of water contamination but does not specifically address them in concrete action plan. [SOURCE: Page_68]

National pesticide regulatory frameworks are influenced by a multitude of factors, including cultural, political, economic (including trade), health, and food security aspects. The level of development of a country also plays a significant role. Mexico has a sufficient number of legislative guidelines, standards, laws, and temporal strategies in place, but there is a need for harmonized laws that take into account the maximum permissible limits of pesticides in the environment, particularly with respect to Mexican ecosystems and the priority given to the health of all farmers and agriculture workers. Some studies highlight the gaps and offer real and applicable solutions (Ortíz, Avila-Chávez and Torres, 2014; OECD, 2016, 2021; Bejarano- González, 2017; L. A. Albert, 2019; L.A. Albert, 2019), nonetheless, the lack of direct support from the decision-makers to these recommended projects makes all the effort in vain. [SOURCE: Page_68]

46 [SOURCE: Page_68]

Chapter II Study site [SOURCE: Page_69]

This chapter examines the geographic characteristics of the eastern region of Michoacán, with a particular focus on three key zones used in the thesis. These zones include two agricultural watersheds located in the northern and southern parts of the primary polygon of the Monarch Butterfly Biosphere Reserve (MBBR). These watersheds have their recharge zone within the third zone under study: the core zone of the MBBR. The main goal is to provide a comprehensive understanding of the environmental framework, including physiographic features, hydrological patterns, soil composition, climate variations, and land use classifications specific to each zone. [SOURCE: Page_69]

47 [SOURCE: Page_69]

Community in Zitácuaro, Michoacán (Merlo, 2021) [SOURCE: Page_69]

Chapter II: Study Sites [SOURCE: Page_70]

2.1 Eastern Region of Michoacan The state of Michoacán is located in west-central Mexico with a surface area of 59,864 square kilometers, and 213 kilometers of coastline on the Pacific Ocean. Michoacan is divided into 113 municipalities and grouped into several regions according to their similar geographical economic, and cultural characteristics (Gobierno de Michoacán, 2021). The Eastern Region of the state of Michoacan features mountainous landscapes within the Transversal Volcanic System. Most of the area lies at an altitude of approximately 2,020 meters above sea level (m.a.s.l.). However, there are variations within the region, with elevations lower than 380 m.a.s.l., and higher than 2,580 m.a.s.l. (Thayer, 1916). The primary land uses include agriculture, livestock, and forestry. There are rainfed and irrigated agriculture, the latter one is served from several dams and canalization dedicated to irrigation, which are supplied by the hydrographic system that originates in the forest lands at higher altitudes. The Monarch Butterfly Biosphere Reserve (MBBR) is part of the most extensive forest land cover of the Region and its status of Natural Protected Area helps to the recharge of aquifers of regional watersheds. [SOURCE: Page_70]

Three study zones Field data collection took place at sites within three different zones, each one representative of the predominant land cover types in the region. These three zones included two agricultural watersheds, which represents two different predominant agricultural practices, and the core zone of the Monarch Butterfly Biosphere Reserve which represents a non- agricultural zone (Figure 2.1). [SOURCE: Page_70]

The Zitacuaro’s watershed (447 Km²) is located in the southwest of the MBBR. It is an agricultural watershed characterized by its monoculture production and greenhouse farming. The outlet of the watershed is the “El Bosque” dam, which serves as an irrigation reservoir for local communities and as an important element of the Cutzamala water distribution system. The city of Zitácuaro is located within this watershed, it is the most densely populated city in the region (157056 inhabitants [INEGI,2020]), where 98.11% of the total population lives in the rural area, composed of 160 communities. [SOURCE: Page_70]

The Senguio’s watershed (86 Km²), is the second studied watershed, located in the north of the Reserve, named after its most populated municipality, Senguio. With 19883 inhabitants, Senguio is the municipal seat of 61 rural communities, representing 98.36% of the 48 [SOURCE: Page_70]

total municipality population. The main agricultural practice is rainfed agriculture, mainly focused on the cultivation of fodder and maize. Other crops include vegetables such as green husk tomatoes and strawberries. Avocado production is absent from the varieties grown in this watershed. [SOURCE: Page_71]

The core zone of the Monarch Butterfly Biosphere Reserve, has an extension of 13,551 hectares and is covered by sacred fir (Abies religiosa) and fir-pine (Abies-Pinus sp.) forests (Navarrete, Ramírez and Pérez-Salicrup, 2011; Ramírez et al., 2019). Sacred fir forests are the habitat where colonies of monarch butterflies (Danaus plexippus L.) overwinter during November-March in Mexico (UNESCO, 2008). The core zone delimitation comes from the official decree of the MBBR, only activities for research, low impact tourism and environmental education are allowed (DOF, 2020). Core zones are defined as those that contain natural resources considered of major importance, as well as species representative of the region's biodiversity included within one or more ecosystems that have not been significantly altered by humans or that require preservation and restoration (CONANP, 2001). [SOURCE: Page_71]

Figure 2.1. Macro-localization of the three study sites. Elaboration from available layers (INEGI, CONANP) [SOURCE: Page_71]

49 [SOURCE: Page_71]

2.2 Physiography and Geology The eastern region of Michoacan belong to the physiographic province of the Transversal Volcanic Axis, which marks the southern end of the Mexican High Plateau and separates it from the Balsas Depression (Figure 2.2). The Transversal Volcanic System portion of the study site has a discontinuous mountainous system, intensely dissected by strong tectonic processes, mostly composed of a group of mountains and steeped hills, separated by small intermontane valleys and plains. These range of high elevations can be founded within the Reserve comprising the recharge zone for both watersheds, with pronounced slopes above 15 degrees. The highest elevations in the region correspond to Sierra Chincua with altitudes from 3180 to 3600 m.a.s.l. in the north, whereas in the southern, the “Cerro Pelón” mountain (3500 m.a.s.l.) and “El Cacique” mountain (3300 m.a.s.l.) highlight the distinctive topography of the region (CONANP, 2001) (Figure 2.3 [a]). [SOURCE: Page_72]

Figure 2.2. Physiographic regions of Mexico. Source: Board of Regents, The University of Texas, 1975 [SOURCE: Page_72]

In general terms the area of the Reserve exhibits a complex topography resulting from abundant volcanic-basaltic eruptions that spread through fissures, faults, and northeast- southeast-oriented chimneys. These volcanic products overlaid the previous relief, creating a new landscape characterized by numerous volcanic cones, lava flows, volcanic breccia deposits, and ashes. The mountainous terrain that defines the area is composed of Tertiary volcanic rocks overlaying metamorphic sequences and Mesozoic sediments, undergoing [SOURCE: Page_72]

50 [SOURCE: Page_72]

intense fluvial dissection, resulting in steep slopes and a scarcity of alluvial deposits, indicating the area's youthful stage. These conditions confer high permeability, with surface currents being relatively undeveloped despite their abundance (CONANP, 2000). According to the primary relief features, in the region of the protected natural area, exist intermediate extrusive igneous rocks mixed with andesitic fragments, basalts and tuffs, forming massive layers originating from the Tertiary period. This area displays varying degrees of weathering from deep to shallow, along with moderate permeability capacity. In lower altitudes the metamorphic rocks are also present along with some alluvial deposits (Figure 2.4). [SOURCE: Page_73]

( (                                               b) a) [SOURCE: Page_73]

Figure 2.3. Digital Elevation of the three studied zones (a) and characteristic relief features (b) Layer source: Digital Elevation Model from INEGI (2015). [SOURCE: Page_73]

51 [SOURCE: Page_73]

Figure 2.4. Geology map of the Michoacan eastern region. [SOURCE: Page_74]

2.3 Climate and hydrology The Monarch Butterfly Biosphere Reserve, and both watersheds, have in general terms a climate predominantly temperate with high humidity, in accordance with Köppen classification modified by Enriqueta Garcia (2004) for Mexican climatic characteristics (Figure 2.5 (a)). This type of climate presents intense summer rainfalls between an average annual of 700 mm and 1,200 mm (Figure 2.5 (b)) in the months from May to October and less than five percent of winter rains. The higher altitude within the Reserve has a semi-cold climate with average annual temperatures between 5° to 12 °C and, between -3° and 18 °C in the coldest month. Whereas for both watersheds their lower altitude presents warmer temperatures, ranging from 8º to 22º C for temperate semi-wet climate and a temperature media annual above 18 º C for semi-temperate wet (CONANP, 2000). Figure 2.6 shows the historic precipitation (1981-2023) from CHIRPS data base, for Senguio and Zitácuaro watersheds, which represent the climatic variations in each geographic extreme of the MBBR, and figures [SOURCE: Page_74]

52 [SOURCE: Page_74]

2.7 (a) and 2.7 (b) illustrate the annual precipitation variability for Senguio and Zitacuaro watersheds, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_75]

Many watersheds, formed in constructional depressions in the volcanic surface, are scattered over the province (Thayer, 1916). Two big hydrological networks are in the region: the Lerma-Toluca sub-watershed, and the Cutzamala sub-watershed, draining into the Pacific Ocean through Lerma River watershed and Balsas River watershed, respectively. Within these major hydrological systems, localized micro-watershed are delineated by regional topography (Figure 2.8). The mountainous relief that characterizes the study sites is made up of extensively carved by fluvial processes, resulting in limited presence of alluvial deposits, indicating that the region is in a youthful stage of development. These conditions foster high permeability, leading to the minor development of surface currents. [SOURCE: Page_76]

Senguio watershed can be catalogued as a micro-watershed, with runoff flowing from the northwest of the Reserve to supply water to Senguio municipality and surrounding [SOURCE: Page_76]

54 [SOURCE: Page_76]

communities. The Sierra Chincua provides some permanent tributaries flowing to Senguio’s main river, where the river Zapatero is the only one that manages to form small retention bodies, the rest are permanent, but their flow is minimal (CONANP, 2021). [SOURCE: Page_77]

In the other hand, the Zitácuaro watershed part of the Balsas hydrological region and of the Cutzamala sub-basin, has more developed rivers, with permanent tributaries from Cerro pelon, El Cacique and the rest of the Hills within the Central-West Reserve’s mountains. These runoffs play a crucial role in the recharge of El Bosque dam. This dam serves as water source for the irrigation canals of Zitácuaro municipality and the Cutzamala System, which is recognized as one of the world’s most complex hydrological infrastructures, responsible for supplying water for consumption in the Valley of Mexico and Mexico City (Hernández, 1988; Haddad, 1991). [SOURCE: Page_77]

Figure 2.8. Hydrological system of the eastern Michoacan [SOURCE: Page_77]

55 [SOURCE: Page_77]

2.4 Soils The predominant soil type through all the MBBR and Zitácuaro watershed is andisol. In Zitacuaro watershed acrisol soils is the second most prevalent, followed by litosols and vertisols at lower altitudes. Within Senguio's watershed, luvisol and vertisol soil types share equal representation with andisol type (INEGI, 2007) (Figure 2.9). [SOURCE: Page_78]

Figure 2.5. Soil map of the three study zones. Source of layer: INEGI Edaphology shape (2007) [SOURCE: Page_78]

The main characteristics of predominant soil types are described below. [SOURCE: Page_78]

56 [SOURCE: Page_78]

2.4.1 Andosols/Andisols Andosols or andisols are soils formed on volcanic ash and cinders and having andic properties are distributed along the circum-Pacific belts and occur sporadically elsewhere (1.8% of the land mass). The Andisols have mineralogical composition ranging from volcanic glass, short-range-order minerals such as allophane and imogolite, and variable amounts of halloysite. This mineralogical association gives unique properties to such soils, including a high phosphate-fixing capacity, low cation retention, and a high water-holding capacity (Eswaran and Reich, 2005). In the eastern region of Michoacan, Andisols are characterized by its response to intense processes of organic matter decomposition and its composition influenced by the local lithology, which consists of andesites, basalts, rhyolites, and other volcanic rocks. [SOURCE: Page_79]

The bulk density of Andisols is typically low, usually less than 0.9 g/cm^3. This is due to the well-developed particle aggregation and the high amounts of short-range-order and non- crystalline materials they contain (Shoji et al., 1993). The color of Andisols can range from dark due to organic matter to reddish in certain conditions, soils developed from low-silica, high- iron, and -magnesium ash may exhibit a reddish color due to the presence of iron oxide, which masks the black color of organic matter (Quantin, 1986). [SOURCE: Page_79]

2.4.2 Acrisols Acrisols, from Latin acer, very acid, are strongly weathered acid soils with low base saturation at some depth, they have a wide variety of parent materials, especially from the weathering of acid rocks; and notably in strongly weathered clays that are undergoing further degradation (IUSS, 2015). They are found mostly in old land surfaces with hilly or undulating topography, in regions with a wet tropical/monsoonal, subtropical or warm temperate climate. Forest is the natural vegetation type, large areas of Acrisols are under forest, ranging from high, dense rain forest to open woodland. Pedogenetic differentiation of clay content, with a lower content in the topsoil and a higher content in the subsoil; leaching of base cations owing to the humid environment and advanced degree of weathering (Nachtergaele, Velthuizen and Verelst, 2008; IUSS, 2015). [SOURCE: Page_79]

Agroforestry is recommended as a soil-protecting alternative to shifting cultivation to achieve higher yields without requiring expensive inputs. Rotation of annual crops with improved pasture maintains the organic matter content. Adapted cropping systems with complete fertilization and careful management are required if sedentary farming is to be practised on Acrisols. These soils are suitable for production of rainfed and irrigated crops only after liming and full fertilization (IUSS, 2015). [SOURCE: Page_79]

57 [SOURCE: Page_79]

2.4.3 Luvisols The main characteristic of Luvisols is an argic horizon, a subsurface zone with higher clay content than the material above it. This typically arises as clay is washed downward by water and accumulates at greater depth. The clay minerals have not been extensively weathered and are therefore of the high-activity, 2:1 type, giving these soils high cation exchange capacities and high base saturation. In uneroded landscapes, a lighter, clay- depleted eluvial horizon occurs above the argic horizon. Luvisols have favorable physical properties, such as granular or crumb surface soils that are porous and well aerated. They are known for their suitability for a wide range of agricultural uses due to their moderate stage of weathering and high base saturation. Luvisols are found in flat or gently sloping land in cool temperate regions and in warm regions with distinct dry and wet seasons. They have a brown to dark brown surface horizon overlying a (greyish) brown to strong brown or red argic subsurface horizon. In wet environments, the surface soil may become depleted of clay and free iron oxides to the extent that a greyish eluviation horizon forms under a dark but shallow A-horizon (Nachtergaele, Velthuizen and Verelst, 2008) [SOURCE: Page_80]

2.4.4 Vertisols Vertisols are clay-rich soils that exhibit significant shrink and swell behavior as their moisture content changes (Nachtergaele, Velthuizen and Verelst, 2008). They shrink when dry and swell when moistened. Vertisols make up a relatively homogenous order of soils because of the amount and kind of clay that is common to them. The hydraulic conductivity in vertisols is low, that present problems for irrigation in agricultural lands, bypass flow in open cracks is a common situation (Eswaran and Reich, 2005). The bulk density of a Vertisol may vary from approximately 1 to 2 g/cm-3 depending on the moisture content. Bulk density usually tends to increase with depth, due to compression caused by overburden weight (pressure exerted on a soil layer by the weight of the materials overlying it) (Baker, Yarranton and Jensen, 2015; Wang et al., 2022). [SOURCE: Page_80]

2.4.5 Lithosols Lithosols, also known as leptosols, are a type of soil characterized by their shallow depth, typically less than 25 cm, and high content of rocks or gravel. They are formed in areas with steep slopes or in mountainous regions where the drag of weathered material is high, preventing the development of deep, well-defined soil horizons (Nachtergaele, Velthuizen and Verelst, 2008). The name “lithosol” derived from the Greek: lithos, meaning stone, referring to the stony condition at very shallow depth (FAO-UNESCO, 1974). Due to their physical limitations, particularly their shallow depth and high rock content, they have restricted [SOURCE: Page_80]

58 [SOURCE: Page_80]

agricultural use. However, with proper management, they can be used for certain crops, grasslands, and forest plantations. Despite their poor characteristics, lithosols play an important ecological role, supporting various types of plants and animals that can only live in dry and limited environments. They also act as buffers for water flow and groundwater storage (Jaramillo and Murray-Tortarolo, 2019). [SOURCE: Page_81]

2.5 Land use land cover (LULC) Forest represents the most extensive land cover in the MBBR and in Senguio watershed. In both watersheds, the forest land is located inside the limits of the MBBR. The highest altitude zones in the core zone of the MBBR (2400 – 3600 masl) are characterized by sacred fir forest, specially Abies religiosa, which represents the characteristic habitat of Monarch butterfly in Michoacan. Medium altitudes (2400 – 3000 masl) and perturbed zones, present forest composed of fir and pine, with also a mix of pines and oak forest (Pinus- Quercus). Many other species such as Alnus, Arbutus, Salix and Prunus (CONANP,2001), as well as scrubland and rainfed agriculture are presented in some plains and hillsides in the buffer zone of the MBBR. These different type of land classes can be present all together in small areas surrounded by forest, that is called transitional land cover (Figure 2.10). [SOURCE: Page_81]

Agriculture is the second largest land use class of Senguio watershed and the largest in Zitácuaro watershed. This land use involves two big categories: rainfed agriculture and irrigated agriculture. The rainfed agriculture encompasses mainly cereals and grains, also known as traditional crops. Corn is the main traditional crop grown in the region, followed by fodder crops such as oak, peas, cowpea, chickpea, etc. Within Senguio watershed, staple crops continue to dominate the agricultural landscape, alongside expanding plantations of strawberries and husk green tomatoes. Unlike the corn and fodder crops, these plantations depend on irrigation for cultivation. [SOURCE: Page_81]

In the other hand, the Zitácuaro watershed has undergone significant land cover transformations in its agricultural areas, facing a shift from traditional crops to newer varieties. Blackberries cultivated within greenhouses and fruit plantations have become prominent (Špirić et al., 2023). Avocado, peach, and guava plantations stand out as the primary monocultures. Furthermore, greenhouse farming, predominantly situated in the western region of the watershed, focuses on the production of blueberries and native flowers like the Euphorbia pulcherrima. [SOURCE: Page_81]

59 [SOURCE: Page_81]

Figure 2.6. Land use and land cover maps of the MBBR, Senguio and Zitácuaro watersheds. Source of layers: Ramírez et al., 2019 [SOURCE: Page_82]

Land cover of avocado plantations in the east of Michoacan Michoacán serves as the primary hub for Mexican avocado production, harvesting, and exportation to the U.S. Its unique ability to thrive year-round enables continuous production and exportation throughout the year. This distinction is owed to its optimal geographical and meteorological conditions, which include nutrient-rich volcanic soil, ample sunlight, a consistent climate, and well-timed rainfall (Descalsota, 2022).The land use intensification related to avocado plantations is concentrated in western and central Michoacán as was shown in Chapter I. This zone is known as the 'Avocado Strip' or 'Avocado Belt' (Figure 2.11). This area is favoured due to its suitable climatic conditions for avocados and its fertile volcanic soils, which are rich in organic matter (Barsimantov and Navia Antezana, 2012). Over the last three 60 [SOURCE: Page_82]

decades, avocado orchards have been consistently expanded across different land conditions and numerous municipalities within the state, reaching the eastern region of Michoacán. In the east of Michoacan, there are nine municipalities producing avocado, five of them encompass the 94% of the total production: Irimbo, Tuxpan, and Ocampo in the north, Zitácuaro in the center, and Susupuato in the south of the region. Zitácuaro highlighted by a black outline in the map is the leader producer of the region, with an annual production of 13 820 tons in 2023, accounting for 61% of the total production in the eastern region (SIAP, 2024). [SOURCE: Page_83]

Figure 2.7. Municipalities of the Avocado Belt in Michoacan. Elaborated with layers from INEGI (2007). [SOURCE: Page_83]

61 [SOURCE: Page_83]

Chapter III Agricultural Practices in Eastern Michoacán & Human Health Risk Assessment of Used Pesticides [SOURCE: Page_84]

This chapter presents the detailed methodology followed to respond the first objective of this thesis. The results present the descriptive statistics and the analysis based on the interviews carried out in communities of the two studied watersheds. Then, the results of the human health risk assessment are displayed and discussed. [SOURCE: Page_84]

62 [SOURCE: Page_84]

Application of pesticides on avocado trees. (Medina, 2023)                   Fertilisation of a maize field (Merlo,2021) [SOURCE: Page_84]

Chapter III: Agricultural practices in eastern Michoacán and human health risk assessment of used pesticides [SOURCE: Page_85]

3.1 Introduction Identifying the specific agricultural practices carried out by farmers in high-priority region serves as a fundamental first step in developing strategies for sustainable agriculture. Agricultural practice analysis involves comprehensively cataloguing and understanding the diverse methods used in crop cultivation and land management (Xu et al., 2022). The assessment provides a clearer picture of the techniques, systems, and methods used by farmers and agricultural stakeholders. [SOURCE: Page_85]

In this chapter, the term 'agricultural practices' refers to all activities that take place before, during, and after the harvest of a crop (Tilman et al., 2002). The individual practices vary according to the crop, the experience or acquired habits of the farmer, and regional practices. Xu et al. (2022) describe differences among each process during maize production and highlight the benefits of adopting Good Agricultural Practices (GAPs) in three general phases: pre-harvest, harvest, and post-harvest. GAPs include several principles and norms to achieve economic viability, environmental and social stability, and food safety and quality (Globalgap.org, 2022) which emphasize the importance of farmers being well informed about the correct management of their chemical inputs. [SOURCE: Page_85]

The term "Good Agricultural Practices" is more closely aligned with the principles of sustainable agriculture, which contrasts with conventional agricultural practices and is distinct from "organic agriculture", although their share a common focus on sustainability (The Soil Association, 2021). Organic farming is a method that avoids the use of synthetic fertilisers, pesticides or genetically modified organisms (GMOs) and is primarily concerned with the production side of farming, the products used to grow crops (The Soil Association, 2021). Sustainable agriculture, on the other hand, aims to meet the food and fibre needs of the present without compromising the ability of future generations to meet their needs (Tilman et al., 2001). While organic farming focuses strictly on using methods that benefit the environment without the use of synthetic pesticides, chemical fertilizers and GMOs, sustainable farming not only rejects artificial methods but also emphasises farming practices that are economically viable and socially supportive. Therefore, while organic farming is considered the cleanest method of growing crops, vegetables, fruits and other plants, sustainable farming is not necessarily organic and takes into account economic and social factors as well as environmental ones (Tilman et al., 2002). [SOURCE: Page_85]

63 [SOURCE: Page_85]

On the other hand, conventional agriculture is differentiated along multiple technical, social and economic dimensions resulting in a great diversity of practices (Sumberg and Giller, 2022). Although most authors define it as a dichotomy of organic agriculture, making a direct association with high input of agrochemicals. [SOURCE: Page_86]

Pesticides play an important role in conventional agricultural development, there is a heavy dependence on pesticide applications to meet the increasing demand for crop production. Farmers are directly exposed to pesticides via their hands and by inhaling chemicals during their preparation and application in the field. Dermal, oral, and respiratory routes are the main common pathways by which pesticides enter the human body. Farmers are high risk groups directly exposed to pesticides. Equipment and techniques used for pesticide application (spray), protective equipment for the farmers (mask, gloves) as well as disposal of pesticide cans are important parameters to limit the exposition of the farmers. [SOURCE: Page_86]

This work does not support this generalized association of convectional agriculture. This work make agreement with the discussion by Shennan et al., 2017), which is referred by Sumberg and Giller (2022) in an analytical review of terms. Placing the term convectional agriculture tin a more complex description, where no relative superiority to other agricultural practices, and it is necessary to explore the agricultural practices at a local level to avoid generalizations. [SOURCE: Page_86]

This chapter aims to achieve the first objective of the thesis. The goal is to provide a comprehensive understanding of agricultural practices in eastern Michoacán, with a specific focus on avocado production and maize cultivation, and more specifically on activities associated with the use of pesticides. The justification for this focus lies in the need to assess the health risks posed by pesticide management to those who handle these chemicals, including farmers, field workers, and people living near agricultural fields. Despite the importance of this issue, as described in the first chapter, Mexico lacks programs for monitoring agricultural practices, and its pesticides legislation is riddled with contradictions and confusion, making it challenging for decision-makers to navigate. Additionally, efforts to address pesticide safety have not adequately included vulnerable populations, such as farmers and rural residents. While initiatives such as the 2019 Manual for the proper use and handling of pesticides in the field exist (SADER and SENASICA, 2019), they may not effectively reach isolated rural communities or small-scale agricultural production areas. The importance? of this research is emphasized by the fact that official records of pesticide poisoning cases may not accurately represent the reality on the ground, as demonstrated by perspectives from other agricultural zones (Guzmán-Plazola et al., 2016). Therefore, by understanding specific chemicals and practices used by farmers in Eastern Michoacán, this thesis's first objective [SOURCE: Page_86]

64 [SOURCE: Page_86]

aims to fill a crucial knowledge gap and contribute to efforts to mitigate the human health risks associated with pesticide use. [SOURCE: Page_87]

3.2 Methods [SOURCE: Page_87]

3.2.1 Data collection Survey strategy. [SOURCE: Page_87]

To collect information on agricultural practices in the region, a qualitative approach was used to carry out interviews addressed to farmers from communities in both watersheds. The selection of the communities/ejidos to be interviewed was based on four criteria; (1) that the predominant agricultural production does not derive from protected agriculture, (2) that the fields of crops/plantations were located near to a water body, (3) that the local authorities of each community/ejido agreed to collaborate in the project, and (4) that the communities/ejidos were located close to the MBBR’s influence zone. The criteria are detailed In the Table 3.1. [SOURCE: Page_87]

Table 3.1. Criteria for the selection of the communities/ejidos interviewed. [SOURCE: Page_87]

Criteria      Justification [SOURCE: Page_87]

1    Protected agriculture refers to all crops grown under controlled environmental conditions, usually in a greenhouse. Although the construction of greenhouses is increasing in the region, it is not yet the predominant form of agriculture, nor is it the most appropriate for the further processing of all the variables in this study. Our analysis considers the natural conditions of the watersheds, i.e., the climatic variables (precipitation, temperature, radiation, etc.) that we collect from weather stations, therefore, the inclusion of greenhouse crops that do not follow the natural dynamics of such variables would compromise the reliability of the results. [SOURCE: Page_87]

2    The transformation of pesticides used is dependent on time, sunlight, and degrading microorganisms, therefore, it is recommended to consider crop land/plantations near accessible water bodies for sampling, to detect concentrations close to those discharged, thus, the surface or groundwater sampling sites will be directly related to the pesticides used in the surrounding plots. [SOURCE: Page_87]

3    The population of each ejido and community has its own local authority and must be informed of all external activities that take place within its territory. It is [SOURCE: Page_87]

65 [SOURCE: Page_87]

mandatory to get to know well the persons in charge of the community and to obtain their written consent before starting any interviews and/or water and soil sampling. [SOURCE: Page_88]

4   According to Article 3, Section XIV of the regulations of the General de Equilibrio Ecológico y Protección al Ambiente (LGEEPA) in the regulations for natural protected areas, the zone of influence is defined as "Areas bordering the polygonal zone of a protected natural area that maintain a close social, economic and ecological interaction with it". To delimit the zone of influence, the limits of the Senguio and San Juan Zitacuaro River watersheds were considered, prioritizing the communities and ejidos bordering the MBBR polygon [SOURCE: Page_88]

The farmers interviewed were randomly selected based on, but not limited to, the crop they produce. In the communities of Zitácuaro our target group was avocado producers and in the communities of Senguio it was corn growers. [SOURCE: Page_88]

The first contact with the farmers was always done by a local non-governmental organization (NGO). The NGO helped us to contact the local authority of each community, to organize an appointment and to identify a local resident to drive us to the area. Once we had met the local authority, we presented the project objectives to them. After obtaining their consent, we interviewed them onsite and asked them to identify some other respondents from their community, following the “snowball” sampling technique. The snow sampling method works better when there are already some preview interviews, but it is necessary to collect more information (Newing, 2011). [SOURCE: Page_88]

The first contact with the farmers was always done by a local non-governmental organization (NGO). The NGO helped us to contact the local authority of each community, to organize an appointment and to identify a local resident to drive us to the area. Once we had met the local authority, we presented the project objectives to them. After obtaining their consent, we interviewed them onsite and asked them to identify some other respondents from their community, following the “snowball” sampling technique. [SOURCE: Page_88]

3.2.2 Qualitative data treatment The interviews were based on a semi-structured questionnaire elaborated according to the methodology proposed by Kallio et al. (2016). An important aspect of this type of survey is its effectiveness in fostering reciprocity between the interviewer and participant (Galletta, 2012). This method allows the interviewer to spontaneously generate follow-up questions [SOURCE: Page_88]

66 [SOURCE: Page_88]

based on participants' responses and provides room for the individual verbal expressions of the participants (Kallio et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_89]

For the analysis of the interviews, the responses were grouped into three sections: [SOURCE: Page_89]

a) socio-demographic characteristics and information about the crops grown: •   Personal information: Name, age, gender, origin, land tenure status, activities besides agriculture, ownership of lands. •   Crop and land information: type of crops, area of the land, time growing these crops, experience growing new crops, reason of change (if it is the case), irrigation system, knowledge of water irrigation source. b) information about the treatment and pesticides used: •   Treatment: Type and cause of treatment (weed, fertilization, elimination of plagues), frequency of application and quantity. •   Knowledge about the types of pesticides used: products used, knowledge about products names/brands and distributor store, physical characteristics of substances (powder or liquid), time using pesticides. c) handling pesticides practices and general perspectives •   Handling practices: Mode of application, technical knowledge, period/season of application, use of personal protective equipment, final disposal of empty containers. •   General perspectives: Inversion, compliance with results, knowledge of adverse effects (human and environmental health), interest of alternative products. [SOURCE: Page_89]

The entire questionnaire is included in Appendix A. [SOURCE: Page_89]

3.2.3 Human health risk assessment (HRA) Based on the agrochemicals collected in the interviews and data indexed in databases on the pesticides toxicological and eco-toxicological properties (USEPA, 1996; Samuel et al., 2012; WHO-IARC, 2015; PPDB, 2022) the health hazard index was calculated using the pesticides risk indicator of QUEBEC-IRPeQ developed by the Quebec National institute of public health, the Quebec ministry for Sustainable development, the Environment and the parks and the Quebec Ministry for Agriculture, Fisheries and Food (MAPAQ) (Son et al., 2018; Chouinard, Pelletier and Vincent, 2021; Koffi, Kouadio and Son, 2022). [SOURCE: Page_89]

IRPeQ calculates a human health risk index (HRI) (Equation 3.1) which considers all the toxicological properties of the active ingredients of each pesticide (acute toxicity, chronic toxicity modulated by a factor related with persistence and bio-accumulation) and certain [SOURCE: Page_89]

67 [SOURCE: Page_89]

properties of the commercial products (type of formulation) (Ahouangninou et al., 2012; Samuel et al., 2012). [SOURCE: Page_90]

𝑇𝑅𝐼 ×𝑊𝐹𝑓 ×𝐶𝐹𝑑 𝐻𝑅𝐼𝑖 =                                     Eq. (3.1) 10 [SOURCE: Page_90]

The HRIi of each active ingredient, is calculated by multiplying the Toxicological Risk Index (TRI), the weight factor of pesticide formulation (WFf) (Table 3.2), and the compensation factor to account for the concentration of the active ingredient in the end-use product and the applied dose (CFd). All is divided by 10 to obtain an HRI of an acceptable order of magnitude. Theoretically, the HRI for an active ingredient could have a value in the range of 1.25 to 17,306. [SOURCE: Page_90]

Formulations, liquid or solid, that are designed to be released as a gas (GAS) will receive a score of 2. [SOURCE: Page_90]

The first step to calculate the health risk index is to calculate the Toxicological Risk Index of each active ingredient (TRIi) (Equation 3.2). [SOURCE: Page_90]

𝑇𝑅𝐼𝑖 = [∑ 𝐴𝑐𝑢𝑡𝑒 𝑟𝑖𝑠𝑘𝑠 + (∑ 𝐶ℎ𝑟𝑜𝑛𝑖𝑐 𝑟𝑖𝑠𝑘𝑠 × 𝐹𝑃𝑒𝑟)]²                                      Eq. (3.2) [SOURCE: Page_90]

Which is determined by the summation of points assigned according to the different acute toxicity criteria (Table 3.3), plus the summation of points assigned according to chronic toxicity criteria of active ingredients (Table 3.4), and afterward multiplied by a factor linked to the persistence and the bioaccumulation potential in humans (Table 3.5). 3.3 Results and Discussion [SOURCE: Page_92]

3.3.1 Socio-demographic characteristics Fifty-five volunteer farmers were interviewed. Forty-eight farmers are from four indigenous communities in the municipality of Zitácuaro and seven farmers from three ejidos in the municipality of Senguio. The minority of responses were from women, three women in Zitácuaro and one woman in Senguio participated in the surveys (Figure 3.1). Each of them managed their respective lands for different reasons. Two of them inherited their lands from their fathers, while the other two took the lead of the property for supporting their husbands who had migrated to the United States of Amer [SOURCE: Page_92]

Women         Men 7% [SOURCE: Page_92]

93% [SOURCE: Page_92]

Figure 3. 1. Distribution of gender among the interviewed farmers (n=55). [SOURCE: Page_92]

Most of the participants are in their thirties, with a significant proportion in the 45 to 65 age group. The younger respondents, aged between 25 and 35, inherited their farms from their [SOURCE: Page_92]

70 [SOURCE: Page_92]

fathers     and     chose     to   cultivate     avocados          due   to     its   profitability     (Figure   3.2). [SOURCE: Page_93]

10 [SOURCE: Page_93]

6 5 [SOURCE: Page_93]

Figure 3. 2. Age distribution of farmers interviewed. [SOURCE: Page_93]

3.3.2 Crops grown and irrigation practices [SOURCE: Page_93]

The farmers interviewed from the communities of Zitácuaro comprised one dedicated gladiolus flower cultivator, one exclusive blackberry grower, two corn growers, and 44 avocado growers. Of the avocado producers, 48% were exclusively focused on avocado cultivation, while the remaining 52% also engaged in the cultivation of other crops for sale. These additional crops included corn (36%) and blackberries (9%) among others (Figure 3.3). [SOURCE: Page_93]

Avocado and Corn 36% Blackberry 9% [SOURCE: Page_93]

Limes 2% [SOURCE: Page_93]

Other 19% Peach 2% [SOURCE: Page_93]

Gladiolus flower                  Broad beans Only Avocado                                                   2%                              4% 45% [SOURCE: Page_93]

Figure 3. 3. Crops grown by interviewed farmers in the Zitácuaro watershed. [SOURCE: Page_93]

All the avocado producers interviewed stated that they had previously grown corn crop for sale, before planting or buying their avocado orchards. They decided to change because [SOURCE: Page_93]

71 [SOURCE: Page_93]

of its profitability. Furthermore, they all continued to cultivate corn for personal consumption in their own backyard plots. Corn cultivation is a rainfed crop in the region, which follows a traditional cultivation cycle depending on the rainy season. On the other hand, the avocado orchards require an additional source of water during the dry season. Based on the responses concerning to the irrigation system employed by avocado producers, it was revealed that the predominant method implies the extraction of river water (Table 3.6). A smaller group of respondents (11%) have dug wells in their orchards due to the scarcity of river water or their facility to reach the aquifer on their land (Figure 3.4). [SOURCE: Page_94]

In the municipality of Senguio’s, all the farmers were involved in corn and beans cultivation, following the rainy season for irrigation. However, in response to the alteration in the timing of seasonal rainfall, they have been using canalized water sourced from rivers. In the ejido of Senguio two field workers who were involved in strawberry and green tomato cultivation were also interviewed in the field. They stated that canalization of water as the main irrigation system which those crops used. [SOURCE: Page_94]

72 [SOURCE: Page_94]

3.3.3 Farm size All the interviewed avocado producers are classified as small-scale producers, with farms ranging from 0.25 to 3 hectares. Of these, 44% had plots between 0.25 and one hectare, while 56% mentioned owning orchards between 1 and 3.5 hectares. At the time of the interviews, the age of the avocado orchards ranged between 3 and 17 years old, with an average age of 10 years old. In contrast, interviewed landowners in Senguio possess larger farms, ranging from 3 to 5 hectares. Regarding the time owning or working the land, all the farmers indicated that they were working from a young age assisting their fathers to after inheriting the land and continuing working on it, either by switching to avocado or continuing with traditional crops. [SOURCE: Page_95]

3.3.4 Land treatment and pesticide usage Of all the farmers interviewed, only one farmer had not used chemicals for 7 years in his 12-year-old avocado orchard, the rest of the farmers interviewed had always used some chemicals. The farmer with the “organic orchard” mentioned that the change was not easy, production was lower, and the use of natural pesticides is also expensive. Seven years ago, he gradually switched to applying mostly organic products, and some synthetic pesticides when very resistant pests endangered his entire orchard. Despite this, he is unsure about maintaining a strictly organic orchard, as his target market is national, and the demand for organic avocados is still low. [SOURCE: Page_95]

Farmers who practice conventional agriculture apply different types of pesticides primarily to prevent and tackle well-known pests in the region. The most commonly reported pest for avocado and corn growers was the red spider mite (Tetranychus urticae). For avocado plantations, the responses were associated with two mains pests: thrips (Thysanoptera), and the branch borer (Copturus aguacatae Kissinger). Corn growers also mentioned the whitefly (Aleyrodidae) and the beetle larva (Phyllophaga spp.) as important pests in their corn fields. To control these pests only 1.6% of the farmers have received technical training on which pesticides to use and how to apply them. The transmission of experience and knowledge as well as the advices from the pesticide seller are the two most common responses regarding the acquisiotion of knowledge on pesticide application. [SOURCE: Page_95]

Most of the farmers we interviewed could not remember the names of the pesticides they had used. In such cases, they kindly agreed to schedule a follow-up meeting where we could visit their pesticide storage areas or obtain purchase receipts to identify the active substances of the pesticides in question. [SOURCE: Page_95]

73 [SOURCE: Page_95]

The results of the second section of the survey also indicate that all the farmers apply a fertilizer at least twice a year. They apply mainly major nutrients such as nitrogen, phosphorus, and potassium. Nonetheless, only 7% of farmers have employed analytical services to sample the soil on their lands in order to know the real requirements of the soil. The fertilizers that farmers use can be the same every year, or something different recommended by the store seller. [SOURCE: Page_96]

3.3.5 Types and toxicity of pesticides used by farmers Based on the interviews, the pesticides used by farmers in the region included 28 active ingredients, some of which are grouped in a sole brand formulation (Table 3.7). These pesticides included insecticides (56 %), fungicides (33%) and herbicides (11%) from the neonicotinoid/pyrethroid, organophosphate, and benzimidazole groups, respectively. Among these, some are internationally banned but allowed in Mexico, such as benomyl and paraquat, and 80 % of are classified as highly hazardous pesticides (HHPs) according to the 2021 Pesticide Action Network (PAN)’s international list of HHPs (PAN, 2021). [SOURCE: Page_96]

Figure 3.5 shows the pesticides reported by farmers, along with the target crops they mentioned, the insecticide bifenthrin was reported exclusively for corn cultivation, and not by avocado producers. In general, the pesticides most frequently reported by the farmers are glyphosate (29%), imidacloprid (27%), and benomyl (24%). The herbicides paraquat (18%) and 2,4-D (11%), the insecticide permethrin (13%), and the compound copper sulphate (11%) were also often mentioned. Copper sulphate is labelled as an ‘organic fungicide and bactericide’; due to its mineral compounds, it is considered a pesticide of concern in the US and Europe. Glyphosate the best-known pesticide among all corn growers; it was referred to by its different brand names as the herbicide that they applied most over time. Categorized as [SOURCE: Page_97]

of relatively low acute toxicity to humans, glyphosate formulations can contain additional components that may influence its overall toxicity. In March 2015, it was classified as “probably carcinogenic by humans” Group 2A) by the International Agency for Research on Cancer (IARC, 2015a). Moreover, glyphosate has been linked to adverse effects on soil macro- fauna and pollinators (Abraham et al., 2018; El jaouhari et al., 2023). These findings highlight concerns regarding its impact on terrestrial ecosystems and crucial pollinator populations, contributing to ongoing discussions about its environmental safety and regulation. [SOURCE: Page_98]

Figure 3.5. Active ingredients of pesticides and their targeted crop based on interview responses. [SOURCE: Page_98]

Imidacloprid, the world’s most extensively used neonicotinoid insecticide, is also the second most frequently reported by farmers. In Mexico, it holds an important number of authorizations (60) and is applied to combat pests in various crops, including strawberries, green tomatoes, corn, and avocado orchards. This insecticide is considered to have low toxicity causing only minor symptoms such as vomiting, abdominal pain, headache, and diarrhea (Mohamed et al., 2009). Studies on chronic exposure to imidacloprid are limited, but some have reported health effects, such as changes in the nervous system and reproductive system (Cimino et al., 2017). [SOURCE: Page_98]

76 [SOURCE: Page_98]

Benomyl, the most used fungicide among the interviewed farmers, is a highly toxic pesticide banned in more than 30 countries. It is classified as possible human carcinogen (Group C) (US EPA, 2018). Studies on the human toxicology of benomyl have shown its toxicity at very low concentrations (Hidetaka et al., 2004; Kara et al., 2020; MohammadSadeghi, Adeli and Bameri, 2024). Along with its metabolite carbendazim, benomyl has been catalogued as an endocrine disruptor chemical, which stimulates the cytochrome P450 aromatase enzyme, responsible for maintaining the homeostatic balance between androgens and estrogens in both sexes. This enzyme is expressed by many human tissues, including the ovary, testis, placenta, brain, liver, adipose, muscle, and breast tissue (Hidetaka et al., 2004). [SOURCE: Page_99]

Permethrin, a pyrethroid insecticide, shares minor short-term effects with imidacloprid. Both chemicals are also highly toxic to bees and other beneficial insects (Easton and Goulson, 2013; Green et al., 2023). Permethrin has been associated with liver enlargement and nerve damage, with impacts on the immune system observed in animal studies (Sun et al., 2022). While its carcinogenic potential remains a subject of debate, showing no mutagenic effects in some assessments, it acts as a mutagen in human cell cultures. Consequently, the EPA has classified permethrin as a "likely carcinogen" (PAN, 2021), highlighting the complexity of its health impact profile. [SOURCE: Page_99]

Paraquat and 2,4-Dichlorophenoxyacetic acid (2,4-D) are two widely used herbicides that share some adverse short-term effects such as skin and eye irritation, nausea, and vomiting. Paraquat exposure, even to a small amount, can cause immediate and severe harmful effects, and there is no antidote available for paraquat poisoning. If a person survives the toxic effects of paraquat poisoning, long-term lung damage (scarring) is highly likely, and other long-term effects may also occur, including kidney failure, heart failure, and esophageal strictures. Large ingestions of paraquat are unlikely to be survivable (Gawarammana and Buckley, 2011; Tajai and Kornjirakasemsan, 2023). The chronic effects of 2,4-D have been linked to several serious health problems in animals, including kidney and liver damage, reproductive issues, neurotoxicity, increased risk of lymphoma, and certain types of cancer. Based on a comprehensive review of pertinent scientific data, the IARC Working Group classified 2,4-D as “possibly carcinogenic to humans” (Group 2B) (Loomis et al., 2015). [SOURCE: Page_99]

3.3.6 Frequency of pesticide usage Based on all the responses from the farmers interviewed, the frequency of pesticide application is influenced by several factors, including whether the crop/plant was recently planted, the age of the tree (for orchards), the pests found in the field, and the economic status of the farmer. In the case of avocados, if the plant has been recently planted, it is common to 77 [SOURCE: Page_99]

apply fertilisers and insecticides approximately every two weeks. As the tree matures, more than 85% of the farmers surveyed switch to a monthly schedule for fertiliser and insecticide application. This regularity is mainly aimed at preventing the well-known 'red spider mite' and 'avocado thrip' pests, which include the red mite (Olygoncychus spp.) and a thysanopter (Heliothrips spp.) frequently found in avocado orchards (Aguilera-Montañez and Salazar- Garcia, 1991; Ramírez Dávila et al., 2013). Others reported using insecticides every two or three months, depending on their budget or whether they detected a pest. On the other hand, farmers who grow rainfed crops, such as maize, beans, or cereals for livestock feed, reported using fertilisers and pesticides twice a year and just before and during the rainy season. [SOURCE: Page_100]

To the best of our knowledge, research on the frequency of pesticide applications is quite limited, with studies mainly focusing on the avocado strip located in western and central Michoacan. In this area, pesticides are also sprayed regularly, ranging from four to twelve applications per year, as a preventive control measure (Anaya and Burgos, 2015). The Hass variety of avocado is a plant that can have up to four flowering flushes, allowing for year-round avocado production in mature trees (Rocha-Arroyo et al., 2011). This characteristic requires continuous management practices throughout the year, resulting in significant energy consumption, the use of fossil fuel inputs, machinery, N-fertilisers, huge pesticide inputs, etc., with unavoidable ecological impacts (Astier et al., 2014; Denvir, 2023). [SOURCE: Page_100]

The dose applied varies significantly between pesticides and crops. The farmers mentioned 'measurements' such as one spoonful or one capful (using the cap of the bottle containing the product being measured) mixed in 15 L of water (typically the volume of backpack sprayers) for young and small avocado plants. These doses were determined using the fungicide benomyl and branded formulations that combine chlorpyrifos and permethrin. Paraquat and 2,4-D, the two most used herbicides after glyphosate, were applied mostly in corn plantations, with a ratio of half of or one complete bottle (which varies between 900 mL and 1 L) for 15 or 20 L of water, directly to weedy areas. On the other hand, for mature avocado trees, stronger dilutions can be used, such as awhole bottle of pesticide mixed with a volume of water varying from 200 L to 1000 L per hectare, depending on the recommendations of the farmer's supplier. Nonetheless, when comparing the available pesticide reference data sheets of identified pesticides, the dosage applied by the farmers interviewed is in most cases at the same level as that recommended by the manufacturer. According to the interviews, the only pesticides for which the recommended doses are doubled are chlorpyrifos, permethrin, and lambda-cyhalothrin. [SOURCE: Page_100]

78 [SOURCE: Page_100]

3.3.7 Handling of pesticides All farmers interviewed were questioned about their use of safety equipment when mixing and spraying pesticides, as well as their practices for disposing of empty pesticide containers. Most farmers (84%) responded that they did not use any safety equipment other than long-sleeved shirts. The rest of the farmers (16%) responded that they sometimes used a nose mask or a scarf to avoid direct inhalation and a hat or cap (Figure 3.6). This low use of protective equipment could be linked to the little to no education and training concerning good agricultural practices that the farmers have received. This lack of knowledge, the lack of alternative pest control techniques, and the influence of retailers are factors that increase the risk of exposure for pesticide applicators; on the contrary, higher levels of education and training correspond to lower exposure to pesticides (Lebailly et al., 2009; Jallow et al., 2017). [SOURCE: Page_101]

Figure 3. 5. Field-worker applying pest treatment without using disposal mask and cap as personal protective equipment in an avocado orchard. Photo taken by the avocado orchard owner: private share. [SOURCE: Page_101]

Among the responses, another identified handling practice is the predominant use of two pesticide application techniques: the manual technique and the motorised technique. The use of stretcher power sprayers in mature avocado orchards is driven by the need to spread the pesticides over the entire tree canopy and to apply larger volumes of mixed solutions (Figure 8). Some farmers indicated that it is common to rent these sprayers due to their high 79 [SOURCE: Page_101]

cost. A manual backpack sprayer with a capacity ranging from 15 L to 30 L is the second technique employed; it is mainly used for treating small or young trees, as well as for the trunks and lower branches of older trees. The use of the backpack sprayer is the most common method for applying pesticides in corn fields. In contrast, when it comes to applying base powder or granulated fertilisers, it is often mentioned that the application is based on the amount that can be held in a closed fist. [SOURCE: Page_102]

The frequency and dosage of pesticide application and the handling of pesticides must strictly adhere to technical guidelines in order to mitigate health risks and prevent environmental contamination. This encompasses the use of personal protective equipment and the careful consideration of optimal weather conditions for pesticide application. Factors such as the wind direction, high temperatures, and the possibility of rain can significantly lower the risk of dermal and respiratory exposure (Son et al., 2018). [SOURCE: Page_102]

Figure 3. 6. Field-worker using a stretcher power sprayer in a mature avocado: Photo taken by the avocado orchard owner: private share. [SOURCE: Page_102]

Regarding the disposal, a larger proportion of farmers (76%) dispose of pesticide containers by throwing them directly into the domestic trash, while 18% of the farmers surveyed store them somewhere in their field or in their houses (Figure 3.8). A smaller percentage of farmers (6%) return them to the store. No recycling collection system is currently in place, and [SOURCE: Page_102]

80 [SOURCE: Page_102]

most of the interviewed farmers had never received information or instructions regarding the proper disposal of pesticide containers. [SOURCE: Page_103]

Figure 3. 7. Photo taken while the farmer was showing us where he stores the empty pesticide bottles, using sacks and mixing pesticides bottles with other beverage plastic bottles. Note: the bottles were taken out of the bags before the photo was taken. [SOURCE: Page_103]

3.3.8 Human health risk assessment The calculation of the health risk index (HRI) revealed that Lambda Cyhalothrin (λ- Cyhalothrin), Chlorpyrifos and Benomyl are the three active substances that showed the highest risk to human health (Table 3.8). λ-Cyhalothrin is a pyrethroid insecticide that even it was mentioned in a small frequency (7%) by the interviewed users, is a substance with high acute toxic effects (Sudan et al., 2022), requiring a maximum personal protection equipment (Jepson et al., 2020). It is classified as possibly carcinogenic to humans, according to the Health Canada's Pest Management Regulatory Agency (ARLA, 2017). Chlorpyrifos is an organophosphate insecticide that is currently not associated with carcinogenic effects, but is associated with several other problems such as nervous system effects, respiratory and gastrointestinal symptoms, musculoskeletal, cardiovascular renal and ocular effects, as well as reproductive and developmental effects (Nandi et al., 2022). Benomyl, a benzimidazole fungicide used by almost a quarter (24%) of our interviewed farmers, is associated to oxidative stress and apoptosis in neural cells (Kara et al., 2020), and is linked to problems of mutations [SOURCE: Page_103]

81 [SOURCE: Page_103]

and reproduction according to the EU GHS (PAN, 2021).                                    Benomyl rapidly degrades to carbendazim (MBC) which is also of toxicological concern, both considered as Endocrine Disruptor Chemicals (EDCs) (Hidetaka et al., 2004). Effects associated with both benomyl, and MBC include liver toxicity, developmental toxicity (such as fetal eye and brain malformations and increased mortality), and reproductive (testicular) effects. Both benomyl and MBC are also considered possible human carcinogens (EPA, 2002). [SOURCE: Page_104]

Table 3.7 Value of the assigned points of acute and chronic toxicity and the results of the Toxicological Risk Index (TRI) and of the Health Risk Indicator (HRI) calculated for the active ingredients identified in the interviews. [SOURCE: Page_104]

Acute and Chronic human health effects Table 3.8 also shows the distribution of acute and chronic toxicity within the HRI for every active ingredient employed. The summed sub-indicators of acute toxicity indicate that paraquat and thiram are the most toxic among all the active ingredients. Conversely, when evaluating the chronic toxicity, mancozeb is recognised as the most toxic, and it is followed by pyrethroids such as lambda-cyhalothrin, cypermethrin, and permethrin. Mancozeb is a fungicide that poses various health risks. Its primary mechanism of toxicity is through skin contact, leading to contact dermatitis and dermal sensitization. Additionally, it has [SOURCE: Page_104]

demonstrated teratogenic and reproductive effects, impacting reproductive and endocrine structures and leading to reduced fertility (Thiruchelvam, 2005). The breakdown of mancozeb can yield subproducts with elevated toxicity, and its main subproduct, ethylenethiourea (ETU), has been linked to various health effects, including potential carcinogenicity, reproductive and developmental impacts, and thyroid disruption (Abdourahime et al., 2020). Thiram, a pesticide widely used as a fungicidal seed is considered to be moderately toxic via the inhalation route of exposure (Toxicity Category II). Thiram is a neurotoxicant and can also act as a developmental toxicant. The major toxic effects of thiram in humans are thyroid dysfunction and liver toxicity (Talcott, 2012). [SOURCE: Page_105]

Analysing these specific findings gives farmers a clearer understanding of the associated toxicity for each ingredient, thus facilitating their ability to make informed product choices based on the particular risk that they aim to mitigate. [SOURCE: Page_105]

Conclusion Understanding common agricultural practices facilities, the identification of areas where improvements can be made to enhance sustainability. It allows the integration of more environmentally friendly methods, the adoption of efficient water management techniques, the correct pesticide handling techniques, the promotion of soil conservation practices, among others. In addition, this knowledge provides a basis for engaging with local communities and stakeholders, encouraging collaboration and co-creating links to future sustainable agricultural strategies within these watersheds. [SOURCE: Page_105]

The interviews revealed differences and similarities in agricultural practices within both watersheds. In terms of differences, these are mainly driven by the types of crops cultivated. The Senguio watershed, encompassed annual-cycling crops, such as maize, fodder crops, strawberries, and husk tomatoes. On the contrary, Zitácuaro, perennial plantations are most commonly found, alongside guava, peach, and apple orchards, avocado plantations are the most popular and productive. This watershed revealed a high frequency of pesticide application. Farmers maintain monthly treatments to prevent infestations in their avocado plantations, which are predominantly monocultures in the watershed. While in Senguio watershed, annual-cycling crops implies the application of pesticides only during the production period. [SOURCE: Page_105]

The demand for irrigation is also different from both types of crops: in avocado plantations is necessary a constant irrigation before yielding, typically around 7 years. To achieve that producers commonly irrigate using surface water and groundwater, often [SOURCE: Page_105]

83 [SOURCE: Page_105]

extracted from rivers, and dug wells, supplemented by rainfall in wet season. For maize, in both watersheds, rainfall is the main source of irrigation. The agricultural practices with similarities between both watersheds rely mainly on the lack of personal protective equipment usage. Farmers in both watersheds are not aware of the importance of its use. It is evident that they have inadequate training regarding the proper application of pesticides, and they lack information on the health risks posed by the use of highly toxic chemicals. Along with the proper pesticide handling, including storage, application timing, and disposal practices. [SOURCE: Page_106]

The mentioned types of used pesticides covered 28 active ingredients, with glyphosate, imidacloprid, and benomyl being among the most commonly used in both watersheds. The calculation of the human health risk assessment shows the highest values to the insecticides lambda cyhalothrin and chlorpyrifos along with the fungicide benomyl. These active ingredients presented the highest toxicity and based on evidence of adverse effects on human health, requiring priority attention in their use and handling. The insecticide imidacloprid posed the lowest toxicity on humans, despite its high toxicity in pollinators. [SOURCE: Page_106]

To reduce the impact of pesticides on human health and the environment, it is necessary to emphasise the implementation of integrated pest managements strategies, with focus on prevention, monitoring, and control of pests using environmentally sensitive techniques. Safe pesticides handling practices involve storing them correctly, strictly following label instructions, and using Personal Protective Equipment (PPE). Understanding the right techniques of application, considering weather and soil conditions, and the adoption of proper disposal practices are all critical in reducing pesticides environmental impacts. Education, training, and compliance with regulations play a key role in ensuring their safe use for the environment and humans. [SOURCE: Page_106]

84 [SOURCE: Page_106]

Chapter IV Pesticides in Eastern Michoacán: Occurrence and Environmental Risk Assessment Chapter IV describes the water sampling strategy followed. Then, the detailed overview of the methods used by the pesticide analysis and the validation analytical method are presented. To finalize the methodological part with the explanation of the performed environmental risk assessments. The results show and discuss the spatio-temporal occurrence of pesticides and the associated risks posed to aquatic organisms. [SOURCE: Page_107]

Chapter IV: Pesticides in eastern Michoacan: occurrence and environmental risk assessment. [SOURCE: Page_108]
