### 4.1. Introduction

The presence of pesticides in water sources has significant adverse effects on the entire ecosystem. Surface runoff and leaching of pesticides from agricultural fields into water bodies can result in chemical concentrations that exceed safety limits for aquatic plants and invertebrates, with an impact on the health of these organisms (Leong et al., 2020). In addition, as mentioned in Chapter 1, bioaccumulation and biomagnification can increase adverse effects at many levels of the ecosystem, disrupting food chains and affecting water quality. [SOURCE: Page_108]

To explain the contamination of pesticides in water sources and its impact on ecosystem, concentrations are typically quantified using methods based on High Performance Liquid Chromatography with tandem Mass Spectrometry (UHPLC-MS/MS) (Hamilton and Sewell, 1982), and used as variables to evaluate the risk assessment on the environment using the potential-no-effect-concentrations and the effect and kill media concentrations for useful bioindicators aquatic organisms (EC, 2003; Lewis et al., 2016; Chidya et al., 2022). [SOURCE: Page_108]

For this work, the potential aquatic ecological risks of the pesticides detected in water samples were assessed in terms of both acute exposures based on toxic units (TUs) and chronic exposures based on risk quotients (RQs) (Ccanccapa, Masiá, Navarro-Ortega, et al., 2016; Finizio, Grenni, et al., 2022). The risk assessments considered the level of exposure based on the environmental concentrations obtained from the pesticide analysis and the toxicity endpoints of non-targeted organisms of three taxonomies (algae, crustacean, and fish) that represent three trophic levels in the aquatic ecosystem. These three trophic levels represent different stages of the food chain, and considering all three can elucidate how pesticides bioaccumulate and magnify throughout the ecosystem (Belanger et al., 2021). The individual risk calculations were summed for the pesticide mixture detected at each sampled site. [SOURCE: Page_108]

4.2. Material and Methods 4.2.1 Sampling sites selection Sampling sites for both watersheds were first selected based on hydrographic and geographic criteria. Following the hydrological network, sites were located on the main river, [SOURCE: Page_108]

86 [SOURCE: Page_108]

considering their accessibility and their proximity to agricultural field, such as avocado orchards in the Zitácuaro watershed and maize fields in the Senguio watershed. Sites in the Monarch Biosphere Reserve were strategically selected based on the water sources used by the permanent ecosystem and the Monarch butterfly colonies. Exploratory fieldwork was carried out to verify the selected water sampling sites. Due to the lack of a continuous natural stream as it can be seen in Figure 4.1, the choice of our sampling sites had to be changed. This was due to two main reasons: the diversion of water to agricultural canals, and the discharge of waste waters into the river, making it a different matrix to be evaluated with a different pretreatment procedure than the one planned for this work. In addition, some of the selected sampling sites were inaccessible either because they were surrounded by private property, or because they involved entering communities that were in social conflict at the time. On the other hand, in the Senguio watershed, the exploratory fieldwork made us aware of the severe drought in which the watershed is immersed, which was the main reason for selecting only key sites. [SOURCE: Page_109]

Figure 4. 1. Photos taken during the fieldwork in the main Zitácuaro river. Source: personal file [SOURCE: Page_109]

Water collection [SOURCE: Page_109]

Surface water sampling was carried out at ten sites in the Zitácuaro watershed, at two sites in Senguio and at four sites in the core zone of the MBBR. Sites in the Zitácuaro watershed included streams, dug and natural wells passing through indigenous communities, and one site in the watershed outlet, just before the El Bosque dam. The Senguio sites are located into the main river, including one in the ejido Senguio and the second at the outlet of the watershed. The MBBR sites were in surface runoff zones within the reserve. Specifically, two sites were located in the northern part of the reserve, in Chincua ejido, while another two sites were sampled in the southern Sanctuary of the MBBR, located in El Rosario ejido. [SOURCE: Page_109]

87 [SOURCE: Page_109]

Table 4.1 gives the description of the main hydrogeographic characteristics of each site, and the Figure 4.2 show photos of some site points. For the MBBR sites, a federal permit was obtained from the Natural Protected Areas National Commission (CONANP in Spanish) for each sampling campaign, allowing us to collect surface water within the protected area. Also, for the rest of our sampling sites, as mentioned in chapter 3, a prior consensus was given by local authorities in each community and ejido sampled. Figure 4.3 displays the map with all the verified sites and the final water sampling points. The campaigns were carried out at two contrasted seasons: during the rainy season in July 2021 and during the dry season in February 2022. Water samples and field blanks were collected in 500 ml pre-washed polypropylene bottles, then placed in a ziplog bag and transported to the laboratory in cool boxes with ice bags to maintain a low temperature. Field blanks were collected and stored in a manner as close to surface water samples as possible to account for potential contamination during the sampling and analysis. [SOURCE: Page_110]

89 [SOURCE: Page_111]

Figure 4. 3. Map of the three study zones, water sampled sites and water sampling points from the field verification. [SOURCE: Page_112]

4.2.2 Pesticide Analysis The pesticide analysis covered 21 active ingredients, selected prior to the initial fieldwork using a validated analytical method designed for non-polar pesticides. This selection was based on the laboratory's existing pesticide list and a literature review of the most commonly used pesticides in avocado and maize cultivation. Following the interviews, additional pesticides were incorporated into the analytical method where feasible. [SOURCE: Page_112]

4.2.2.1 Chemicals and reagents The certified reference native standards and internal standards (> 98% purity) were purchased from Sigma-Aldrich (USA), Fisher Scientific (Germany), AccuStandard (USA), Cayman Chemicals (USA), DR. Ehrenstorfer (Germany) and ITW Reagents (USA). High purity solvents (LC-MS grade) methanol and acetonitrile were from Biosolve (France). Acetic and formic acid (LC-MS grade) were from LiChropur (Germany) and ammonium formate (LC-MS grade) from Biosolve (France). Ultra-pure water was obtained from Veolia ELGA Purelab chorus system. Whatman Filters (GF/F) were from Sigma-Aldrich (China) and OASIS HLB cartridges (6 cc, 200 mg, 30 μm) from Waters (Ireland). [SOURCE: Page_112]

90 [SOURCE: Page_112]

4.2.2.2 Sample preparation After collection, all water samples were cooled down to room temperature and filtered through Whatman glass microfiber filters (GF/F, pore size = 0.7µm, diameter = 47mm). All samples including procedural blanks, field blanks and duplicate samples were spiked with 20 μL of a mixture of internal standards (1,000 μg L−1). The samples were enriched using Solid-Phase Extraction (SPE) and OASIS HLB cartridges (6 cc, 200 mg, 30 μm, Waters). The Oasis HLB cartridges were first conditioned with 6 mL methanol followed by 6 mL ultra-pure water and then all the volume of filtered samples were loaded on the cartridges using 50mL syringes barrels. The cartridges were dried under vacuum, packaged in aluminium paper, and stored at -20°C, then transported to France in isothermal boxes. [SOURCE: Page_113]

4.2.2.3 Sample extraction The analytes were eluted with 6 mL methanol. The eluates were collected in pre cleaned polypropylene tubes and then gently concentrated down at 32°C under a gentle stream of nitrogen to approximately 150 µL. The extracts were vortex and reconstituted by adding 350 µL of pure water and then vortexed for 30 sec more. The samples were then transferred into HPLC vials for LC-MS/MS analysis using disposable glass Pasteur pipettes. Figure 4.4 displays an illustrated example. In Mexico [SOURCE: Page_113]

Packing to store [SOURCE: Page_113]

Filtration             Spiking               SPE procedure         Drying [SOURCE: Page_113]

Elution [SOURCE: Page_113]

Transfer to vials Evaporation                                 In France LC-MS/MS Analysis Figure 4. 4. Steps followed for the LC-MS/MS analysis. Photos taken during the validation method and during the sample’s extraction. Red lines expressed the steps followed in Mexico and blue lines show the steps followed in France. [SOURCE: Page_113]

91 [SOURCE: Page_113]

4.2.2.4 LC-MS/MS conditions The     pesticides    of   interest    were   analyzed     using       ultra   performance   liquid chromatography (UHPLC, Exion) coupled to a tandem mass spectrometer (QTrap 5500 Sciex). The separation of targeted compounds was achieved on a Kinetic® Biphenyl column (100 x 2.1 mm i.d., 2.6 µm, Phenomenex®). The column was heated to 40°C and with a stable flow rate of 0.6 mL/min. The mobile phases consisted of phase A: water (with 5 mmol/L ammonium formate) and phase B: methanol (with 5 mmol/L ammonium formate). A gradient ramped as follows: 0-1 min: 10% B; 1-2 min: increase to 50% B; 2–10 min: increase to 100% B and held for 3 min; at 13.1 min decreased to 10% B and kept for a 3 min equilibration before the next injection. The total run time was 16 min. The injection volume was set as 5 μL. An electrospray ionization source was used with a polarity switching to ionize all the analytes and combined with scheduled multiple reaction monitoring (sMRM). Transitions, polarity, and other MS parameters of the target analytes have been optimized and are shown in Table 4.2 The ion source parameters were optimized to the following final conditions: ion spray voltage (IS): +4500 V and - 4500 V; temperature (TEM) 550°C; Curtain gas (CUR) 35 L.min-1, and ion source gas (GS$_1$ and GS$_2$) at 70 psi. High purity nitrogen was used as the curtain and collision gas. Identification and confirmation of each analyte were achieved using retention time and comparing the ratios of MRM transition intensity between the samples and the standards within the same batch of analysis. [SOURCE: Page_114]

4.2.2.5 Quantification and quality control Quantification was conducted using mass-labeled standards. Calibration curves covered 8 levels of concentration: 0, 0.25, 0.5, 1, 5, 10, 25, 50 and 100 μg L−1. Calibration standards were injected up to three times in each batch of sample analysis. Quality control standards were added to the batch and injected after every 10 samples to check for instrumental variations. Field blanks, procedural blanks, and instrumental blanks were included in each batch of samples to assess potential contaminations of targeted compounds during the sampling, the samples preparation or during the analysis, respectively When field blanks presented values higher than the method limit of quantification (MLOQ), these values were subtracted to the corresponding samples. The data set obtained was processed using Sciex OS (Sciex Now) software. The limits of detection (LOD) and quantification (LOQ) were estimated using the lowest concentration of an analyte with a signal to noise (S/N) ratio equal or superior to 3 and 10, respectively. The displayed results encompassed only the quantified compounds. [SOURCE: Page_115]

4.2.2.6 Method validation A validation study was performed in terms of accuracy (recovery), linearity, matrix effect, limit of quantification, as well as intra-day and inter-day precision of the method. The recovery and standard deviation of the extraction method were estimated based on the average of 5 replicates of water samples at a spike level of 0.3 µg/L (close to LOQ) and 20 µg /L. The absolutes recoveries were determined by comparing the obtained concentrations between the spiked-extracted sample and the blank matrix samples spiked at the same concentration after the extraction. Six duplicate samples were analysed in different batches to assess the repeatability of this method, and the RSDs were below 30%. Linearity was evaluated in calibration made in solvent at eight concentration levels. The regressions (R$_2$) of the calibration curves were all ≥0.990. method limits of quantification (MLOQs) were determined as the analyte concentration of a low matrix standard or the lowest concentration that produced a peak signal ten times the background noise (s/n) from the chromatograms. Intra-day and inter-day precision were determined from five analyses of samples spiked at four concentration levels: low concentrations level (0.1 ng/mL and 1 ng/mL), medium concentration [SOURCE: Page_115]

93 [SOURCE: Page_115]

4.2.3 Environmental Risk Assessment The potential ecological risks of the pesticides detected in surface water were assessed for acute exposures based on the calculation of toxic units (TU) and chronic exposures based on the calculation of the risk quotients (RQ) (Ccanccapa, Masiá, Andreu, et al., 2016; Finizio, Di Guardo, et al., 2022). Both risk assessments considered pesticide concentrations in surface water obtained during our monitoring, and the toxicity endpoints of nontargeted organisms of three taxonomic groups (algae, crustacean, and fish). The three organisms are selected to take into account three different trophic levels in the aquatic ecosystem. The risk assessment was done first considering the substances individually. To assess the cumulative risk arising from the co- exposure to all measured pesticides, the individual risk quotient was summed for the pesticide’s mixture detected in each sampled site. [SOURCE: Page_118]

To determine the Toxic Units (TUs) and Risk Quotient (RQ) for each detected pesticide, we followed the European guidelines (CD:93/67/EEC) (EC, 2003). The parameters necessary for the environmental risk assessment were gathered from the ECOTOX Knowledgebase (Olker et al., 2022; USEPA, 2022) and the Pesticide Properties Database (PPDB) (Lewis et al., 2016; PPDB, 2022). [SOURCE: Page_118]

4.2.3.1 Calculation of Toxic Units [SOURCE: Page_119]

For the TU, acute toxicity values were used, included the concentration of test substance diluted in water that is calculated to affect (EC50) or to kill (LC50) 50 percent of a test population during continuous exposure over a specified period of time (CFR, 2018). The parameters were chosen only for freshwater species: (i) for the invertebrate water flea Daphnia magna (48-hour EC50 to effect of immobilization), (ii) for algae Scenedesmus subspicatus or Chlamydomonas reinhardtii (72-hour EC50 to effect growth inhibition) and (iii) for fish from species Tilapia Sp., Cyprinus carpio (96-hour LC50 survival effect). [SOURCE: Page_119]

The equation (4.1) is used to determinate the cumulative effect in organism through toxic units (Rasmussen et al., 2015; Ccanccapa, Masia, et al., 2016; Rodríguez-Rodríguez et al., 2021; Finizio, Grenni, et al., 2022), for the group of detected pesticides in each sampled site. [SOURCE: Page_119]

𝑀𝐸𝐶𝑖 ∑ 𝑇𝑈𝑗 = ∑𝑛𝑖,𝑗=1                (Eq. 4.1) 𝐴𝐶𝑗 [SOURCE: Page_119]

MECi is the measured environmental concentration detected in water samples for each pesticide (i), and ACj the acute endpoint (LC50 or EC50) of each trophic level (j). The presence of risk was determined according to the European safety thresholds for pesticide concentrations in stream water: TU = 0.1 for algae, and TU = 0.01 for fish and invertebrates (D. magna) (Rasmussen et al., 2015). [SOURCE: Page_119]

4.2.3.2 Calculation of Risk Quotients For the RQs, the chronic toxicity values represented by the no-observed effect concentration (NOEC) were used for the same species mentioned above. This value represents the highest concentration of a substance used in the toxicological test that does not have an adverse effect on organisms. For these values the periods of NOEC chose for each organism were 21-day (algae), 72h (Daphnia m.), and 21-day (fish), respectively (Ccanccapa, Masia, et al., 2016; Iturburu et al., 2019; El-Nahhal and El-Nahhal, 2021). Values are shown in Table 4.4. [SOURCE: Page_119]

The risk quotient method (Equation 4.2), is based on the PNEC of each pesticide (i) (Equation 4.3), resulting from the lowest concentration value among the NOECs divided by an assessment factor (AF). Where NOECs for all three taxa were absent, the lowest value of acute toxicities was employed (Papadakis et al., 2015). The AF reflects the degree of uncertainty when [SOURCE: Page_119]

97 [SOURCE: Page_119]

there are limited number of species, to the broader context of the natural environment, the sparser the available toxicity data, the higher the uncertainty/assessment factor will be (Cesnaitis, Versonnen and Sobanska, 2014). Where acute values were used a value of 1000 is given as AF. In case of two and three employed NOECs, the factors is taken as 50 and 10 respectively (EC, 2003; Brovini et al., 2021; Finizio, Grenni, et al., 2022). Then the RQ of each pesticide detected were summed up for site. [SOURCE: Page_120]

The low risk, medium risk, and high risk are represented by 0.01 ≤ RQ < 0.1, 0.1 ≤ RQ < 1, and RQ ≥ 1, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_120]

𝑀𝐸𝐶                                                𝑁𝑂𝐸𝐶 Ʃ𝑅𝑄𝑖 = 𝑃𝑁𝐸𝐶𝑖            (Eq. 4.2)            𝑃𝑁𝐸𝐶𝑖 = 𝐴𝑠𝑠𝑒𝑠𝑠𝑚𝑒𝑛𝑡 𝑖𝐹𝑎𝑐𝑡𝑜𝑟    (Eq. 4.3) 𝑖 [SOURCE: Page_120]

4.3. Results and Discussion 4.3.1 Spatial and temporal occurrence of pesticide residues Of the 21 pesticides and transformation products (TPs) targeted by our analytical method, 13 were quantified at least once in the water samples collected during each season. The quantified compounds included four herbicides (atrazine, diuron, metolachlor, and 2,4-D), two insecticides (fipronil and imidacloprid), one insect repellent (N,N-diethyl-meta-toluamide [DEET]), and one metabolite of pyrethroid insecticides (3-phenoxybenzoic acid [3-PBA]). In addition, four fungicides (azoxystrobin, metalaxyl, propiconazole, and thiabendazole), and one fungicide metabolite (carbendazim), were quantified (Figure 4.5). At sites SE$_1$ and ZIT$_7$, both located at the watershed outlet, all 13 quantified pesticides were detected. On the other hand, the same pesticides except for 2,4-D were quantified in the groundwater samples compared to the surface water samples. Specifically, diuron was not detected, and 2,4-D was not quantifiable in the dry season. [SOURCE: Page_120]

98 [SOURCE: Page_120]

Figure 4. 5. (a) Total concentrations of targeted pesticides and metabolites (ng/L) at each sampling site in the rainy (‘site’R) and dry (‘site’D) seasons. (b) Zoomed-in version of (a) to show values at concentrations <250 (ng/L). [SOURCE: Page_121]

The highest water concentrations were observed for the insecticide imidacloprid (1139 ng/L) and the fungicide carbendazim (806 ng/L) in water samples from the Senguio's and Zitacuaro's watersheds, respectively. However, these higher concentrations occur at sites draining agricultural plots on steeper slopes upstream (ZIT$_4$ and SE$_1$). Imidacloprid, a neonicotinoid, is known to be highly toxic to bees and other pollinators, so its presence in water sources could pose a critical threat for biodiversity (Hooven et al., 2006). In the case of carbendazim residues, it is also catalogued as a high toxic chemical, for humans, animals, and aquatic organisms. It can cause embryonic, reproductive, developmental, endocrine and haematological toxicities to some model organisms of zebrafish, rats and mice, threating the biosafety of terrestrial and aquatic ecosystems (Zhou et al., 2023). The detection of these pesticides can be attributed to their moderate persistence in the environment and frequent field application. For instance, carbendazim can be readily adsorbed by organic matter or degraded by microorganisms in water and sediments. However, in the absence of these conditions, its half- life, which ranges from 1 to 4 months, can significantly contribute to its frequent presence (Paszko and Skrzypek, 2010; Zhou et al., 2023). According to the interview results in chapter III, these pesticides are among the five most commonly used pesticides in maize crops and avocado plantations. Several agricultural practices, including application methods (manual or motorized) and application rates, as well as the weather conditions during pesticide application, contribute to the migration of these pesticides beyond the intended application areas (Gish et al., 2011; Boithias et al., 2014). Table 4.5 displays the specific pesticide concentrations (ng L-1) measured in surface water at each sampling site and for both seasons. [SOURCE: Page_121]

Atrazine is considered to be highly persistent in the environment due to its resistance to abiotic hydrolysis (Chowdhury et al., 2021). It can remain in water and soil for decades, even after it has been banned in some countries (Jablonowski, Schäffer and Burauel, 2011). Therefore, detecting these two herbicides can be related to a present or an ancient application. The adverse effects of residual concentrations of diuron and some of its degradation products have been widely documented, including morphological, biochemical, physiological, and behavioral alterations in non-target organisms (Vinggaard et al., 2000; Mhadhbi and Beiras, 2012; Sánchez- Muros et al., 2013) [SOURCE: Page_122]

During the dry season, three compounds (3-PBA, DEET, and propiconazole) were detected within the MBBR study area (MM sites), where synthetic pesticides are not allowed. The concentrations exhibited variability across all sites for 3-PBA and DEET, while propiconazole remained consistently within the range from 0.15–0.21 ng/L. The repellent DEET was the sole compound present also during the rainy season, with a concentration of 27.5 ng/L in the MM$_2$ site. From the core zone sites, the metabolite of pyrethroid insecticides (3-PBA) displayed the [SOURCE: Page_122]

highest concentration values of all the compounds, ranging from 27 ng/L to 68 ng/L at sites within Chincua's property and increasing to values between 75.7 ng/L and 150 ng/L within the property El Rosario (Figure 4.6). These high values even exceed those obtained from samples taken in the agricultural watersheds, suggesting that the input may be near the sampling sites. A seasonal pattern could be identified for the MM sites, as the sites sampled during the rainy season did not have detectable concentrations. This distinction could be associated with the variation in the timing of pesticide applications and the dilution process resulting from surface runoff due to intense rainfall events that are characteristic in this zone. These concentrations indicate the pesticide residues to which aquatic and terrestrial insects, including bees and butterflies, are directly exposed to. [SOURCE: Page_123]

Figure 4. 6. Spatial and seasonal variations in the total concentrations of pesticides and transformation products quantified (ng/L) at each sampling site. Note: For MBBR sites, only three pesticides and TPs (3-PBA, DEET, and propiconazole) were detected; thus, box plots were not generated. Instead, individual concentration values are presented. The concentrations are represented on a logarithmic scale, and outliers are displayed as filled circles. [SOURCE: Page_123]

Pyrethroids are one of the most widely used pesticides and are present in numerous formulations used in agriculture for pest control in crops, forestry, horticulture, and gardening. The main commercially available pyrethroids include allethrin, bifenthrin, lambda cyhalothrin, cypermethrin, deltamethrin, permethrin, tetramethrin, etc. (Saillenfait, Ndiaye and Sabaté, 2015). From most pyrethroids, the metabolite 3-PBA is one of the major transformation products. The concentrations of 3-PBA in the core zone of the MBBR indicate the potential use of most pyrethroid insecticides. Consequently, the presence of this metabolite at high altitudes within a NPA, where there are no agricultural lands, indicate that the application of these pesticides may come from a forestry treatment, in close proximity to the water sampling sites of the MBBR. Recent studies conducted in the same area have documented outbreaks of forest pests [SOURCE: Page_123]

101 [SOURCE: Page_123]

associated with recent droughts (Gómez-Pineda et al., 2022). These outbreaks have led to the issuance of governmental sanitary permits for phytosanitary treatment to control bark beetle infestations in the core zone of the reserve (Gómez-Pineda et al., 2023). The presence of the detected metabolite raises the possibility that the phytosanitary treatment of outbreaks may involve the unauthorised use of pesticides within the MBBR. Figure 4.7 shows the sanitary logging authorisations (pink dots) and the sampling sites for this study within the core zone of the MBBR. [SOURCE: Page_124]

Figure 4. 7. Sites of sanitary logging authorizations and water sampling points of the Monarch Butterfly Biosphere Reserve. Source of logging authorisations: General Direction of Forest and Soil Management, Ministry of the Environment and Natural Resources (SEMARNAT by its Spanish acronym) through Gomez-Pineda et al., 2022. [SOURCE: Page_124]

4.3.2 Frequency of detection Table 4.6 shows the mean and maximum concentrations (ng/L) of the 13 pesticides detected above the LoQ values at each sampling site, grouped based on the three study areas. In the case of the Senguio's watershed, the two unique samples are displayed as minimum and maximum concentrations. Particularly in the rainy season, most of the detected pesticides, apart from diuron, propiconazole, and metolachlor compounds, are commonly present in more than 50% of the sites. In the dry season, diuron, 2,4-D, and fipronil are the three pesticides with the lowest percentage of detection. Carbendazim and imidacloprid show an apparent correlation between the frequency of detection and the highest concentration values during the rainy season, Figure 4.8 represents the global frequency related to the sum of concentrations for each pesticide. [SOURCE: Page_124]

4.3.2 Environmental risk assessment 4.3.2.1 Acute ecotoxicity risk The cumulative TUs for acute toxicity did not reach the range from 0.1 to 1, indicating that the aquatic organisms are not exposed to moderate (TU>0.1) or high levels (TU>1) of short-term toxicity at the pesticide concentrations measured in the water samples. The sum of TUs obtained for water were <0.01 in all sites (Table 4.6). In the Zitácuaro watershed, the highest total TU values, referred to as Zitmax, were observed for the invertebrate Daphnia during both seasons. These values suggest a low acute risk, but they also highlight the sensitivity to the combination of pesticide residues at these locations of Daphnia, as the representative organism for the zooplankton-category invertebrates. This sensitivity has been found in other studies, with an even bigger list of pesticides (Ccanccapa, Masiá, Navarro-Ortega, et al., 2016; Carazo-Rojas et al., 2018; Herrero-Hernandez et al., 2020). Although our results show a very low risk assessment for aquatic organisms at actual pesticide concentrations, bioaccumulation could pose a higher risk to the whole ecosystem and even to terrestrial species such as pollinators (Main et al., 2020). [SOURCE: Page_126]

The individual TU results of each pesticide in every group of sites are displayed in Table B$_1$ for the dry season and Table B$_2$ for the rainy season within Appendix B. [SOURCE: Page_126]

Table 4. 7. Accumulative pesticide TUs for the mean concentration and maximum concentration of Zitacuaro’s watershed and the minimum and maximum values at the MBBR and Senguio sites. [SOURCE: Page_126]

[SOURCE: Page_126]

4.3.2.2 Chronic ecotoxicity risk The four main pesticides contributing at least 1% (concentration >0.001 ng/L) to the ƩRQ are carbendazim, atrazine, diuron, and the metabolite 3-PBA. In the rainy season, high chronic risks were posed by carbendazim and diuron at three sites of Zitacuaro's watershed (ZIT$_4$, ZIT$_5$, ZIT$_7$) and the site at the outlet of Senguio's watershed (SE$_1$) (Figure 4.9). During the dry season, RQs higher than one were observed at sites ZIT$_5$, ZIT$_7$, and SE$_1$ due to the presence of diuron, carbendazim, and the 3-PBA compounds. The metabolite of pyrethroids was present at some 104 [SOURCE: Page_126]

sampled sites from the MBBR (MM$_2$, MMC$_1$, and MMC$_2$) at levels that also pose a high environmental risk (RQ > 1) (Figure 4.10). Pesticides and their degradation products often coexist in the same system and have the capacity to interact and influence toxicity responses. Research has shown that the degradation products of permethrin, such as 3-PBA, can exhibit a higher toxicity than the original pesticide itself (Stratton and Corke, 1982). The two herbicides shown in these results (atrazine and diuron) are known to be persistent molecules (DT50 = 75 and 146 days, respectively (PPDB, 2022)) , that is, their chemical structure makes them less prone to microbial attack, which means that they can remain in water and soil for a long time (Curran, 1999). The individual values of the risk quotients of each pesticide in every sampled site are displayed in Table B$_3$ for rainy season and Table B$_4$ for dry season within the Appendix B. [SOURCE: Page_127]

Figure 4. 9. Sum of pesticide RQs for the rainy season. The risk values are plotted on a logarithmic scale. [SOURCE: Page_127]

105 [SOURCE: Page_127]

Figure 4.10. Sum of pesticide RQs for the dry season [SOURCE: Page_128]

Conclusion The detection of 13 pesticides in the waters of eastern Michoacán, particularly in agricultural watersheds, confirms the current contamination of several water bodies. The detected pesticides include herbicides, insecticides, insect repellents, fungicides, and some specific metabolites. The observed seasonal variations in pesticide detection suggest complex interactions between pesticide application practices, weather conditions, and hydrological processes. The higher concentrations and most frequent detections were for the insecticide imidacloprid and the fungicide carbendazim, two pesticides widely used in both watersheds. The neonicotinoid imidacloprid did not raise concerns about aquatic organisms, however, it is well- known about the high risks on pollinators. On the other hand, the fungicide carbendazim is a highly toxic compound aquatic organisms, as well as for humans as was discussed in chapter III. [SOURCE: Page_128]

Inappropriate use and regulation of pesticides has an impact beyond the boundaries of agricultural fields. The presence of pyrethroid metabolites at high altitudes within the core zone of the Monarch Butterfly Biosphere Reserve where agricultural activities are restricted, raises concerns about unauthorized pesticide use, possibly for forestry treatments. This finding points out the indirect consequences of poor management in phytosanitary treatment practices. This problem also arises indirectly from climate change, intensified by severe droughts in the region. This further complicates environmental management and emphasizes the necessity for more efficient mitigation strategies in forestry. 106 [SOURCE: Page_128]

Ecotoxicological assessments indicated low acute risks to aquatic organisms at the measured pesticide concentrations, as cumulative Toxic Units (TUs) remained below thresholds of concern. However, the risk assessment indicating long-term risks were identified. Particularly during the rainy season, cumulative concentrations of carbendazim, atrazine, diuron, and 3-PBA pose very high environmental risks. Water sampling sites located at the outlet in both watersheds along with site ZIT-5 located downstream of agricultural lands, presented ecotoxicological high risk during both seasons. Meanwhile, other sites displayed marked seasonal patterns: MBBR sites showed high ecotoxicological risks from 3-PBA exclusively during the dry season, whereas sites in the Zitácuaro watershed exhibited moderate to high risks only during the rainy season. [SOURCE: Page_129]

This emphasizes the importance of continuous monitoring and targeted management strategies to mitigate long-term risks to aquatic ecosystems. Additionally, it highlights the need for further research into the specific mechanisms driving pesticide transport and accumulation in water bodies during different seasons, enabling more effective mitigation measures to be implemented. [SOURCE: Page_129]

107 [SOURCE: Page_129]

108 [SOURCE: Page_130]

Chapter V [SOURCE: Page_131]

Soil Characteristics & Aquifer Pesticide Contamination Vulnerability The first part of the chapter presents the results derived from the physical, chemical, and hydraulic analysis of soil samples collected in both watersheds. Then, these results coupled with other features, display the mobility of selected pesticides. The chapter continue with an assessment of the aquifer vulnerability to pesticide pollution in a broader scale, using specific hydrogeographic characteristics of each watershed. [SOURCE: Page_131]

109 [SOURCE: Page_131]

Ploughed soil (Merlo, 2021) [SOURCE: Page_131]

Chapter V: Soil characteristics and aquifer pesticide contamination vulnerability 5.1 Introduction [SOURCE: Page_132]

Groundwater contamination vulnerability Groundwater, which accounts for approximately 30.1% of the Earth's replenishable water, is a vital resource, with only 0.86% being fresh water and only 0.022% being readily accessible (Gupta, 2014). It serves as a comparatively safe and reliable source, especially when compared to surface water. Despite its inherent resilience to pollution, once contaminated, restoring the integrity of groundwater is a daunting, costly, and time-consuming. In some cases, restoration efforts may prove impossible. Factors such as growing populations, human activities (including agriculture, industry, and waste disposal), changes in topography, and land use patterns have combined to degrade and overexploit this invaluable resource. The increasing demand for water, coupled with the declining availability of surface water, further intensifies the stress on groundwater reserves (Hiscock, 2005; Gupta, 2014). [SOURCE: Page_132]

The growing scarcity of groundwater is global FAO (2022) reports compelling evidence to suggest that numerous aquifers are being exploited at unsustainable rates across the world. Additionally, several studies have evidenced the indiscriminate use of fertilizers and pesticides being the biggest human-made cause of pollution to underground reservoirs (FAO, 2022). Approximately 70 percent of global groundwater withdrawals are used to grow food and industrial crops and raise livestock, while about 30 percent of all irrigated water worldwide comes from below the ground, which is why the agricultural sector must play a critical role in addressing the issue (FAO, 2022). [SOURCE: Page_132]

In this context, the intrinsic aquifer vulnerability models are essential, as they provide information on the vulnerability of groundwater to pollution over large areas in a short time and at minimal cost (Asfaw and Mengistu, 2020). Vulnerability to contamination of an aquifer or groundwater is the intrinsic characteristics that determine its susceptibility to being adversely affected by a contaminant load that produces chemical, physical, or biological changes that exceed drinking water regulatory limits (Foster, 1987). [SOURCE: Page_132]

Systematic methods that allow a relative assessment of off-site impacts of pesticides are of great value, they are useful tools assisting the several steps in decision making and policy 110 [SOURCE: Page_132]

formulation (Kookana, Correll and Miller, 2005). PIRI (Pesticide Impact Rating Index), is a software package based to rate different pesticides in terms of their pollution potential at the farm scale, it is based on pesticide use, site and environmental conditions (Pollock, Kookana and Correll, 2005) (Csiro, 2014). This chapter used the soil characteristics coupled with pesticide information derived from interviews to assess the mobility of six pesticides, selected according to its frequency on use and on occurrence in sampled water samples. [SOURCE: Page_133]

In a broader scale, the aquifer vulnerability assessment are used, one of the most widely used groundwater vulnerability- assessment methods is DRASTIC, developed by the US Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) as a method for assessing groundwater pollution potential (Aller et al., 1987). The acronym DRASTIC corresponds to the initials of the seven hydrogeologic parameters: depth to water, net recharge, aquifer media, soil media, topography, impact of vadose zone media, and hydraulic conductivity of the aquifer. These parameters are the most important factors which control the groundwater contamination potential and with available mappable data, they can be processed through geographical information systems (GIS) (Ahmed, 2009). [SOURCE: Page_133]

DRASTIC is a method based on some basic steps following the overlay and index of these parameters. These methods include analysis of raw data, ranking of features on a map, integration of maps and classification of the integrated map based on an index. DRASTIC has been applied more widely than other models and it has been modified according to specific regional objectives (Brindha and Elango, 2015). Some of the most known modifications methods consider the addition of land use/land cover factor (DRASTIC-L/Modified DRASTIC/CDRASTIC) (Jesiya and Gopinath, 2019; Asfaw and Mengistu, 2020; Malakootian and Nozari, 2020), and the modification of weight range values to consider the potential pollution from pesticides (Modified pesticide DRASTIC) (Ahmed, 2009; Al-Abadi, Al-Shamma’a and Aljabbari, 2017; Nadiri et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_133]

A contamination vulnerability assessment for the agricultural basins of Senguio and Zitácuaro is essential due to their current agricultural practices that present an inherent risk of groundwater contamination due to the extensive use of pesticides. In addition, sustainable groundwater management is critical to maintaining agricultural productivity, particularly in regions like Michoacan where droughts are getting longer, and surface water is becoming increasingly scarce. A contamination vulnerability assessment supports regulatory compliance efforts, enhances scientific understanding of groundwater dynamics, and promotes stakeholder [SOURCE: Page_133]

111 [SOURCE: Page_133]

engagement in collaborative decision-making processes aimed at protecting water resources and promoting the long-term resilience of the region's agricultural systems. [SOURCE: Page_134]

5.2 Methods 5.2.1 Soil characterization - Sampling strategy The choice of soil profile sites was determined by specific criteria aimed at achieving three distinct objectives: physical characterization, hydraulic analysis, and pesticide analysis. The primary criteria involved identifying sites that encompassed the most representative soil types in both watersheds, relying on the edaphology map of the region. Consequently, andosols, vertisols, and acrisols were chosen as the target soil types (Figure 5.1 (a)). Additional considerations included the proximity to agricultural lands, particularly areas cultivating specific crops such as avocado in the Zitácuaro watershed and corn in the Senguio watershed. This was aimed at analyzing pesticide residues within the soil profile. Ensuring accessibility and security of the sites became crucial factors in determining the locations for digging soil profiles and extracting samples. Moreover, obtaining permission from the respective landowners was an essential prerequisite before beginning any work on their lands. [SOURCE: Page_134]

5.2.2 Soil samples Intact soil samples for physicochemical and hydraulic parameters analysis were extracted from five soil profiles of one-meter depth. The intact soil cores were hand sculpted for each horizon (Figure 5.1 (b)). The sculpted monolith was properly cut with a metallic knife and placed into a PVC tube of 9 cm internal diameter and 15 length. The samples were packed and carefully transported to the laboratory where they were stored at 4 °C before the experiments. The samples for bulk density analysis were taken from each horizon using a hand auger with metallic tubes measuring 5 cm diameter and 5,4 cm in length. [SOURCE: Page_134]

112 [SOURCE: Page_134]

(b) (a) [SOURCE: Page_135]

(b) [SOURCE: Page_135]

Figure 5. 1. (a) Soil types in two studied watersheds and sampled soil sites. (b) Intact soil sample before packing it into the PVC tube. [SOURCE: Page_135]

5.2.3 Physical properties The soil profiles were excavated and described on-site with methods based on the guidelines outlined in the Soil Description Guide of the Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO, 2009). The description on-site encompasses surface characteristics (presence of organic matter, land use, etc.), texture, soil color, content of carbonates, andic characteristics, soil structure, consistence, porosity, coatings and roots. Laboratory analysis of bulk density, texture, pH, electrical conductivity, organic carbon, total carbon and total nitrogen total were also performed, their techniques are described in the next paragraphs: [SOURCE: Page_135]

113 [SOURCE: Page_135]

Bulk density [SOURCE: Page_136]

The estimation of bulk density was given by the cylinder method. The cylinder method involves inserting a rigid cylinder of known diameter (5cm) and height (5,4 cm) into the soil to collect an undisturbed soil sample. The bulk density is then calculated as the ratio of the soil sample's dry mass divided by its volume, expressed in grams per cubic centimeter. [SOURCE: Page_136]

Texture [SOURCE: Page_136]

The texture was also estimated in the laboratory through the granulometric analysis of each horizon. The granulometric analysis of soils involves determining the percentage content of each fraction of clay (< 0.002 mm), silt (0.002-0.05 mm), and sand (0.05-2.0 mm) (Rząsa and Owczarzak, 2013). This analysis was performed using the sieve analysis method, which separates particles into size ranges to determine the particle-size distribution (gradation). [SOURCE: Page_136]

pH and Electrical conductivity (EC) [SOURCE: Page_136]

The pH and EC were measured following the Procedures for Soil Analysis from the ISRIC, 2002 (Reeuwijk, 2002). The pH of the soils was measured with a potentiometer and the EC with a conductometer. A suspension of 2.5 parts of deionized water mixed with 1 part of sampled soil was used. [SOURCE: Page_136]

Organic carbon [SOURCE: Page_136]

Organic carbon was determined using the method of acid digestion, where the inorganic carbon is eliminated from the matrix by decomposition of carbonates, using chlorohydric acid (HCl 5N). [SOURCE: Page_136]

Total carbon and total nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_136]

For the determination of total carbon and total nitrogen, a CNHS/O elemental analyzer was used. Thermo Scientific Flash 2000 in CN configuration, under the following analytical conditions: helium as carrier gas, combustion temperature of 950 °C, reduction temperature of 680 °C a thermal conductivity detector, and a calibration standard of aspartic acid. [SOURCE: Page_136]

5.2.4 Soil Hydraulic properties The hydraulic properties of the soil (infiltration, hydraulic conductivity, water retention, and available water capacity) were measured using the HYPROP (HYdraulic PROPerty analyser). HYPROP measures the water tension/water content relation (“retention curve”, “pF/WC curve”) [SOURCE: Page_136]

114 [SOURCE: Page_136]

of a soil sample. It also measures how the unsaturated hydraulic conductivity depends on the water tension/content (“Ku curve”). [SOURCE: Page_137]

HYPROP Method [SOURCE: Page_137]

This is based on the evaporation method according to Wind (1968) in Schindler’s model (1980). With this method two tensiometers are positioned in two depths of a soil sample sitting in a sample ring (Figure 5.2). The plane in the middle between the two tensiometers is identical with the horizontal symmetry plane of the column. The sample is saturated with water, basally closed, and set on a balance. The soil surface is open to the ambient atmosphere so that the soil water can evaporate. The change of the sample mass over time is determined by weighing. The medial pF value of the sample is calculated based on the average value of the two tensions. The medial water content is calculated based on the mass change. This results in one measuring value per point in time for the pF/water content curve (Pertassek, Peters and Durner, 2015). [SOURCE: Page_137]

The evaporation rate results from the mass differences, based on this the volume flow is calculated at each point in time. The values of the hydraulic conductivity with increasing desiccation are a result of how well the soil can transport this water to the top where it evaporates. The detailed calculation basics of the method as well as the check of its validity are explained in (Peters and Durner, 2008) and (Pertassek, Peters and Durner, 2017). [SOURCE: Page_137]

Figure 5. 2. Mechanism of HYPROP instrument. Source: : Campbell et al., 2015. [SOURCE: Page_137]

115 [SOURCE: Page_137]

5.2.5 Groundwater vulnerability [SOURCE: Page_138]

5.2.5.1 Datasets To assess the vulnerability to pesticide contamination of the aquifers of the Senguio and Zitácuaro watersheds, a comprehensive approach using multiple data sets within a GIS framework was applied. The dataset integration included both field data and satellite imagery. Field data collected during the 2021 fieldwork included detailed information on soil physicochemical and hydraulic properties, supplemented by data from national databases, as well as satellite meteorological data from various international open-source platforms such as the dataset CHIRPS10 (Funk et al., 2014) from the US Geological Service for historical rainfall records (1981-2023) and the evapotranspiration values from Terra and Aqua satellites using the MODIS- MOD16 series (Mu et al., 2013). Due to heterogeneous data availability between the two watersheds, different methods were applied depending on the available parameters. Table 5.1 displays, the information of datasets, sources and followed methods, and Figure 5.3 the methodological flowchart of this work. [SOURCE: Page_138]

5.2.5.2 PIRI software PIRI employs a quantitative risk assessment approach. The detriment is calculated as the product of the three components, the pesticide load (L), the transport factor (T), and the asset value factor (V): [SOURCE: Page_141]

𝐷𝑒𝑡𝑟𝑖𝑚𝑒𝑛𝑡 = 𝐿𝑇𝑉 [SOURCE: Page_141]

Several equations were applied using these parameters: soil properties (fraction organic carbon, bulk density, volumetric field capacity, soil type); handling of pesticides (frequency of pesticide application, dosage of pesticide, proportion of active ingredient, proportion of area with pesticide applied); site and environmental properties (days after application to first rainfall/irrigation event, slope, rainfall amount, surface water depth) and; pesticide properties (sorption coefficient based on organic carbon (Koc)). [SOURCE: Page_141]

The detailed description of the PIRI model equations (Kookana, Correll and Miller, 2005) are displayed in Appendix C. [SOURCE: Page_141]

The pesticides selected to perform the PIRI analysis were two insecticides: imidacloprid and lambda cyhalothrin, two fungicides: benomyl and carbendazim, and two herbicides: 2,4-D and glyphosate. Each pesticide corresponds to pesticides highly used in eastern Michoacan, and highly hazardous to human health and ecosystems. Rainfall rates were taken into account according to the location of the soil classification derived from the laboratory texture results (also taken into account), where the soil profile was located the rainfall volume displayed with the CHIRPS database was selected. Handling of pesticides, and some parameters of the site and environmental properties, pesticides properties and site and environmental properties were obtained from the results of previous chapters. [SOURCE: Page_141]

5.2.5.3 DRASTIC method DRASTIC is a methodology which allows the pollution potential of any area proposed by Aller et al., (1987). It is an overlay and index model designed to produce vulnerability scores by combining several thematic maps (Al-Abadi, Al-Shamma’a and Aljabbari, 2017). Hydrogeologic settings incorporate the major hydrogeologic factors which are used to infer the potential for contaminants to enter groundwater. These factors form the acronym DRASTIC and include depth of the water table, net recharge, aquifer media, soil, topography, impact of the vadose zone, and hydraulic conductivity (Aller et al., 1987). The DRASTIC index is obtained using the following equation: [SOURCE: Page_141]

119 [SOURCE: Page_141]

DRASTIC Index (DI) = DrDw + RrRw + ArAw + SrSw + TrTw + IrIw + CrCw [SOURCE: Page_142]

D,R,A,S,T,I, and C stand for water table depth, net recharge, aquifer media, soil media, topography, the impact of the vadose zone, and hydraulic conductivity, respectively, and “r” and “w” denote the rating and weight of each factor, respectively (Aller et al., 1987) (Figure 5.4). Due to the dynamic land use/land cover of eastern Michoacan where the two watersheds of this study are located, the factor L was added, then, the equation used in this work corresponds to DRASTIC-L (Jesiya and Gopinath, 2019): [SOURCE: Page_142]

DRASTIC-L Index = DrDw + RrRw + ArAw + SrSw + TrTw + IrIw + CrCw + LrLw [SOURCE: Page_142]

Figure 5. 4. Illustrated DRASTIC parameters in a hypothetical zone. Source: (Bennet, 1987) [SOURCE: Page_142]

Ratings and weightings applied in this work to the pollution potential are presented in Table 5.2 and are related to land use based on the DRASTIC-L index and on the DRASTIC pesticide Index. The DRASTIC pesticide version is a variation of the former DI which is designed to be used when the activity of concern is the application of pesticides to an area. The difference between the two indexes is in the assignment of relative weights for the seven DRASTIC factors (Ahmed, 2009). [SOURCE: Page_142]

The weights of each factor and their range and rate given to each classified factor are shown in Table 5.2. Weights and ratings are given to each of the eight parameters, each is classified into classes on the scale of 1-10, in which 1 denotes least vulnerable while 10 is for the most vulnerable areas. This rating is further scaled into weights based on the importance of the [SOURCE: Page_142]

120 [SOURCE: Page_142]

parameter in determining aquifer characteristics, these scaled on 1-5 where^1 is least significant and 5 is most significant. [SOURCE: Page_143]

29-41                               6 41-82                               8 >82                                 10 Rainfed agriculture                 9 Perennial crops                     7 Heterogeneous agriculture           6 Land use/land cover*                 Urban area                          8          5 Moderate vegetation cover           2 Forest                              1 Bare land and water bodies          1 *not covered in the original DRASTIC method The DRASTIC model has four assumptions: (1) the contaminant is introduced at the ground surface; (2) the contaminant is flushed into the groundwater by precipitation; (3) the contaminant has the mobility of water; (4) the area being evaluated by DRASTIC is 0.4 km^2 or larger (Pathak et al., 2009). The aquifer sensitivity and groundwater vulnerability mapping procedures carried out in this study to incorporate the use of a Geographic Information System (GIS). A GIS is a computerized mapping and spatial data analysis system, which enables the developing and analysis of spatially referenced information to describe the relationship between landscape features (Pathak et al., 2009). The DRASTIC index score is relative, with no specific units. A higher DRASTIC index represents a greater contamination potential and vice versa. This index only provides a relative estimation and is not created to make a complete assessment (Nadiri et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_144]

5.2.5.4 Aquifer vulnerability mapping The overlay index procedure using geographic information system was performed for both watersheds mapping each DRASTIC-L parameter as was shown in Figure 5.3. Each thematic map has been generated in ArcMap using the reclassify tool to assign the ratings and ranks to each parameter according with the own characteristics in each watershed. The final map was generated using the weighted sum of each of the seven maps. However, the information is not homogeneous in both watersheds for all the parameters. The aquifer media, soil types, topography (expressed as slope in degrees), and the impact of the vadose zone were extracted from the national cartography databases for both watersheds, obtaining the three respective maps from the same input national database. However, for the depth to water table, net recharge, and the hydraulic conductivity factors, a different set of methods was used to generate the map inputs. For the sake of practicality and ease of reading, only the input maps of these three components are shown in the results section. The thematic input maps can be consulted in Chapter II. [SOURCE: Page_144]

For the depth to the water table, the case is different: for the Zitácuaro basin, the D-factor was delimited using depth values of agricultural dug wells obtained from field work, as well as information from the piezometric network of the National Water Commission (CONAGUA in Spanish), but as shown in Figure 5.5, there are no data representing the depth to the water table for the Senguio watershed. To replace the D-factor in Senguio’s watershed, the result of an assessment of Groundwater Potential Zones (GWPZ) was used: the result denotes low, intermediate or high grades of potential GW zone. Instead of obtaining a numerical value of the water table depth, these categorical values were translated into categories (very deep, deep, shallow, etc.) and then used as indicator kriging to map the system (Armengol, Sanchez-Vila and Folch, 2014). The field observations allowed us to make this assumption, due to the widespread of springs in the zone. [SOURCE: Page_145]

Figure 5. 5. CONAGUA piezometric sites and agricultural dug wells evaluated in the field for Senguio and Zitácuaro watersheds. [SOURCE: Page_145]

5.2.5.5 Groundwater Potential Zones (GWPZs) as D-factor Assessment of GWPZs using Analytic Hierarchy Process (AHP) coupled with GIS [SOURCE: Page_145]

In Mexico, the piezometric network is poorly mapped and, in some cases, there are no piezometers covering areas of interest, as is the case for Senguio. Contrary to Zitácuaro, the survey to farmers did not provide information on the status of the dug wells, thus the information on the depth of the water table is not available. For this reason, for Senguio watershed the factor D in the drastic index, was replaced by the results of an assessment of groundwater potential zones (GWPZs). [SOURCE: Page_145]

We assumed that the characteristics of potential groundwater zones, such as soil, geology and land cover can describe the soil permeability, which is directly related with the water table depth. In some cases, highly permeable soils allow water to flow more freely, maintaining a [SOURCE: Page_145]

123 [SOURCE: Page_145]

shallower water table compared to less permeable formations where water may be stored at greater depths. In the eastern region of Michoacan, available measurements of water depth exhibit shallow aquifers in high altitudes where andosol soils are predominant. [SOURCE: Page_146]

To evaluate the potential water depth of Senguio’s watershed, the groundwater potential zones were delineated using earth observation satellite data and thematic available maps, which provides information on the different effective components of groundwater and its movement: [SOURCE: Page_146]

▪   Rainfall, it represents the main source of groundwater recharge. The CHIRPS dataset (2003-2023) was used. ▪   Geology represents the different types of rock formations influencing the saturated zone, we used as input the layer of geology (1:250 000) provided by the National database INEGI. ▪   Soils, soil texture play a crucial role in permeability, which influences the retention and movement of water. ▪   Land use/land cover and slope features are interpreted as important elements to water consumption and vertical runoff, respectively. ▪   Lineament density uses properties that expresses the underlying structural features such as fractures, faults, cleavages, and discontinuity surfaces. These were taken from the geological features from the national database provided by INEGI. [SOURCE: Page_146]

All the information was combined with GIS tools using creating specific maps of these parameters (Jha et al., 2007). The AHP method using normalized weights to evaluate these potential zones is described in the next section. [SOURCE: Page_146]

Analytic hierarchy process (AHP) [SOURCE: Page_146]

Each geohydrological parameters were ranked with the one-to-nine Satty scale (Saaty, 2008), these subjective points were assigned according to their importance of influence in groundwater potential. The score of 1 represents equal influence between the two thematic maps, and a score of 9 indicates the extreme influence of one thematic map compared to other one (Table 5.3) (Pinto et al., 2017). The hierarchy model network for eight hydro-geological parameters was constructed using the excel sheet created by (Goepel, 2018) which allows to calculated the eigenvector, expressing the ordering of layer influence on groundwater potentially through the pair-wise comparison matrix (Table 5.4) (Pinto et al., 2017; Goepel, 2018, 2019). The principal eigenvalue (λmax) represents a function for the matrix divergence from consistency. The [SOURCE: Page_146]

124 [SOURCE: Page_146]

allocated weights are considered consistent only if the consistency ratio is equal or less than 10% (Machiwal, Rangi and Sharma, 2015; Goepel, 2019). [SOURCE: Page_147]

Table 5. 3. The one-to-nine scale of parameters significance (Saaty, 1980). [SOURCE: Page_147]

Intensity      Definition                       Explanation 1              Equal importance                 Two elements contribute equally to the objective 3              Moderate importance              Experience and judgement slightly favor one element over another 5              Strong importance                Experience and judgement strongly favor one element over another 7              Very strong importance           One element is favored very strongly over another, it dominance is demonstrated in practice 9              Extreme importance               The evidence favoring one element over another is of the highest possible order of affirmation [SOURCE: Page_147]

2,4,6,8                                       Can be used to express intermediate values [SOURCE: Page_147]

Table 5. 4. Pairwise comparison matrix for the AHP based groundwater potential zoning in watersheds of Eastern Michoacan. [SOURCE: Page_147]

Identification of Groundwater Potential Zones of Senguio watershed [SOURCE: Page_147]

The groundwater potential zones of Senguio watershed were evaluated with an index computed with overlay techniques, following the next equation: 𝑚    𝑛 [SOURCE: Page_147]

𝐺𝑟𝑜𝑢𝑛𝑑𝑤𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑟 𝑃𝑜𝑡𝑒𝑛𝑡𝑖𝑎𝑙 𝐼𝑛𝑑𝑒𝑥 = ∑ ∑(𝑊𝑗 × 𝑋𝑖 ) 𝑤=1 𝑗=1 [SOURCE: Page_147]

Where Wj represents the normalized weight of the j parameter, Xi refers to the weight of the i class of the parameter (in a 1-5 scale, 1 considering the lowest GW potential), m denotes the number of the parameters, and n denotes the number of classes within a specific parameter. The parameters, classes, weights and class rank applied in the GIS procedure, as well as the [SOURCE: Page_147]

Potential infiltration as R-factor [SOURCE: Page_148]

For both watersheds, the net recharge parameter (R-factor) was also mapped with many assumptions due to the lack of data, the potential infiltration of each watershed, was used instead to express the parameter of recharge in the original DRASTIC method. [SOURCE: Page_148]

Precipitation that infiltrates through the land surface to the water table is the primary recharge of groundwater. Net recharge is the amount of water per unit area of land that infiltrates through the land surface and reaches the water table. This recharge is available to transport [SOURCE: Page_148]

contaminants vertically to the water table. It also controls the amount of water available for dispersion and dilution in the vadose and saturated zones (Aller et al., 1987). Infiltration is a component of the general mass balance hydrologic budget, once it is taken from the equation it can be calculated in the simplest way as a subtraction of inflows minus outflows assuming a yearly basis. [SOURCE: Page_149]

𝐼𝑛𝑓𝑖𝑙𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛 = (𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑖𝑝𝑖𝑡𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛 + 𝑖𝑛𝑑𝑢𝑐𝑒𝑑 𝑤𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑟 𝑒𝑛𝑡𝑟𝑖𝑒𝑠) − (𝐸𝑣𝑎𝑝𝑜𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑝𝑖𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛 + 𝑠𝑢𝑟𝑓𝑎𝑐𝑒 𝑟𝑢𝑛𝑜𝑓𝑓) [SOURCE: Page_149]

Precipitation values were extracted as average values of 20 years CHIRPS dataset (2003- 2023) using a 33-kernel machine learning algorithm in Google Earth Engine, then the maps were classified into five classes with the average historical precipitation. The induced water inputs such as the volume of water applied to dilute and apply pesticides was considered at the beginning, according with the information collected from the interviews with farmers for the Zitacuaro watershed. Regardless, the amount of water considered was insignificant for the result, and precipitation was the unique considered as input. In the Senguio watershed, annual precipitation was also the only input considered. Evapotranspiration values were extracted from MOD16, this algorithm follows the Penman-Monteith equation. Includes evaporation from wet and moist soil, evaporation from rainwater intercepted by the canopy before it reaches the ground, and the transpiration through stomata on plant leaves and stems. The MOD162A/A$_3$ ET products are produced at the 8-day, monthly and annual intervals (Mu et al., 2013), the extracted data (2003- 2023) were then calculated at annual basis to be matched with the precipitation data. [SOURCE: Page_149]

The calculation of infiltration begins by determining the average precipitation per zone, from which the volume of evapotranspiration and surface runoff is subtracted. The potential surface runoff is computed by multiplying the annual precipitation by the area covered and a runoff coefficient. This coefficient (C) represents the proportion of water drained superficially during rainfall relative to the total precipitation volume over a defined period, a year in this case (Lallam, Megnounif and Ghenim, 2018). Estimation of the runoff coefficient can be facilitated through tables correlating runoff with surface characteristics, mostly based on soil type, slope degree, and land use/land covers (Moll Barber, 2013). According to the average of different coefficients analyzed by (Moll Barber, 2013), the runoff coefficient in this work was based on Table 5.6. [SOURCE: Page_149]

127 [SOURCE: Page_149]

Table 5. 6. Runoff coefficients based on LULC, slope, and soil texture. [SOURCE: Page_150]

5.3 Results and discussion [SOURCE: Page_150]

5.3.1 Physical and chemical soil characteristics Table 5.7 outlines the parameters assessed in the field for the five sampled soil profiles (Figure 5.1 (a)), alongside the textural percentages determined through laboratory analysis. Each profile, as interpreted in the field, encompasses a varying number of horizons, collectively covering a depth of at least one meter. All profiles showed a color within the yellow reddish gamma and present slight variations between the lightness and intensity of color among the different horizons. Both avocado soil profiles display a mixture of loam and silty clay loam textures, with little differences in their root density and size of aggregates, the first one with predominantly coarse aggregates and the second one with medium size aggregates, providing suitable conditions for root growth and drainage. The profile located in the riverside presents varying structures, with some horizons showing massive aggregates and others lacking distinct aggregates, potentially affecting root penetration and water movement. This profile includes horizons with strong variations, suggesting different sources of deposits. Sandy loam soil has a higher proportion of sand particles, offering excellent drainage and aeration but lower water and nutrient retention. The profile excavated in an ancient forest land which now is covered by secondary vegetation, primarily consists of loam texture, supporting a high root density which balances the predominant presence of thin aggregates, allowing moisture retention and drainage, it is the profile with the highest percentage of rocks. While beneficial in natural settings, such as forests, the abundance of rocks could present challenges in agricultural settings, complicating [SOURCE: Page_150]

Figures 5.6 to 5.8 display the results of pH and electrical conductivity, bulk density, and percentage of organic carbon, total carbon, and total nitrogen. The differences among the soil profiles can be observed in their pH levels and electrical conductivity (EC) values across the different horizons. pH levels indicate the acidity or alkalinity of the soil, while EC measures the soil's ability to conduct electrical current, which is often correlated with soil salinity. [SOURCE: Page_153]

Overall, each profile exhibits a neutral pH, except for horizon C$_1$ in the Riverside Profile, which indicates higher acidity specifically at that depth. Profiles LAE- 3 and SE-5 display the widest range of pH values across their horizons, indicating greater variability in soil acidity or alkalinity. Profile CAR-2 exhibits relatively consistent pH levels, suggesting more uniform soil conditions. Profiles CUR-4 and SE-5 demonstrate significantly higher EC values compared to the other profiles, indicating elevated levels of salts or minerals in the soil. This higher salinity may affect plant growth and soil fertility, especially in agricultural lands such as for profile SE-5. [SOURCE: Page_153]

Comparing bulk density values across different profiles reveals differences in soil density for the same horizon. Horizon A varies significantly among the five profiles, ranging from 0.55 to 1.33 g/cm³. This variability could be attributed to differences organic matter content, and management practices across the profiles. Profiles with higher bulk density values, such as profile SE-5 (vertisol) exceeding 1.0 g/cm³ in some horizons, in contrast, profiles with lower bulk density values, like Profile FS-1 (andosol), present values below 0.70 g/cm³ in most horizons, which may exhibit better soil porosity and support healthier plant growth. [SOURCE: Page_153]

The differences in total carbon, organic carbon, and total nitrogen percentages among the soil profiles highlight variations in soil fertility, organic matter content, and nutrient availability. Across the soil profiles, there is considerable variability in both total carbon and organic carbon percentages. Profile CUR-4 exhibits the highest percentages of total carbon and organic carbon, with values around 7%, which is associated with his low impact management. Followed by Profiles FS-1 and CAR-2, with values above 5% and 4%, respectively, these high values suggest significant organic matter accumulation in the soil, which are verified according with the field observations. [SOURCE: Page_153]

In terms of total nitrogen, similar to total carbon and organic carbon, variability is observed among all soil profiles. Typically, horizon A of each profile exhibits the highest nitrogen content, which gradually decreases with depth, with the exception of profile LAE-3. In this case, the sandy texture of the soil may be applying some influence on nitrogen levels. [SOURCE: Page_153]

5.3.2 Hydraulic soil properties The HYPROP results were processed in its coupled software LABROS SoilView Analysis. To obtain the saturated hydraulic conductivity (Ks) several models based on the Van Genuchten equation (Eq. 5.1) were used (Van Genuchten, 1980; Durner, 1994). All the modalities and models can be consulted in the HYPROP manual (Pertassek, Peters and Durner, 2017; Peters et al., 2023). [SOURCE: Page_155]

Where θe is the effective moisture content (L3L-3), Ks is the saturated hydraulic conductivity, θr and θs are the residual and saturated moisture contents (L3L-3), respectively, and α (L-1), n and m (=1- 1/n) are empirical parameters (Van Genuchten, 1980). [SOURCE: Page_156]

The first graph shows the volumetric water content versus pF (decimal log of tension, expressed as pressure head in the unit of cm). The second graph shows the log of the hydraulic conductivity versus pF, and the third graph the log of hydraulic conductivity versus volumetric water content. [SOURCE: Page_156]

Except for the last two horizons in profiles SE-5, which exhibit a clayey texture, all the Ks values show high hydraulic conductivity. These values can be attributed to several factors, including the presence, size, and continuity of macropores, a high content of sand particles, significant organic matter content, and the presence of living microorganisms in the soil (Oschner, 2018). High hydraulic conductivity values are indicative of well-drained soils, promoting effective water movement and drainage. However, excessively high values of saturated hydraulic conductivity may also pose challenges, such as nutrient leaching and soil erosion, particularly in landscapes with steep slopes, which are predominant in the study area (Tiktak et al., 2012). [SOURCE: Page_160]

5.3.3 Depth to the water table - GWPZs Senguio watershed [SOURCE: Page_160]

Figure 5.9 displays the seven geohydrological mapped parameters (drainage density, lineament density, LULC, geology, soils, slope, and precipitation) for Senguio watershed which were processed to generate the final Groundwater potential zones map. This resulted in the classification of three distinct zones (Figure 5.10), which were classified into deep, medium, and shallow categories to represent the hypothetical D-factor in DRASTIC-L index (Table 5.12). [SOURCE: Page_160]

Moderate             Medium             43.74 [SOURCE: Page_160]

Figure 5. 9. Reclassified thematic maps of drainage density, lineament density, LULC, geology, soils, slope, and precipitation, factors used in the GWPZs calculation for the Senguio watershed. [SOURCE: Page_161]

139 [SOURCE: Page_161]

Continuation Figure 5.9. Reclassified thematic maps of drainage density, lineament density, LULC, geology, soils, slope, and precipitation, factors used in the GWPZs calculation for the Senguio watershed. [SOURCE: Page_162]

For Senguio watershed, the high groundwater potentiality is located is the northeast of the watershed, where forest, permeable soils and high altitudes are located, where superficial wells are very common within the Monarch Butterfly Biosphere Reserve. The lower volume of annual precipitation and low permeable soils coincide with the lowest potentially GW area, in the south and southwest of the watershed. This area works as a zone with the potentially deepest water table for this watershed. Then, the larger area of the watershed was mapped to the moderate GW potentiality category, which is heterogeneous throughout the watershed, as is shown in Figure 5.12. [SOURCE: Page_162]

140 [SOURCE: Page_162]

Figure 5. 10. Groundwater Potential Zones for Senguio watershed [SOURCE: Page_163]

Zitácuaro watershed Using data from 20 dug and surface wells within the Zitácuaro watershed, a depth-to-water table map (Figure 5.11) was created using interpolation techniques. This map highlights the shallowest portion of the aquifer in the central northern region of the watershed, which is characterised by predominant forest cover and andosol soil types. Field observations indicate that the use of dug wells for irrigation purposes is widespread in this area, due to the shallow water table and the limited availability of surface water in dry season. In contrast, the deepest groundwater levels are [SOURCE: Page_163]

141 [SOURCE: Page_163]

located on the periphery of the municipality of Zitácuaro, where three out of eight piezometric site measurements from 2003 indicate depths between 45 and 80 meters at that specific zone. [SOURCE: Page_164]

Figure 5. 11. Depth to the water table map for Zitácuaro watershed. [SOURCE: Page_164]

5.3.4 Net Recharge - Potential infiltration Net recharge for both watersheds was estimated based on the potential infiltration, followed by the next equation: [SOURCE: Page_164]

𝐼𝑛𝑓𝑖𝑙𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛 = 𝑃𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑖𝑝𝑖𝑡𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛 − 𝐸𝑣𝑎𝑝𝑜𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑝𝑖𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛 − 𝑠𝑢𝑟𝑓𝑎𝑐𝑒 𝑟𝑢𝑛𝑜𝑓𝑓 [SOURCE: Page_164]

Rainfall map was generated by interpolation mean of annual precipitation (mm/year) from the dataset of CHIRPS (23 years) given in a spatial grid for each watershed, then evapotranspiration data from MODIS satellites was crossed with the yearly precipitation grid data. Runoff coefficients were selected according to the table 6, based on slope, soil, and LULC maps and located in the mapped zones corresponding to precipitation and evapotranspiration data. Then the equation was calculated using the field calculator of the GIS, to finally interpolate (IDW) the values and classify in five ranges (Figure 5.12). [SOURCE: Page_164]

142 [SOURCE: Page_164]

Figure 5. 12. Infiltration rates map for Zitácuaro (left) and Senguio (right) watersheds. [SOURCE: Page_165]

In the maps, areas with the lowest volumes of water infiltration are represented by brown colours. This pattern is influenced by a combination of factors such as soil permeability and slope, particularly evident in the northern region of Zitácuaro. Despite experiencing higher precipitation levels, this area does not necessarily translate into significant volumes of water infiltration, the zone exhibiting the lowest volumes of infiltrated water encompasses the entire urban area, along with various types of low and intermediate permeable soils. In the Senguio watershed, the computed data highlights notably low volumes of infiltrated water in areas with clayey soils. Conversely, the southern part of this watershed, characterised by higher elevations and dense forest cover, exhibits intermediate infiltration rates. [SOURCE: Page_165]

5.3.5 Hydraulic conductivity To generate this layer, saturated hydraulic conductivity (Ks) data of five soil profiles, derived from the modelled HYPROP values presented previously, were interpolated, and mapped. Since the Zitácuaro watershed comprised 4 of the 5 profiles analysed, some average saturated hydraulic conductivity values were found in the literature to complete for the three most important predominant soil types not studied in the Senguio watershed (Table 5.9). [SOURCE: Page_165]

5.3.6 Pesticides potential mobility Table 5.10 shows the main results of the potential mobility for the six evaluated pesticides (carbendazim, benomyl, imidacloprid, 2,4-D, glyphosate, and lambda cyhalothrin). Key characteristics included in the analysis are soil type, organic carbon percentage, rain period (May- October), and the average accumulated rainfall. Slope less than 10 degrees was also considered, as well as the applied volume per hectare, percentage treated field (one hectare), and the fraction of the active ingredient from the commercial used brand, in this case: 50% for carbendazim, benomyl, and 2,4-D; 30% for imidacloprid; 36% for glyphosate, and 7% for lambda-cyhalothrin. [SOURCE: Page_166]

Groundwater mobility impact is displayed as low for carbendazim and benomyl in all the different combinations, the rest of pesticides remain in very low impact except for imidacloprid. Imidacloprid presented a high mobility impact in sandy loam soils with 2% of organic carbon content. The surface water mobility was also displayed to compare both impacts. All the evaluated pesticides presented in this case, medium or more than medium impact. Clay soils exhibit the highest impact except for glyphosate and lambda cyhalothrin. [SOURCE: Page_166]

5.3.7 Groundwater contamination vulnerability based on DRASTIC-L The final maps displaying the DRASTIC-L index followed a ranking corresponding to their specific classes in each parameter (Table 5.11). The weights followed were based on the pesticides DRASTIC-L model (Ahmed, 2009; Al-Abadi, Al-Shamma’a and Aljabbari, 2017; Montoya et al., 2019) which was slightly modified depending on the availability datasets for each watershed in our study zones. The groundwater vulnerability contamination of Zitácuaro and Senguio watersheds were also computed without the parameter of land use and land cover, to compare the impact of this import layer for the study watersheds. The Zitácuaro watershed map shows the resulting range of potential groundwater vulnerability to pollution, rating values extended from 67 to 262 in the DRASTIC-L overlay and a range between 62 and 215 in the [SOURCE: Page_167]

145 [SOURCE: Page_167]

DRASTIC overlay. The case of Senguio watershed vulnerability map resulted in a range of potential groundwater pollution index extended from 93 to 215 for the case of the DRASTIC-L and a range of 88 to 175 for the DRASTIC index without the land use parameter. These ranges are shown in Table 5.12, they were divided in equal intervals which covered different percentages of the total watershed area. The higher the groundwater pollution vulnerability index is, the larger the groundwater relative pollution can be. [SOURCE: Page_168]

Senguio watershed groundwater vulnerability The results of groundwater vulnerability in Senguio watershed were delineated into five distinct categories, each reflecting varying degrees of susceptibility. Very low vulnerability (93-117) covering 31.8% of the total watershed area, low vulnerability (118-142) which represents the 23.6% of the total watershed, moderate vulnerability (143 to 166) in 15.3 km² that represents the 18.1% of the watershed, high vulnerability (167-191) with the 16.9% of the watershed area, and an area of 8 square km² as very high vulnerability (191-214). The vulnerability categories identified in this study can be delineated into two primary zones: a central to southern zone characterised by higher vulnerability and a central to northern zone exhibiting lower vulnerability. This distribution pattern is consistently observed and can be attributed to distinct altitude gradients and land use classifications. [SOURCE: Page_169]

147 [SOURCE: Page_169]
