In the southern direction, lower altitudes predominate, facilitating extensive rainfed agricultural activities. These agricultural practices contribute significantly to increased vulnerability in this zone. Conversely, as one moves towards the northern direction, higher elevations dominate, primarily comprising forest lands that cover the northern part of the main polygon of the Monarch Butterfly Biosphere Reserve. [SOURCE: Page_170]

The role of land use and land cover (LULC) in the vulnerability model is notable. For instance, the presence of dense forest in the recharge zone of the Senguio watershed acts as a mitigating factor, decreasing vulnerability within that zone. This is evident when comparing vulnerability assessments with and without the LULC layer. Without considering land use and land cover, vulnerability assessments show an increase in moderate vulnerability and a decrease in high and very high vulnerability. This highlights the direct impact of agricultural activities on groundwater vulnerability. [SOURCE: Page_170]

Figure 5.13 illustrates the layers used to assign rankings and weights in the DRASTIC-L model. Subsequently, Figure 5.14 displays the map showing groundwater vulnerability to pesticide pollution for the Senguio watershed map without the LULC layer. This is followed by Figure 5.15, which illustrates the final overlayed DRASTIC-L map, allowing visualization between vulnerability differences. [SOURCE: Page_170]

[SOURCE: Page_172]

150 [SOURCE: Page_172]

Zitácuaro watershed groundwater vulnerability [SOURCE: Page_173]

The DRASTIC-L index range applied to the Zitácuaro watershed was also divided into five distinct vulnerability categories, each represented by equal intervals. These categories are showed on the map as follows: very low vulnerability (67-106), low vulnerability (107-145), moderate vulnerability (146-184), high vulnerability (185-223), and very high vulnerability (224- 262). Of particular concern is that high and very high vulnerability categories encompassed more than the 35% of the total watershed area.       This highly vulnerable area is mostly located in the northeast section of the watershed, characterised by permeable soils and extensive monoculture plantations, predominantly avocado orchards. The combination of these factors, along with shallow water table depths, highlight the importance of prioritizing monitoring efforts in this area. Another notable section of this high vulnerability zones lies in the southern part of the watershed, just upstream of the El Bosque dam, where as well as in the northeast, shallow water table depths and permeable soils are present. [SOURCE: Page_173]

Moderate vulnerability covers 33.6% of the watershed, with its distribution showing a relatively irregularity across the watershed, indicating widespread susceptibility to contamination throughout the watershed. Notably, the eastern part of the watershed constitutes the largest portion of this moderate vulnerability zone, adjacent to the areas with higher vulnerability. Areas classified as very low and low vulnerability constitute 28% of the watershed. These regions are found in the water-saturated area of the El Bosque DAM and in elevated forested lands. These areas present lower pollution vulnerability that can be translated in lower potential risk levels of contamination due to natural factors. The same DRASTIC index was performed without the LULC factor, to evaluate the weight of this variable in the final overlay. It has increased the zone of moderate vulnerability, and it has lowered the percentage assigned to low vulnerability and very high vulnerability in the first model. Figure 5.19 illustrates the layers utilized for the overlay in the DRASTIC-L index, providing insight into the data integration process. Figures 5.20 and 5.21 show the groundwater pesticide pollution vulnerability maps of the Zitácuaro watershed, generated using the DRASTIC and DRASTIC-L methodologies, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_173]

Conclusion The aquifer contamination vulnerability assessments conducted in the Zitácuaro and Senguio watersheds emphasize the complex interaction of natural and anthropogenic factors. Both watersheds display unique vulnerability distributions and contributing factors. The application of the PIRI model for assessing pesticide mobility provides a deeper insight into the implications of pesticide leaching in the highly vulnerable regions. It offers a preliminary diagnosis of potential pesticide leaching in areas identified as particularly susceptible, enhancing our comprehension of the associated risks. [SOURCE: Page_176]

The Senguio watershed exhibits a low potential for pesticide leaching in its agricultural areas, mainly attributed to the high clay content in its soils. However, the correlation between runoff and pesticide transport suggests a significant impact downstream, as evidenced in Chapter IV. On the other hand, the moderate to high vulnerability of aquifers at higher altitudes is influenced by more permeable soils and geological features, highlighting potential risks in these areas. [SOURCE: Page_176]

The mobility of pesticides modelled with soils characteristics from Zitácuaro watershed presented mostly low and very low impact. The sandy loam soils sampled at the watershed outlet exhibited high potential for leaching of the insecticide imidacloprid, aligning with the moderate and highly vulnerable categories identified by the DRASTIC-L index within the same zone. However, the remaining areas categorized by DRASTIC-L as high and very high vulnerability in the north- eastern of Zitácuaro watershed did not demonstrate a significant impact on pesticide mobility, as indicated by the PIRI results considering the evaluated pesticides (glyphosate, 2,4-D, benomyl, carbendazim, imidacloprid, and lambda cyhalothrin). [SOURCE: Page_176]

This discrepancy can be attributed to the assumption made by DRASTIC-L, which does not account for the intrinsic characteristics of pesticides. Conversely, the low mobility of these pesticides observed in the PIRI results may be associated with higher organic matter content and steeper slopes in these areas. These findings underline the importance of considering soil properties and pesticide characteristics in risk assessments to ensure more accurate predictions of pesticide mobility and potential environmental impacts. [SOURCE: Page_176]

While neither PIRI nor DRASTIC-L can provide absolute predictions regarding groundwater detriment, they serve as valuable initial diagnostics for formulating improved [SOURCE: Page_176]

154 [SOURCE: Page_176]

management strategies and conducting more robust future analyses. Effective watershed management demands a comprehensive understanding of the regional geographical features, land use practices, and pesticide inputs specific to each evaluated area. To enhance accuracy, future analyses should prioritize the incorporation of actual pesticide degradation and sorption rates specific to the soils in question. This approach ensures a more precise assessment of groundwater contamination risks and facilitates the implementation of targeted mitigation measures. [SOURCE: Page_177]

155 [SOURCE: Page_177]

Chapter VI: General conclusion and perspectives 6.1 Conclusion In order to achieve the general objective which was to evaluate the risks to human health and water contamination posed by pesticide use in eastern region of Michoacan. We implemented an original integrated approach, that included interviews with local farmers on their agricultural practices, pesticide analysis in recipient water samples, environmental and health risk assessment related to pesticide use, and the characterization of vulnerability of groundwater resources. These approaches were interconnected at various stages to provide a meaningful and reliable groundwork to the human, environmental and water contamination risks posed by pesticides in eastern Michoacan. [SOURCE: Page_178]

By focusing on farming practices specifically on pesticide usage, we unveiled the current heterogeneous misuse of pesticides in two watersheds having different land use. The inappropriate handling of pesticides has potential impact on the health of the most exposed population group, such as farmers, pesticide appliers, and nearby residents. Commonly used pesticides like benomyl and λ-cyhalothrin pose the highest acute and chronic risks to human health. Additionally, less frequently mentioned pesticides such as chlorpyrifos, thiabendazole, and thiram are also highly toxic compounds. Despite their lower usage rates, it is imperative to handle them with personal protective equipment, including gloves, goggles, protective clothing, and masks. The lack of compliance with these safety requirements was another limitation identified among all the interviewed farmers. The interview approach highlighted similarities between both studied watersheds, predominantly the shortcomings about the inadequate handling of used pesticides. However, distinct differences emerged between watersheds, mainly attributed to variations in irrigation needs and the frequency of pesticide applications, which were influenced by the types of crops cultivated: annual-cycling crops in the Senguio watershed and perennial plantations in the Zitácuaro watershed. [SOURCE: Page_178]

Based on the responses obtained from avocado producers during interviews, it is clear that avocado plantations are subject to more frequent pesticide applications than areas with annual-cycling crops. Despite this, the occurrence of high pesticide concentrations in all sampled sites within both watersheds and the core zone of the MBBR underlines the need for a holistic comprehension of the origins and dynamics of pesticide contamination in the region. The most heavily contaminated site is situated at the outlet of the Senguio watershed, surrounded by maize, [SOURCE: Page_178]

156 [SOURCE: Page_178]

fodder crops, and husk tomato fields. Moreover, within the core of the MBBR, elevated levels of a particularly toxic pyrethroid metabolite (3-PBA) were detected. These findings suggest that while land use and cover patterns play a role, additional factors contribute significantly to increased pesticide contamination, requiring a deeper exploration of such influences. [SOURCE: Page_179]

In the case of the MBBR pesticide contamination, inconsistent legislation plays a role in its contamination, due to the inadequate management of phytosanitary treatments. The contamination of sites in Zitácuaro watershed is also of high concern: the results empathize the lack of good agricultural practices, indicating the useless application of pesticides during the rainy season, which ends up being washed away from soils and mobilized in downstream water bodies. The environmental risks evaluated in three trophic levels, indicate high risk posed by four main pesticides: diuron, 3-PBA, atrazine and carbendazim. These pesticides present a high toxicity for algae, invertebrates, and fish, suggesting that they have the potential to cause harm to primary producers (such as algae or plants), primary consumers (such as zooplankton or small fish), and secondary consumers (such as larger fish or predators) within the aquatic ecosystem. They disrupt the balance and health of the entire ecosystem, potentially leading to cascading effects on biodiversity, ecosystem functioning, and eventually, human well-being if the affected ecosystem is used as a source of food or other resources. [SOURCE: Page_179]

The final section of the thesis investigated the potential mobility of six pesticides: Glyphosate, the herbicide most frequently used by farmers; 2,4-D, a highly persistent herbicide mentioned sparingly in the interviews but found in high concentrations in certain water samples; Imidacloprid, the most commonly mentioned insecticide, presenting high concentrations in all water samples from both watersheds; Lambda-cyhalothrin, identified as the most toxic active ingredient; Benomyl, the most commonly used fungicide; and its metabolite carbendazim, one of the pesticides with the highest concentrations observed in all samples from the watersheds. The study revealed that various factors such as soil type, organic matter content, precipitation, topography, and pesticide characteristics play significant roles in determining the mobility of pesticide molecules in the environment. The insecticide imidacloprid was the only compound to show high mobility to groundwater, particularly in permeable soils with a balanced textures comprising nearly equal parts of sand, silt, and clay. Conversely, the mobility of the remaining pesticides across all soil types was generally low or very low. These findings were then compared with the results of the assessment of the aquifer contamination vulnerability. Though, the computed index considers pesticides moving like water (i.e. non-reactive), two different results can be evaluated between both models. However, when there is not information about the active [SOURCE: Page_179]

157 [SOURCE: Page_179]

ingredient concentration and the application ratio, the DRASTIC-L index can be interpreted as a preliminary indicator, particularly considering assumptions of pesticide contamination coming from very polar pesticides and highly permeable soils with low organic matter content. [SOURCE: Page_180]

The general findings emphasize the urgent need for integrated pest management strategies, regulatory reforms, and ecosystem-based approaches to mitigate pesticide pollution and safeguard aquatic ecosystems. Collaboration among policymakers, agricultural stakeholders, scientists, and local communities is essential to address the complex challenges posed by pesticide contamination and promote sustainable agriculture practices that prioritize environmental and human health. [SOURCE: Page_180]

Some results from chapter III and chapter IV were published in January 2024 as a research article for the Journal “Science of the Total Environment”, appendix D included the publication. [SOURCE: Page_180]

6.2 Limitations The project was initially conceived to focus on the mobility of pesticides in intact soil columns. However, complications arose due to new French regulations on importing foreign soil, requiring a change in the main proposed method. As a result, an alternative approach had to be developed. The pesticide analysis method in water was implemented to complement the thesis, alongside the original plan to conduct interviews to identify pesticides based on local agricultural practices. [SOURCE: Page_180]

The COVID-19 pandemic further disrupted the project, causing delays in the fieldwork schedule, including water collection and laboratory analysis. During this period, the analytical method was developed, and interviews contributed to identifying additional compounds. However, the list of detected and quantified pesticide active ingredients reflects only an approximate contamination profile, which requires further investigation. [SOURCE: Page_180]

The targeted crops did not include greenhouse farms, which are rapidly expanding in another part of the Zitácuaro municipality. This type of agriculture likely contributes to pesticide contamination, but it was beyond the scope of this work. [SOURCE: Page_180]

Efforts were also made in collaboration with the Institute of Geology at UNAM (Mexico) to analyze the soil's physico-chemical characteristics, a key factor in the mobility of pesticides towards surface or groundwater. These parameters were intended to be integrated into a 1D pesticide mobility model. However, the time spent on these methodological adjustments, along [SOURCE: Page_180]

158 [SOURCE: Page_180]

with the delays in laboratory work, exceeded the initial time frame and prevented a deeper exploration of pesticide mobility and the inference of DRASTIC-L findings. [SOURCE: Page_181]

6.3 Applied results This study has established the groundwork towards more sustainable agricultural strategies in the region, where several research directions and implementation actions can be taken. From the project's inception, there has been a plan to implement the thesis findings through workshops, targeting farmers and individuals engaged in agriculture. In February 2024, the main results of this thesis were used in a workshop implemented in Zitácuaro. Researchers from the Center of Environmental Geography Research (UNAM campus Morelia) in México and the non- governmental organization Alternare A.C. directed this workshop after weeks of joint work. It was carried out in the framework of an auto-evaluation of the sustainability of their agricultural practices of avocado production. Participants were involved in an introspective about how sustainable their avocado orchards were, through a format showing them environmental, social, and economic aspects of their orchards. This workshop covered mostly results from chapter III and chapter IV. Specifically, the acute and chronic toxicity of the pesticides used, supported by the Spanish translation of table 3.7 from chapter III, which was modified after previous meetings, to board in the same workshop the toxicity impact that used pesticides pose on pollinators. The importance of the personal protective equipment and the water contamination risk was also covered. Appendix E displays the auto-evaluation format used in the workshop along with some photos shared from the work team. [SOURCE: Page_181]

6.3 Perspectives More workshops including as much as possible agricultural communities are necessary to carry out, this one was a successful example of how the generated information can be applied, but also an example of how much joint effort is necessary. [SOURCE: Page_181]

There is a clear need for the implementation of integrated pest management strategies that emphasize prevention, monitoring, and control of pests using environmentally sensitive techniques. This could involve punctual capacitation for farmers in the field to let them know about the appropriate pesticides application timing and techniques, the low environmental impact pesticides, among others. [SOURCE: Page_181]

159 [SOURCE: Page_181]

The study highlights the importance of regulatory reforms to improve pesticide management practices. This includes better enforcement of existing laws, and the development of policies that prioritize environmental and human health protection. [SOURCE: Page_182]

Ecosystem-based approaches are essential for mitigating pesticide pollution and safeguarding aquatic ecosystems. This involves considering the broader ecological context in pesticide management decisions, such as the potential impacts on biodiversity, ecosystem functioning, and human well-being. [SOURCE: Page_182]

Collaboration among policymakers, agricultural stakeholders, scientists, and local communities is crucial for addressing the complex challenges posed by pesticide contamination. By working together, stakeholders can develop holistic solutions that balance agricultural productivity with environmental and human health concerns. [SOURCE: Page_182]

There is a need for further research to explore the long-term impacts of pesticide contamination on aquatic ecosystems, biodiversity, and human health. This could involve conducting longitudinal studies, assessing the effectiveness of different mitigation strategies, and investigating the interactions between pesticides and other environmental stressors. [SOURCE: Page_182]

Finally, but not least important, it is necessary a broader pesticide analysis, including key molecules derived from the interviews, that were not included in the developed method for this work because of the different chemical properties incompatibles with the SPE- LC-MS/MS technique, the lack of time and the lack of financial resources. Molecules such as paraquat and glyphosate which pose high adverse effects on human and environmental health could be the priority ones, along with parental compounds of the analyzed metabolite (3-PBA), such as permethrin, cypermethrin and lambda-cyhalothrin. The analysis of other contaminants such as emergent contaminants (antibiotics, hormones, care and industrial products, pharmaceuticals, etc.) is a little explored subject that could also be analysed for their adverse impact on humans and the environment. [SOURCE: Page_182]

160 [SOURCE: Page_182]
